"Here stands a tree" is logically right and metaphysically wrong if the noumenon tree is taken for a phenomenon [122]. 3. Valuing truths by their genesis. As finished truths, all truths are equally valuable. They differ only by the process of their finding [122]. Original questioning (child, early humanity), the "question of actuality" (Wirklichkeitsfrage), is akin to musing and contemplation. Each found fact demands relation to all others, since in actuality everything is connected. So the drive for cognition aims at a gapless system and exchanges the "pain of questioning" for interest in calculable relations between facts. The scientist's tie to actuality becomes a "thin thread" [123]. Finding lived experience is replaced by finding experience (Erfahren), and the lived by the learned. Illustration: the conscientious old-style scholar who lost life behind formulas [124]. 4. Discovery vs learning. A declarative sentence conveys its sense, not the experience of its discovery. Galileo's finding of the law of fall contained a fullness of experience that the physics student never acquires [124]. Science "turns away from every actuality as soon as it... possesses... a formula", and increases abstraction, not depth [124]. Klages cites the disproportion between mechanical, optical and acoustic knowledge and our ignorance of the essence of motion, colour, sound, cause and force, and of the origin of number. Historical knowledge is a "life-crushing burden" with no insight into the driving powers of peoples [124–125]. "Man is never further removed from the experiencing of space than... as measuring geometer" [125]. Scientific temper is research by understanding alone, excluding lived experience. Intellectually it logicizes actuality; materially it subjugates actuality by the "logic of facts". Its "real symbol" is the machine [125]. 5. The distinction drawn. A single correct judgment always had compelling experience in it, in mathematics as much as in landscape painting. But comprehensive systems require dilution of experience [125–126]. Terms: a truth is known (gewußt) when possessed through finding experience, merely acquainted with (gekannt) when acquired through experience and learning. The same truth can be either [126]. Hence the shallow/deep reflection distinction: knowledge develops toward more items of knowledge or toward deeper insight [126]. Logic and theory of science have never asked what motives stand behind research [126]. Only shallow reflection has become "science". Deep reflection risks sinking to "decorative play" [126–127]. 6. Historical-sociological genealogy. Dühring says natural philosophy "never yet led to any real increase" of insight. Klages agrees in a different sense: the Brunos and Caruses rarely added to the Newtons and Darwins, and conversely [127]. Scientificity is barely four hundred years old. Antiquity discovered nearly all methods and basic explanatory concepts [127–128]. Belief in progress is the creed of bourgeois Liberalism against feudal and priestly orders. The spirit of science is a phenomenal form of the same spirit that produced the modern state and capitalism [128]. Co-emergence: the bourgeois ideal of freedom, Roman law, Protestantism, the idea of expansion and progress, calculating self-interest, and inventive science [128]. This is asserted by historical analogy ("biological" consideration of cultural simultaneity). 7. Epistemological core. Everything conceived is the same for the Babylonian and the American: number, red, C-sharp, two minutes, anger, love. Actuality never recurs, so the same anger cannot be lived twice [128–129]. Between the edifice of the noumenal and the "maelstrom of passing away" opens an abyss. Gazing into it produces astonishment and musing, which, given form, is metaphysical knowledge [129]. In it man knows himself as a double being. He knows that Spirit is a stranger in happening and that things return to him only "the world-piercing ray of the I addicted to persistence". These too are judgments, but ones whose matter and impulse come from "the depth of life" [129]. Formula: historical man must either bind life within Spirit or loose Spirit within life [129]. The first path builds "the straight-rising Eiffel Tower of science, which is destined never to be finished". The second is a labyrinth whose passages converge on "the essence of actuality, only ever to be lived" [130]. Logocentric/progressive vs biocentric/contemplative. The logocentric has predominated everywhere, but only in Christendom did it exclude the biocentric. No purely biocentric metaphysician has yet existed [130]. There is no unintentional questioning. Every system of findings serves either the will to objectify and possess or the will to re-dissolve the objectified [130]. 8. Method (three differences). Shallow and deep reflection can use each other's results: 2×2=4 is a shallow truth from deep ones, and using arithmetic to fathom unity points toward depth. An "error of depth", such as the Eleatic denial, is worth more than a thousand shallow truths [131]. (i) Deep reflection chooses its points by "the pulse-beat of lived experience". (ii) It treats causal relating as a clue to the "building-element of actuality" that compels us to relate, not as deeper knowledge. Chains of causes express a will to expand power. (iii) Experience and induction sink to methodical makeshifts, and self-reflection through examination of names becomes the chief instrument [131–132]. 9. Example: triads. Pythagorean number-speculation is madness to science, but metaphysically intelligible as the interweaving of a boundless and a bounding principle [132]. Science explains the sanctity of three by early humans counting only to two. It never asks whether triplicity is a feature of the world. Klages lists triads: three dimensions, three tenses, waking/dreaming/sleeping, solid/liquid/gas, plant/animal/human, der/die/das, three germ layers, three palm lines, and more [132]. He grants it could lead toward astrology, which he does not dismiss without examination. A metaphysician who found the model of threeness would have a reagent for sorting genuine from merely apparent triads, including at least one false one in his own list, and could judge the Pythagorean table of opposites [133]. Science is a fortress; metaphysics is a few dwindling "gypsy" wagons. Today's philosophical output contains "not the least content of genuine metaphysics" [133]. 10. Why names (linguistic-genealogical argument). No thinking without speech. A concept recurs identically in never-identical sounds as the thing does in never-identical appearances, so the concept is anchored "two-sidedly in the image" [134]. The speech-sound is the experience of the answer to the seeking question, so the name preserves the finding experience in its roots [134]. Meanings drift away from their intuitive content. Every sign for the abstract is intuitive taken literally: Zweck was the peg at the centre of a target; "object", "impression", "conceive", "reason" [134–135]. After about age seven we have only "experiences" mediated by named concepts. The man of the world and the bookworm are alike in this: their shared schoolmistress is the mother tongue [135–137]. Klages cites Hobbes, Humboldt, Max Müller and especially Lazarus Geiger: "the prehistoric world... thinks... within us" [136]. Learning is orientation; discovery is pathbreaking. Genius cannot be taught, but there is a method for bursting the fetters of inherited conceptuality: reflection on the content of names [137]. 11. Two applications. (a) Law. Bacon fought the "idols" of names, yet empiricism has been "led on a leash" by conceptual schemata more than anyone. "Laws" imply a lawgiver and obeyers, the human logos. Historically (Zeno and Chrysippus) the "law of nature" is world-reason. Its "fruits" are canalised rivers, forests in rows, captured waters, winds and electricities, and weapons that fetch the last elephant from the last forest [137–138]. "Laws of nature reach exactly as far as the field of play of the human will" [138]. The concept of law is the outward projection of the conceiving I; teleology is that of the willing I [138–139]. (b) Sense (Sinn). It now means purpose. But the same word names the bodily senses, the messengers of images. So the word locates "sense" in impression-giving appearance and the "sense-filled soul" in the world of appearance [139]. 12. Closing. The examination has passed from extreme right (idealism) to extreme left (materialism/mechanism). Both, and rationalism/sensualism, are shallow reflection. Both stand on the judged world and know nothing of living life. "Esse = percipi" read left to right gives idealism, read right to left materialism [139–140]. Next comes Kant's intermediate position, examined through his concept of space [140].

Key terms. flache vs tiefe Besinnung; logozentrisch vs biozentrisch; Wirklichkeitsfrage; findendes Erleben vs Erfahren/Lernen; gewußt vs gekannt; Erkenntnis vs Kenntnisse (insights vs items of knowledge); reflection on the content of names (Besinnung auf den Gehalt der Namen); Gesetz, Sinn; "objects are estranged subjects".

Contribution to the whole. This chapter cashes out the Introduction's Kenntnis/Wissen and will-to-power genealogy of science, using the epistemology of ch. 10–13. It states the method of the whole book: etymology, "triads", and the indicating use of concepts. It supplies the logocentric/biocentric opposition that runs through the rest. It ties the critique of science to cultural history: Christendom, Liberalism, capitalism, the machine, later developed in Book IV and the Pelasgian chapters (71–75). It sets up the Kant critique (ch. 15–17) and the sensualism critique (ch. 18–20) as further cases of shallow reflection.

Critical note. The genetic criterion for evaluating truths (a truth possessed through discovery vs through learning) is original and defensible as an account of understanding rather than of validity. Klages is careful to keep correctness separate from it [125–126]. The most vulnerable step is the method. Etymology is treated as access to "finding experience", but no criterion separates revealing etymologies from accidental ones. The triad list shows how far the method can run without control, and Klages admits it may contain a false member without saying how he would find it. The "law" argument is genetic (laws once implied a lawgiver), so it can only corroborate ch. 13's thesis that actuality contains no lawfulness. It cannot establish that thesis. Klages also concedes that one cannot master what one has not "overheard some features of its real constitution" [138]. That sits uneasily with a nature "wholly and entirely ignorant of law". The concession implies that laws track something real, even if they do not track the ousia of happening.

Arc of Introduction–Ch. 14

The Introduction states the thesis and the programme. Spirit is an alien wedge in the Body–Soul life-cell, and psychology needs a rigorous metaphysics of the three principles. Book I (ch. 1–9) then builds that metaphysics from the most abstract point it can find: the durationless Now. Ch. 1–3 argue that apprehension is punctual and so extra-spatio-temporal. Its objects (the Now, positions, things, properties) are abstract relational points, and duration and existence are constructions that cannot fit real time. The key distinction, objective time vs real time, generalises into Being (Sein) vs actuality (Wirklichkeit). Ch. 4–6 test the distinction on motion. Zeno is right that motion has no being. But Klages reverses the Eleatic valuation. What follows is that Being is not actual, not that actuality is unreal. The Eleatic, Platonic, Kantian and mechanistic traditions all made the opposite choice, out of "hostility to the senses". Ch. 7–9 move inside the person. The faculty of points is Geist. Its presence in a life constitutes a persisting I. The continuous is known by the different power of Erleben (life, soul). Spirit, once actual in a soul, appears as self-assertion: will and inhibition, opposed to drive, passion and Eros.

Book II, Part 1 (ch. 10–14) then defends this ontology against its strongest opponent, "logistic" idealism, which says all talk of the inconceivable is already conception. The replies come in layers. Meaning is not conceiving (ch. 10). What is conceived is only a direction or angle, and every concept also indicates an unconceived experiential occasion (ch. 11). The subject–object over-against is not made by Spirit but found in a vital polarity of experiencing and the experienced as foreign (ch. 12). Universals and "validity" are abstractions with the actual present "as separated". Truth is a score of actuality, not a second actuality, and the only point where thought and actuality coincide is the thinking I (ch. 13). Ch. 14 draws the methodological and cultural consequences. Science is shallow reflection serving Spirit's will to possess the world. Metaphysics is deep reflection, using concepts indicatively and reading the sediment of prehistoric experience in names.

The block presupposes little from outside itself, but it leans heavily on a few unexamined premises. One is that the apprehending act is literally durationless (ch. 1). Another is that any mental power must be either point-positing Spirit or continuum-receiving life, with "no mediating concept" allowed (ch. 8). A third is the asymmetry that life can experience without Spirit (ch. 8–9). The decisive term Erleben is so far defined mainly negatively, as whatever gives us the continuous. Its positive account (beholding (Schauung), images, polarity, vital mirroring) is handed forward to Book II Parts 2–4 and Book III. The block also hands forward: the critique of Kant's space (ch. 15–17); the theory of error as "dislocation of lived experience" (ch. 13, promised); the actuality of the I as "the central problem of metaphysics" (ch. 13 [120] → ch. 39 and Book IV); the will as self-assertion (ch. 9 → ch. 41–54); and the critique of formalism and logistic (ch. 13 → ch. 38).

There are some tensions. (1) Ch. 12 makes colour and seeability inseparable poles: there are "no colours without seeability" [103]. Ch. 13 says colour "remains something visible without the faculty of sight" [116], and that actuality lies "on this side" of subject and object. These are consistent only if seeability is a standing feature of the image, independent of any seeing faculty. But then the polarity of ch. 12 is no longer a relation between two actual poles, and the block never says what a pole without its counterpart amounts to. (2) The persisting I is derived as an existent (ch. 7, 13). Yet Book I argues that all existence is a construction of Spirit's points, so the one actual thought-object is also, by Klages's own analysis, an "impossible state of affairs" [63]. Klages flags this as an open problem rather than resolving it. (3) The Introduction calls the will to power Nietzsche's "decisive error". Ch. 9 then makes a will to self-assertion, of which the will to power is "one side", the very essence of Spirit's actuality, so the "error" becomes a misplaced truth. (4) Klages denies that Spirit is the source of error, since error is a dislocation of experience (ch. 13). This sits oddly with the book's broader indictment of Spirit as the falsifier of the world-picture (ch. 5, 14). The reconciliation seems to be that individual judgments err through life, while systems falsify through Spirit's tendency, but the block does not spell this out. (5) Ch. 14's claim that every "fact" is a noumenon wrongly taken for a phenomenon must also apply to Klages's own enumerated "facts" (the triads). Klages applies his indicating/conceiving distinction to this only programmatically.

Book II, Part 2 "The Kantian Error" (Der Kantische Irrtum, ch. 15–17); Part 3 "The Sensualist Error" (Der sensualistische Irrtum, ch. 18–20); Part 4 "Thing and Image" (Ding und Bild, ch. 21–23); Book III "Consciousness and Experience" (Bewußtsein und Erlebnis), Part 1 "Consciousness as a Disturbance of Life" (Das Bewußtsein als Lebensstörung, ch. 24–28).

Chapter 15 — Subjectivized Space (Der subjektivierte Raum) [141–147]

Thesis. Kant's doctrine that space is an a priori form of intuition is incoherent. It runs together the alienating lived experience that gives us spatial actuality with the act that ascertains it, and so it is about the space-object (the conceived, geometrical space), not about actual spatial appearance. That conceived space really is subjective. Actual being-outside-one-another (Außereinander) never shows up in the Transcendental Aesthetic at all.

Argument. 1. Opening astonishment. Kant sets out to find the ground that makes knowledge possible, then places that ground inside knowing itself. Klages cites Nietzsche's jibe from Beyond Good and Evil that Kant answered "how is knowledge possible?" with "by virtue of a faculty" [141]. 2. Parity argument. Seeing colours needs a faculty of sight, hearing sounds a faculty of hearing, and perceiving extension perhaps a faculty of spatial intuition. Every such disposition is equally a condition of the capacity for experience. So either they are all a priori or none is. Saying "experience needs a capacity for experience" only restates the subject–object correlation already contained in the concept of experience. If space-intuition is a "modification of sensibility" just as colour-sensation is, nothing explains why only space gets apriority [142]. 3. Diagnosis. Klages offers a general maxim: a self-contradiction signals a substitution. Kant's real aim is to ground the apodictic certainty of geometry. He carries on Leibniz's split between vérités de fait and vérités de raison, so what he has in view is "the being of space", the space-object, and not its actuality. The capacity to be affected by the spatial thereby becomes a capacity to produce it, the way the geometer produces points and lines. Klages calls this the confusion of "alienating lived experience" (entfremdendes Erleben) with "ascertaining apprehension" (feststellendes Erfassen), and later names it Kant's proton pseudos [143, 145]. 4. Five objections (a reductio sequence). - (i) If space were in the mind, we could find it only there, because finding ascertains but does not alienate. - (ii) A mind that contains space would itself be spatial, a volume. Whatever encompasses space is also encompassed by space. Retreating to "the condition under which space appears" leads to the same self-cancellation [143–144]. - (iii) Circularity: "outer sense" and "inner sense" borrow their character from the space/time opposition they are meant to ground. Kant's opening sentence, that through outer sense we represent objects "outside us", is a tautology [144]. With this, spontaneity/receptivity collapses too, because receiving presupposes being-outside-one-another. "Empty thoughts" and "blind intuitions" are "wooden iron": contentless thoughts cannot exist, and intuitions without concepts exist and are not blind ("compare the animal world!"). - (iv) An affectable inwardness that is spaceless is inconceivable, since space and time are polar and neither appears without the other. By de-spatializing inwardness Kant de-temporalizes it, and so swaps the experiencing life-principle for the thinking spirit [145]. - (v) The decisive one: objective space is not identical with the actuality of spatial appearance. The space-object arises through acts of delimitation. A boundary of space is not itself space, and the boundless uniformity that the concept of a boundary presupposes cannot come from the boundary-drawing spirit. Kant's "faculty of intuition" is "the willing helpmate" of mathematical understanding and nothing besides. Klages quotes Kant: intuitions are "nothing to us" unless taken up into consciousness [145–146]. 5. Concession. Kant's space is indeed subjective, as a thought-thing that would vanish with the spirits related to it. But no one would ever have posited it without the compulsion that the lived experience of being-outside-one-another exerts on spirit [146]. 6. Question deferred. If the space-object and the existing thing both get the predicate "existence", the difference between them must lie in different characters of the experienced compulsion. Answering this would explain the necessary link between space and time and the thing's dependence on both, which no epistemologist has asked about [146–147]. 7. Lotze. Lotze demolishes Kant's grounds for apriority and shows that mathematical points and lines are not mind-independent, yet he still concludes "space is in us". Klages likens this to the Eleatic denial of motion. Only someone who has stopped confusing spirit with soul, thinking with lived experience, and being with actuality will find this "lunacy" incomprehensible [147].

Key terms. - Raumgegenstand (space-object): conceived, objective, geometrical space. - Wirklichkeit des Raumes vs Sein des Raumes: actuality vs being of space. - Außereinander: being-outside-one-another, the experienced spatial actuality. - entfremdendes Erleben vs feststellendes Erfassen / feststellende Urteilstat: the alienating lived experience vs the ascertaining act of judgement. - proton pseudos: Kant's founding confusion.

Contribution to the whole. This applies Book I's actuality/being distinction (ch. 1–6) and the act theory of ch. 10–12 to Kant. It sets up "sense of actuality" (ch. 16) and "beholding" (ch. 17). The question of different compulsions behind thing and space-object points ahead to the doctrine of the Here-and-Now and Body/Thing (ch. 58–59).

Critical note. The strongest point is (v): geometric, delimited space plausibly presupposes an undelimited given that it cannot itself produce. Objections (i)–(ii) read "space is in the mind" as literal containment, which is the very spatial reading of "in" that Kant's transcendental idealism disowns. Klages anticipates this reply ("merely the condition"), but his answer, that such a condition would itself have to be found in space like a stone or tree [143–144], assumes what is in dispute: that whatever conditions spatial appearing must itself be spatial. The parity argument also ignores Kant's actual reason for privileging space: it is the form shared by all outer sensations, not one more sensation among them.

Chapter 16 — Preliminary Remarks on the Sense of Actuality (Vorläufiges vom Wirklichkeitssinn) [148–155]

Thesis. Idealism's best "evidence", the dream, does not show that spatial actuality is subjective. Idealism and positivism both rest on the confusion of perceiving with representing (Vorstellen). Perception is marked by an undergone compulsion that does not come from the soul. Representation is a mode of thinking and depends on the will.

Argument. 1. Calling space a fact of consciousness de-realizes it. Both idealism's proofs and positivism's refutations misstate the question of actuality. The tradition Klages has in view runs from Descartes through Berkeley to Schopenhauer [148]. 2. The dream argument dissected. The idealist says dreams show that spatial appearance can arise without spatial actuality, so waking space too is only in the self. Klages replies that the dreamer does not deny the dream-space actuality; only the waking person does. So unreality is no quality of the dream-images themselves. The experience of actuality is presupposed whenever one asks whether sensory space is "in the soul" [149]. The fact that different people's dream-spaces differ does no more damage than the fact that their sensory spaces differ. 3. The right question is by what right the waking person denies dream-space an extra-personal actuality. Idealism instead abolishes extra-personal actuality altogether. It ignores that no talk about space between persons would be possible unless all of them co-experienced the person-independence of its appearing [149–150]. 4. Against "The world is my representation" (Schopenhauer). Agreement about space requires that each person co-experiences a compulsion, "not stemming from the soul", to accept both that the spatial exists and how it is. I can represent whatever I like, but I cannot perceive the cloth as black rather than red, or see an unspatial "thing in itself" in place of extended actuality [150]. 5. Conceptual analysis of "representing". To represent is to think in the narrow sense of "thinking of something intuitable". The laws of representation are a subdivision of the laws of thought. Self-arising phantasms (daydream, dream) are distinct from representations: they take consciousness captive and claim full actuality [150–151]. 6. The paleness and gaps of represented content show that representation is measured against perception and takes all its intuitive material from impressions. Idealism equates representation with perception because it has struck the impression-experience (Eindruckserlebnis) out of perceiving and kept only the spiritual act. By doing so it destroys its own starting distinction between perceiving and perceived, consciousness and object. Whoever turns actuality into a fact of consciousness "has thereby at once annihilated consciousness itself" [151–152]. 7. Appeal to authority. Klages quotes at length from G. E. Schulze's Kritik der theoretischen Philosophie (1801), whose demolition of Kant he says was ignored out of "interest-dependence of judgement". The quoted points are these [152–155]: - intuition presents something different from the subject; - if intuitions were representations, everything would be thought of as absent; - the represented scent of a rose would please as much as the real one; - even God acting on an intelligence confined to its representations would yield only more representations; - the inference from representation to external object is a philosophers' invention, traced to Descartes; - mathematics' truth rests on intuitions; - Kantian idealism is no better than Berkeley's, and the post-Kantians who derived the world from reason alone were simply being consistent. 8. Once the subjectivity of space falls, the subjectivity of time falls with it, because a spatial actuality outside time is unthinkable [155].

Key terms. - Wirklichkeitssinn (sense of actuality). - Vorstellen (representing) = thinking of something intuitable; will-dependent. - Wahrnehmen (perceiving): rests on an undergone impression. - Phantasma: self-arising image that disempowers the will (dream, daydream). - Eindruckserlebnis (impression-experience). - "compulsion not stemming from the soul": the mark of the perceptual given.

Contribution to the whole. The perceiving/representing distinction and the compulsion criterion are used again in ch. 20–23 and in the theory of the givenness of images (ch. 29ff.). The dream/perception relation is explicitly deferred ("will occupy us elsewhere"), presumably to the chapters on sense-space and waking/sleeping (ch. 31, 55–56).

Critical note. The reply to the dream argument is sharp: the dreamer's own experience of actuality cannot be used to show that actuality is a mental content. But the compulsion criterion cannot by itself tell perception from dream, since phantasms also bind and are alien to the will. Klages needs his further distinction between what is alien to the I and what is alien to the soul, and here he only postulates it. The chapter's second half argues by quotation from Schulze, not in Klages's own words.

Chapter 17 — Transition to the Concept of Beholding (Überleitung zum Begriff des Schauens) [156–159]

Thesis. Kant's "thing in itself" is Parmenidean being under a new name, and it cannot be the ground of appearances. Still, Kant rightly tied epistemology to the question of intuition. The ground that makes knowledge possible must lie in lived experience, specifically in a deedless, temporal "beholding" (Schauen) of a stream of images. Since beholding alone cannot produce apprehension, the lived experience of actuality must contain at least one further partial process.

Argument. 1. Kant's terminology. Kant calls objects "appearances" (n. 16 gives Klages's equation for Kant: actuality = being = object = appearance). So there must be a further object to which they appear, the thing in itself. The upshot is that "happening becomes a moment of being", the phenomenal a property of the noumenal, and actuality the product of a supra-personal spirit [156]. The intuitus originarius of a "primal being" gives away the theological intent. "I", "consciousness as such", "the Absolute", "spirit", "will", "God" are all names for the same thing [156–157]. 2. Schulze's objection. Kant makes the thing in itself the cause of appearances while restricting causality to appearances [157]. 3. Klages's stronger objection. "The thing in itself appears" is as meaningful as "nothingness appears". If it lacks every determination drawn from appearances, it cannot bear their manifoldness. Pluralizing it into "intelligible characters" is worse than belief in ghosts. Thought through consistently, the position concedes an irreducible phenomenal/noumenal duality: the ground of space and time could not be the intelligible thing in itself [157–158]. 4. Kant's merit. He tied epistemology to the question of intuition, and so to the "world of images". He failed because his "intuition" is already knowledge and his intuited is already objective space and time [158]. 5. Klages's counter-thesis (phenomenological description). Space, time and sense-data cannot be separated in experience. There is no experienced colour or sound without spatio-temporal character, no experienced space not filled with sense-data and flowing, no experienced time except in the change of images. So the "matter + forms" composition gets the order backwards. Each element is obtained by looking away from the others. No conceptual synthesis could give the object its outside-us character unless the compelling actuality already had it [158–159]. 6. Definition. "Beholding lived experience" (schauendes Erleben), or "beholding", is the inward counterpart of the ceaselessly changing actuality of images. It is temporal and "wholly without deed", and it cannot find anything. Kant's "intuition" is really perception, an act resting on alienating experience. Klages's own use of Anschauung is reserved for later [159]. 7. Negative result. Apprehension could not occur on the basis of beholding alone. Something must explain how the poles separated into subject and object, and so into time and space. Hence the lived experience of actuality has at least two partial processes [159]. The second turns out to be sensing (ch. 22).

Key terms. - Schauen / schauendes Erleben (beholding): deedless, temporal, receptive counterpart of images. - Anschauung (intuition): kept distinct; defined later. - Ding an sich: unmasked as the Parmenidean One or the God of negative theology. - Strom der Bilder (stream of images).

Contribution to the whole. This chapter introduces the book's central positive concept, beholding, and states the programme: lived experience of actuality = beholding + another process. Ch. 20 fills it out as the beholding of images or essences, ch. 22 names sensing as the second process and intuition as their fusion, and ch. 29ff. ("Beholding and Mirroring") develop it.

Critical note. The objection that a determination-less ground cannot be the ground of determinate appearances is forceful. The phenomenological claim that colour, extension and duration are inseparable in experience is plausible and anticipates Gestalt critiques. But "beholding" is introduced as the required counterpart of a stream of images that has itself only been postulated. The two are defined by each other, and the chapter's own admission that beholding has so far been reached only by analysis of cognition [159] marks the gap.

Chapter 18 — Self-Contradictions of Sensualism (Selbstwidersprüche des Sensualismus) [160–166]

Thesis. Sensualism (the world consists of sensations) takes sensations to be stable elements that can be combined. In fact no sensation recurs identically. The sensed is always co-determined by accompanying impressions, prior history and mood. Sensualism's "elements" are therefore abstractions, and in truth they are the thing-properties that are the objective occasions of experience, not experience itself.

Argument. 1. Framing. Klages targets the sensualist world-view, not the psychology of sensation as such. He fixes its minimal creed: the world consists of sensations; esse est percipi [160]. 2. A catalogue of internal disagreements (a quasi-dialectical survey). Is the sensed in us? Are space and time sensed? Are things sensed? Is the subject sensed? Is association passive or active? The result is that the world is a bundle of representations, the representer is a bundle, and the bundle is in turn represented, all while claiming to have replaced scholastic word-play with research. This passage carries an anti-English polemic [161–162]. 3. Caveat. The real life-function of sensing "has not yet been discovered" (n. 17 refers to Vom Wesen des Bewußtseins). Until it has, "so-called" is to be understood before sensing, which for now is used as equivalent to perceiving [162]. 4. Abstraction argument. What is perceived is never "red" but a red raspberry, a drop of blood, an evening sky. These reds differ in kind, belong to different objects and stand in different places. "Redness" is reached only by looking away from place, thing and nuance. So no experiential atom should be sought, and any doctrine that tries to find one passes off knowledge of the objective partial cause as lived experience [162]. 5. Empirical argument from contextual dependence. Simultaneous contrast (red on black, white, green), cross-modal influence (sound and temperature affect sight), and successive dependence all mean the same red never produces the same impression twice, either for two persons or for one person at two moments. "Visual sensation" is a thought-thing reached by two omissions: of the accompanying sensations and of the temporal course [163]. 6. Mood-colour (Stimmungsfarbe). The whole outer world is steeped in the observer's mood: character, age, fatigue, even a passing thought. Blue pairs with dejection, red with elation. Sensation would then be one side, distinguishable but inseparable, of a life-process whose other side is feeling [163–164]. 7. Habituation and adaptation. Novelty "makes an impression" and familiarity extinguishes it, as in a city first visited versus one lived in for years. The term "stimulus" hides the fact that stimulus-value keeps changing. Klages quotes his own Vom Wesen des Bewußtseins on adaptation (n. 18) and Hobbes's dictum that to sense always the same is to sense nothing [164–165]. 8. Conclusion. Sensation is a never-motionless process, "a wave in the individually personal stream of life". Objective measurement of colours and other qualities refers to presupposed things, not to experience. Sensualism confuses sense experiences with their "transcendental occasioning grounds" and so spins a world of things out of a lived experience it has already reified. Platonizing logistic deals in judgements while pretending to deal with psychic life, and sensualist logistic deals in mechanisms while pretending to deal with sensuous life [165–166].

Key terms. - Sensualismus. - Empfindung (sensation), so-called. - Stimmungsfarbe (mood-colour). - Reiz (stimulus). - Abstumpfung / Adaptation (blunting / adaptation). - Veranlassungsgrund (occasioning ground) vs the experienced. - Logistik: Klages's name for logocentric intellectualism in its two varieties.

Contribution to the whole. This applies ch. 3–4 (non-identity, ceaseless change of the actual) to the sensory domain. The mood-colour argument prepares the sensation/feeling problem (ch. 19, 23, 24). "Sensing not yet discovered" is a promissory note redeemed in part in ch. 22 (sensing = bodily side, corporeality, waking).

Critical note. The argument from contextual dependence is strong against atomistic sensationalism of the Humean or early-Wundtian kind, and it parallels contemporary Gestalt work. It is weak against a sensationalism that treats sensations as theoretical posits rather than introspected atoms: "never identically recurring" does not rule out a functional type-identity. Klages concedes that a shade must be re-findable as belonging to an object, but assigns this to the object rather than to experience [164]. Yet ch. 23 has experienced characters "resemble one another" closely enough to be re-recognized [208–209], so experience itself must supply the similarity across moments that this chapter withholds from it.

Chapter 19 — The Sensualist Subjectivization of the World (Die sensualistische Subjektivierung der Welt) [167–173]

Thesis. Sensualism cannot even define its central concept. Sensed qualities are all equally heterogeneous to one another, so the "disparateness of sense-zones" belongs to stimuli, not to experience. "Objectivity" does not separate sensing from feeling or dreaming. The doctrine of specific sense energies subjectivizes all qualities, leaves the universe dark and mute, and cannot explain why qualities from different senses are located in one place in one space. Panlogism, Kantianism and sensualism are "three varieties of one and the same nihilism" [173].

Argument. 1. The moderate theorist's reply. Sensation is just an abstraction, and knowledge of thing-properties rests on sense organs (close your eyes and the colours vanish). Klages answers that the disparateness of sense-zones, offered as sensualism's footing in actuality, is its undoing [167]. 2. Mixture argument. Within a modality mixtures yield new qualities: red and yellow give an indivisible red-yellow, and a C-major chord is a new quality. Across modalities no mixture yields anything. But a mixture's indivisibility shows that "elementary sensations" confuse the experienced quality with its physical and physiological occasion. Only stimuli can be composed, never sensations [168]. 3. Universal heterogeneity. We learn what seeing is from the coloured, not the other way round. Objectively all qualities are heterogeneous: red is as different from blue as from a trumpet-sound, and the person who is red-blind can no more be given redness than the person born blind can be given colour. Then by what right are all these "species-receptive" processes put under one genus, sensing [168–169]? 4. Sensing vs feeling. Defining sensation by qualitative character fails, because feelings are equally qualitative: as many feeling-qualities as sensation-qualities. The sensation theorists' own "feeling-tone" for every impression concedes this [169–170]. 5. Definitions by "objectivity" from Wundt, Lipps and Ebbinghaus (n. 19) fail. "Object" means object of thought (virtue, world history), and what is meant is strangeness-to-consciousness. But dream-images and phantasms, and passions of the soul without any sensory input, have that strangeness too. The candle-ray producing a dream of a theatre fire does not make dreaming a kind of sensing. The ray, if sensed, is not a sea of flame, and "confused sensation" is ruled out by sensualism's own principle of exact correspondence between sensation and stimulus [170–171]. 6. Specific sense energies. This dogma puts the production of qualities in the sensorium, which leaves the universe with its suns in darkness and the "muteness of death" while the body's nerves conjure colours and symphonies. It is a worse derealization than the Eleatic denial of motion [171–172]. 7. Spatial-localization argument. Qualities as qualities have nothing spatial in them. So why are sensation-qualities projected into space and feeling-qualities not? Why is there one space rather than a space of sight, a space of touch and a space of hearing? Why is the red seen where the smoothness is felt, and the sweetness tasted in the white lump? How could purely internal states be spatially construed? Sensualism has to borrow a Kantian consciousness to "clamp" space and time onto qualities, which de-spatializes and de-temporalizes the universe as well [172–173].

Key terms. - Disparatheit der Sinnesgebiete (disparateness of the sense-zones). - spezifische Sinnesenergien: the doctrine (Johannes Müller's, though he is not named) of specific sense energies. - Bewußtseinsfremdheit (strangeness to consciousness). - Gefühlston (feeling-tone). - "nihilism": annihilation of the universe of images "through the point outside space and time".

Contribution to the whole. The unity of space across the senses is the problem to which ch. 21 answers "one soul with one image-space" and ch. 22–23 answer "the thing-point as place". It sharpens the sensing/feeling question that ch. 23 and 24 take up. It is also the first explicit unification of the book's opponents under "nihilism", picked up again in ch. 24 (consciousness).

Critical note. The localization argument (step 7) is the chapter's best: it is a genuine binding problem for any view on which qualities are mental states. Step 3 proves too much. If all qualities are equally heterogeneous, Klages's own later talk of kinships of character between high tones and spatial height (ch. 23) needs a non-objective similarity relation that he has not yet earned. He does flag that it is grounded in "expressive content".

Chapter 20 — From the Intuitive Image to the Image (Vom Anschauungsbilde zum Bilde) [174–182]

Thesis. This is the "Copernican reversal". What is experienced in sensibility is neither sensations nor thing-properties but sides of the appearance of images. Intuitive images are wholes in which substrate and property can be swapped, which ignore the boundaries between senses, and which are spatio-temporal events. They are appearances of essences (Wesen), living, besouled powers. The non-intuitive image or archetype (Urbild) is the giving life-centre; beholding (Schauung) is the receiving soul's intercourse with it.

Argument. 1. Sensualism's second error, taking thing-properties into the I, follows from its failure to keep experiencing and the experienced apart, and so to see the polar character of the life-process [174]. 2. Recasting quality. Space, and time mediately, share the alienness to the soul that qualities have. If change of brightness belongs to the impression, the flight of time is sensed. Impressions are singular: the yellow of a rose carries its scent, summer warmth and an unrepeatable stretch of life. There could be a science of "autumn-sensations" as easily as of colour-sensations [174–175]. 3. Least thing-like contents. Night, twilight, dawn, distance, sultriness, haziness, wind are as immediate as smells. Why have we no "night-sensations"? Because colours seem bound to things, while twilight cannot be described by thing-properties. It affects countless things and every colour differently according to distance and course. Its determinacy must come from elsewhere: from the image, which here does not mean copy or visual picture [175–176]. 4. Image-properties. The intuitive image confronts us as actual, as the thing does. But its features keep changing and differ in kind from thing-properties. In "the pennant fluttering in the twilight" substrate and property can be swapped: the twilight belongs to the pennant's image and the pennant to the twilight's. The meaning-unity cannot be built from parts or broken into them. It can be dreamed with eyes closed, so it does not come solely from the eye [176–177]. 5. Trans-sensory argument. The fluttering pennant is both seen and heard, so the process that gives it is not bounded by the senses. It is not "the thing" that appears to both senses, because things are thought-in-addition reference points that cannot appear (ch. 6, 12). Without the intuitive image the thing could never be found or "divined" [177–178]. 6. Linguistic evidence. Ewe has distinct words for the child's gait, the adult's, the shambling gait, the laden gait, but none for gait as such (n. 20, Abegg and Westermann). Klamath marks direction relative to the speaker, visibility, presence and shape in its pronouns and prefixes. These naming units are intuitive images, not things [178–179]. 7. Line and noise. Seen as an image-property, any resting line is a movement: it creeps, runs, rises (Otto Fischer, n. 23; note 22 rejects the geometric "motion of a point"). Nietzsche says a sentence "strikes, leaps, plunges". Pure tones are never heard, only noises, for which German has a vast vocabulary. In Schiller's "es wallet und siedet und brauset und zischt" the unbiased hearer perceives an event, a "water filled with demonic powers" (hence the plural die Wasser) [179–181]. 8. Three numbered findings [181–182]. - (1) The intuitive image is an event, and the thing is its unchanging component. - (2) In the image, actual space and time are immediately present: polar, interconnected, placeless, formed yet unbounded. In the thing they are only relations of an extra-spatiotemporal point. - (3) What holds the image together is that the happening "lives through and through". Images are appearances of living, besouled powers: "an actuality of essences appears". The thing's unity is enforced by positing. The image's unity rests on the dependence of all traits on the essence's inwardness. 9. Archetypes. Beholding is "an inwarding of an outwarding" (Innerung einer Äußerung), the intercourse of a receiving life-centre with a giving one. Klages calls the latter "image or archetype" (Bild oder Urbild). Archetypes are non-intuitive (unanschaulich), so beholding stands sharply apart from intuition, and intuition from perception. Things are described by properties. Images "cannot be described" but only "called into appearance" by pointing to their character [182].

Key terms. - Anschauungsbild (intuitive image) = Erscheinung (appearance). - Bild / Urbild (image / archetype): non-intuitive, the giving pole. - Bildeigenschaft vs Dingeigenschaft (image-property vs thing-property). - Wesen (essence): living, besouled power. - Schauung (beholding) vs Anschauung (intuition) vs Wahrnehmung (perception). - Innerung einer Äußerung.

Contribution to the whole. This chapter is pivotal: it introduces the image-doctrine that Book V (ch. 55–75, "The Actuality of Images") develops in full, and the essence/daemon reading of the world that culminates in Pelasgianism (ch. 71–75). The three-level hierarchy of beholding → intuition → perception structures ch. 22–23 and 29ff.

Critical note. Steps 3–7 make a strong phenomenological case that perceptual meaning-units are holistic and cross-modal, and that thing/property analysis is secondary. The step from "the image is an event that is lived through" to "an actuality of essences (souls) appears" [181–182] is asserted. It trades on "lives" meaning both "is experienced as dynamic" and "is a living being". The appeal to Schiller's demonic waters reads poetic animism as evidence rather than as something needing explanation.

Chapter 21 — Impression-Qualities in the Service of the Presentation of Essence (Eindrucksqualitäten im Dienste der Wesensdarstellung) [183–194]

Thesis. What was originally lived in sensory intuition is essences and their character-traits, not things and properties. The proof is linguistic and phenomenological. The same intuitive name covers thing-incommensurable phenomena, and every name for an intuitive trait serves to characterize human character. Sensory processes are interpretive processes of a single soul that discloses one image-space. Even touch, the most "sensation-laden" sense, beholds characters.

Argument. 1. Twilight as essence. Its name-meaning lies not in optics but in its essence, which only appears optically. So its optical form should be replaceable, the way a smile's mood can be expressed otherwise. That is borne out: "dimming" names the hazy mountain rim at noon and the coast vanishing at sea, so it is distance as well as darkening. Goethe gives twilight through distance: "Schon ist alle Nähe fern" [183–184]. 2. Synaesthetic vocabulary. Klages quotes his own Vom Wesen des Bewußtseins, n. 24: warm and cold colours; rough bark, noise and climate; sharp knife, whistle and vinegar; tone-colours; high and low tones. If essentiality is "bound to appear", human character should be described by thing-property names, and it is: Götterdämmerung, "twilit state", es dämmert ihm, "flightiness" (Flatterhaftigkeit) [184–185]. 3. Spatial value-metaphors. Long lists of high/low, above/below, great/small and narrow/wide terms (high-minded, superiority, downcast, petty, broad-minded, shallow, profound), plus a "sample card" of characterological idioms. These show a "primal relation of man to intuitive space" in which the directional poles were "the directing poles of the souls of the All". Hence also the symbolism of the head: chief, capital [185–186]. 4. Why "powers" and not "forces". Physical force implies a timeless and spaceless substrate of constant efficacy, and it lacks direction (n. 25: the vector direction of physics is abstract). In physics every mover is moved from outside, so there is no active/passive distinction, just as geometry has no left/right. Powers are self-moved and either active or passive: the line rises, the twilight comes, the moon hides. Language preserves "the myth-world of the daemons" [186–187]. 5. Words that cross senses. Weinen (tears and sound), rauschen, schluchzen, melting, scratching. Dumpf (musty air, dull sound). Hell once meant "sounding" (Hall), hence "helle Klänge" and Goethe's "Welch' Getöse bringt das Licht". "Oppressive darkness" and the child's experience of darkness lying on the chest connect to the incubus legends [187–188]. 6. Positive reinterpretation of sensory physiology (an inference to the best explanation). On the sensation theory three riddles stay insoluble: how we know countless intuitive contents that cannot be resolved into sensations; why what we know are parts of an outer world; and why the same names characterize living essences. They dissolve if the image-giving process belongs to the soul, with the senses rendering "indispensable services" [188–189]. There is one soul with one image-space (Bildraum), the counter-pole of the soul. The retuning of the body through the optic-nerve process "awakens in the soul the faculty of beholding", and a colour-appearance arises in image-space. Every sense-process shakes the whole cell-federation. So-called sensations are "interpretive processes" disclosing the image. Since image-space flows in time, each interpretation depends on the earlier ones, as in reading a difficult text [188–189]. 7. Like to like. What appears to the soul is originally a living power: enticing or threatening essences for animals, character-revealing essences for humans with higher beholding-power. It is not vitality but "the understanding of a vitality endowed with spirit" that kills off the images until only things, and perhaps one day only numbers, remain [189–190]. 8. Touch. Mechanics reduces everything to pressure-differences. If the measurable pressure-resisting side were what is sensed, everything specific would be beheld. Activities (tug, knead, weave) and touch-qualities (stone, velvet, flour, dough; smooth, sticky, brittle; stony, silky, doughy) cannot be reduced to magnitudes of pressure. Touch "has eyes". Fabric connoisseurs, shoemakers and the Persian tasting rice with his fingertips show this. Hearing tells needles from buttons and glass from clay [190–192]. 9. Colour words as a limiting case. Colour symbolism (white for innocence, green for hope) is mediated by thought. But Indo-European colour terms were originally indeterminate. White meant bright or shining (Weißwein), black meant dark (Schwarzbrot, schwarzer Verdacht), green meant the growing (auf einen grünen Zweig kommen). Colours were only later fixed as thing-properties, which raises the question of colour's special affinity for the thing [193–194].

Key terms. - Wesen (essence) and Charakterzug (character-trait). - Mächte (powers) vs Kräfte (forces). - Bildraum (image-space): the soul's counter-pole. - Deutungsvorgang (interpretive process). - Schauvermögen (beholding-faculty). - "only like accords with like".

Contribution to the whole. This gives the evidential base for the essence-doctrine of ch. 20 and the powers/forces contrast that returns in ch. 51 ("On Cause and Force"). The single image-space of one soul answers ch. 19's localization puzzle. The question of colour's affinity for things is answered in ch. 23. The daemon motif anticipates ch. 26 (the "It") and Book V.

Critical note. The chapter makes a rich and largely accurate case that much human characterological vocabulary is synaesthetic and spatial in origin. It does not show that the direction of dependence is essence → sense rather than shared bodily-affective resonance projected outward. The step from "language preserves the myth-world of the daemons" to the soundness of that myth-world [187] is the hidden premise. Step 6 is argued only as the better explanation of the three riddles; the physiological mechanism itself ("retuning" of the body awakening beholding) is stipulated.

Chapter 22 — Sense-Experience and Experience (Sinneserlebnis und Erfahrung) [195–199]

Thesis. The "given" is not raw sense-material but the ever-complete totality of temporally connected intuitive images. Experience (Erfahrung) is the never-ending dissection of that totality by spirit, not a building-up. The life of the senses forks into two functions: beholding (the soul-side, present in sleep and waking) and sensing (the bodily side, which grounds waking and gives the images their corporeality). A third process, intuition, fuses the two.

Argument. 1. Recapitulation. Qualities exist only in relation to their opposites and to the manifold of qualities. If the whole world showed one red, there would be no consciousness of it and no concept of colour. Like the three dimensions, it would be a property of space that could not be lived [195–196]. 2. Defining sensation as a "difference of sensation" fails, because then all contents become equally perceptible somethings without specific difference [196]. 3. The act that separated space from time also separated specific qualities from both. That made it seem that the colour-field could be resolved into colours and recomposed. Lived colour depends on neighbouring colours, on preceding colours and on the sound- and scent-images. The isolation is as impossible as separating a colour from its brightness [196]. 4. General thesis. The "given" is the consciousness-independent, completed totality of temporally linked intuitive images. What is generated is the objective world of experience, through a dissection that can never be finished. Appearance is not patched together from elements. Things are cut out of the perfection of appearance. Conscious cognition continues what involuntary experience began. Science studies the genesis of rational knowledge of appearance, not the genesis of appearance itself. Klages calls this "the insight standing at the threshold of a prima philosophia" [196–197]. 5. The fork. Apprehension requires waking and so sensing. So sensing is suspected to be what separates beheld images from the state of the beholder. Beholding is the soul-side and its counterpart is the image. Sensing is the bodily side and its counterpart is the being-for-itself, or corporeality (Körperlichkeit), of the images. Beholding goes on in sleep and waking. Sensing grounds waking. Apprehension rests on sensing wakefulness, and apprehensible thinghood rests on sensible corporeality [197–198]. 6. Sensualism's errors restated. It confused sense-experience with perception, suppressed the spiritual act, had to derive both boundary (thing) and matter from one event, and missed that the senses are organs of both body and soul [198]. 7. Programme. Between beholding and sensing lies intuition (Anschauung). Seen from the side of sensing, the question is how corporeality is experienced in images. Seen from the side of beholding, it is how the non-intuitive happening comes to appear, that is, how beholding becomes an intuition always underpinned by corporeality [198–199].

Key terms. - das Gegebene (the given) redefined. - Erfahrung (experience) = dissection (Zerlegung). - Sinneserlebnis (sense-experience). - Schauen vs Empfinden (beholding: soul; sensing: body). - Körperlichkeit (corporeality of images). - Anschauung (intuition, the fusing third). - prima philosophia.

Contribution to the whole. This is the hinge chapter of the block. It cashes in ch. 17's "at least two partial processes" and fixes the body/soul assignment of sensing/beholding that Book V uses (Soul and Body, ch. 58–61; Waking and Sleeping, ch. 55–56). It also establishes the analysis-not-synthesis model of knowledge that underlies all later chapters on thing-finding.

Critical note. The reversal of "given" is philosophically important and well motivated by the holism arguments. There is no outright clash with ch. 21: what ch. 21 called "interpretive processes" were the falsely so-called sensations, i.e. the beholding contribution, whereas genuine sensing is here the body's side. The real gap is that sensing must both separate image from beholder (the ground of waking and of thing-apprehension) and, to count as a life-process at all, be ensouled by beholding [199]. How one function does both is not shown. The two-function model is laid down "provisionally" and by stipulation.

Chapter 23 — On the Beholding-Capacity of the Senses (Vom Schauvermögen der Sinne) [200–214]

Thesis. What is experienced is never thing-properties but similarities and oppositions of character. Similarities come from kinship of essence and oppositions from conflict of essence. The senses are "messengers of the soul", each in its own way giving access to the same spatio-temporal powers. A thing-property is an image-quality cut off from its essence-context and fixed to a place posited by spirit. Sight inclines to the spatial pole and hearing to the temporal, which is why colour has the strongest affinity with the thing.

Argument. 1. Tones are high or low, bright or dark, heavy or light. There is a kinship of character between high tones and spatial "above". Generally, what is compared across incomparables is their expressive content [200]. 2. Objection: essences and things are incomparable. A high tone is necessarily high, whereas a red ball could be green. Klages's reply: language names neither "sensations" nor the individual thing. "A smooth, red, solid sphere of 10 cm" is made of general concepts, and property is a relational concept (ch. 11). So the question of the finding of properties comes before the finding of the thing. The answer lies in a sensibility that divides each being's world into friendly or alluring powers and hostile powers, and at the primordial-human level into polar, complementary ones [200–201]. 3. Essences cut across categories. There are gods of water and of a particular spring, of night, of wrath, of oath-swearing. In the essence-world every predicate can become a subject. The tendency to reify universals (light, warmth) has a vital source: we say "this brown is warm". "The world of congealed 'Ideas' offers the silhouette of an actuality of once-living gods" [201–202]. 4. Objection from dependence on organs. Rattling and pattering are heard and so absent for the deaf. Klages grants that sensory excitation is indispensable. Only the hearing perceive noises (n. 26 discusses whether the congenitally blind might dream colours). But hearing perceives the same space-time powers that appear to sight and touch in other ways, so they must also be knowable to the deaf, just not as noises. Physiology studies "sensibility minus its ensoulment" [202–204]. 5. Physiognomic and "occult" evidence. Reading faces at a glance, women who "see through" people, horoscopy, psychometry (picking out the murder axe), telepathy, clairvoyance, dowsing. These are taken as manifestations of "primordial beholding" that thing-consciousness must deny or call supersensible. This step is asserted, not argued [204–205]. 6. Romantic "Ur-sense" and animal instinct. The Romantics' "central sense" or "all-sense", from which the senses branched, opened access to somnambulism and to instinct. Treviranus asks how animals recognize water and food. Klages adds the brooding hen, the duckling, the silkworm, the hermit crab, the gnat, the leopard, the horse finding water, animals sensing earthquakes, the homing pigeon. None can be understood from capacities of spirit [205–207]. 7. Love, aversion, idiosyncrasy. Instant attraction or repulsion from a figure or voice, the dog following the bitch's trail, the bull and the red cloth, ants and blue light. "Association" only traces image to image and stalls at elementary sensations, which are themselves expressive traits. Klages quotes his own Vom Traumbewußtsein (n. 27) on scent mood-images (rose: mild fullness; jasmine: hot intoxication). Reiz means charm as well as stimulus [207–208]. 8. Essence-feelings (Wesensgefühle). Sense-mediated feeling is as referable to the world as a sense-datum. Feeling can speak (a) of the feeler's state, (b) of the relation of state to impression, or (c) of the impression's own life-content. Case (c) is the "felt character of the image", or essence-feeling. Essence-feelings are effects, not causes, of beholding. Characters recur closely enough to be recognized, while feeling-tones fluctuate. The essence-revealing mark is depth (traded off against intensity), and its extreme is Ekstasis, being beside oneself, in which beholding is completed and the soul joins "the pulse-beat of universal happening" [208–209]. 9. The "how" (Wiebeschaffenheit). Is "warm red" a mere metaphor? A table's "leg" is a transference: the similarity can be pointed out intuitively. The warmth of a stove and the warmth of a red cannot be pointed out as similar. So transferences presuppose finished things, while similarity of qualities belongs to the experience that first makes thing-properties findable. Actuality is experienced and being only thought. So, beside the actuality and being of space, time and motion, Klages now distinguishes the actuality of the how from the being of the how [210–211]. 10. The place. The thing-point is not temporal but time-persistent. It links timeless universals by the concept of possible simultaneity. A ball cannot be red and blue at once but can be red and warm at once, so colours exclude colours and are independent of temperatures. "Severed from its essence-context, the image-quality becomes the timeless specific property of a thing, insofar as it falls to the sphere of effect of the place posited by the spirit." Essence-appearance is placeless. The genuine process of sensation is to supply the vital occasion of place. The order of dependence is thereby reversed: the dispute over the number of senses and the double use of "it tastes"/"it smells" show that the senses are hard to keep apart, so the classification of the senses presupposes the classification of properties by their relation to "the selfsameness of a place", not the other way round [211–212]. 11. Open questions. Why are there no generic names for smells? Why are sounds depicted only by verbs? Why do only colours depict things? Why "warm colours" but not "coloured warmth"? Sight and hearing are polar, like space and time. Colour covers the surface of the moment and is a mark of space. Sound articulates happening and is a mark of time. The time-persistent thing has more affinity with space, and so with colour. Sound "hovers midway" and "does not cease to speak the language of essences" [212–214]. 12. Transition. The concept of experience must now be measured against "its most dangerous adversary, the modern concept of consciousness" [214].

Key terms. - Schauvermögen der Sinne (beholding-capacity of the senses). - Wesensverwandtschaft / Wesenswiderstreit (kinship / conflict of essence). - Wesensgefühl (essence-feeling). - Ekstasis. - Wirklichkeit des Wie vs Sein des Wie (actuality vs being of the how). - Stelle / Ort (the place posited by spirit). - Übertragung (transference / metaphor) vs experienced similarity. - Ursinn / Allsinn (Romantic primal sense).

Contribution to the whole. This completes Book II by deriving thing-properties from image-qualities plus the posited place. The place theme is developed in "The Here and Now" (ch. 58) and "Body and Thing" (ch. 59). The material on instinct and the occult is later taken up in the doctrine of drives (ch. 44–46) and of elementary similarity (ch. 34). Ekstasis returns in ch. 27, and the experience/consciousness confrontation opens Book III.

Critical note. The criterion distinguishing metaphor from experienced similarity (step 9) is the chapter's sharpest move and a real contribution to the theory of synaesthetic metaphor. But "not pointable" could equally mark a conventional or associative link. The derivation of incompatibility among colours from competition for one place (step 10) is elegant. Treating clairvoyance, psychometry and dowsing as established facts [205] is unargued and weakens the case built on instinct.

Book III — Consciousness and Experience (Bewußtsein und Erlebnis)

Chapter 24 — Critique of the Prevailing Conception of Consciousness (Kritik der herrschenden Bewußtseinsauffassung) [217–228]

Thesis. The modern concept of consciousness is a hybrid that draws life into spirit. It confuses lived experience with knowledge about lived experience, and so makes psychology a disguised epistemology. Its counterpart, the "unconscious", only duplicates consciousness ("an unconscious consciousness"), and in contemporary form it fills the ground of life with a calculating understanding. "Consciousness" is "the basic malady of contemporary thought" [220].

Argument. 1. Framing. As long as logocentric inquiry keeps spirit and life apart, it is harmless. A hybrid concept that "imperceptibly draws life into spirit" is fatal. The Greeks' superiority on the problem of life comes partly from their lacking this concept [217]. 2. Etymology. Bewußtsein is the infinitive of "I am conscious of", meaning I know of, take note of. Two features follow and recur from Augustine (and vaguely the Neoplatonists) through Descartes: the self-certainty of consciousness, and the "gathering together" of everything by the relating I. Descartes' cogitare, Malebranche's raison and Spinoza's intellectus mean the same [217–218]. 3. The core fallacy. To be conscious of what I sensed, dreamed or fantasized, I must have sensed it, and so on. "Consequently consciousness consists of" those processes. The Eleatic confused actuality with being; the theorist of consciousness confuses lived experience with knowledge of it. So "psychology" and "epistemology" share one object, and the border dispute between them never ends [218]. 4. The unconscious. There are two senses. The functional sense: the absorbed reader who recalls the clock-strike. The dispositional sense: latent knowledge and skills. Both mean processes "not yet or no longer" in consciousness but of the same kind. Leibniz's petites perceptions make it "an unconscious consciousness". This doubles what is to be explained, like deriving light from invisible light [218–220]. 5. Parody. The contemporary unconscious plans, schemes and calculates like a shrewd trader. Then come "preconscious", "subconscious", "superconscious", "co-conscious". The spatial mythology of a bright room and a dark room, threshold, narrowness, antechamber, and a corruptible doorkeeper who lets repressed contents slip through in disguise, is an unmistakable though unnamed caricature of Freud. Klages's quarrel is not with figurative speech but with misleading figures that explain nothing [220–222]. 6. The nihilistic point. If the place of the life-ground is taken by an unconscious, the way to life is barred by a "complete logicization of lived experience". Consciousness lets experiences have understanding; the unconscious lets a hair-splitting understanding have lived experience [222]. 7. Perception vs apperception. Splitting consciousness into a merely "having" and a "knowing" consciousness ("in" consciousness but not "for" it; Theodor Lipps, Bewußtsein und Gegenstände) is a Laocoön-like struggle. The star unnoticed in the evening sky is not "perceived by consciousness". Lipps, whom Klages calls the sharpest recent analyst, ends in "a Fichteanism of psychology". His chain runs: actuality = factuality = experienced demand of an object; unity is found only in the gathering act; therefore actuality is the creative doing of a transcendent I (n. 28 quotes Lipps). Whoever speaks of "experiences of consciousness" has already turned experiences into thoughts and abolished the being-outside-one-another that consciousness requires [222–224]. 8. Hidden motive. Lipps had to acknowledge two classes of conscious "content": sensed blue gives the mark of a thing, and felt joy gives the mark of a consciousness. Once both are locked inside consciousness, the difference between sensing and feeling cannot be stated. This leads to the tortured "self-observation", like trying to catch one's own gaze in a mirror or the moment of falling asleep [224–226]. 9. A catalogue of resulting errors. Locke's "inner perception"; emotions as confused thoughts (Descartes, Leibniz) or as bodily sensations (James–Lange); pleasure/displeasure classification; monistic drive theories (Schopenhauer's will, self-preservation, the physicians' "sexus"); "feeling-tone"; finality imputed to the life-ground; reductive "stoichiometry" of the person [226]. 10. Why not just call the unconscious "life"? Klages compares the history of "immaterial" becoming "spiritual". That opposition was one of kind, like light and heat. "Unconscious" is gradational, like dark and bright, so it is built from the substance of consciousness. Romanticism's living unconscious (the "all-life") could not connect with earlier consciousness theories or check later ones. Better to use "unconscious" only adjectivally, for the absence of consciousness-making acts [226–228].

Key terms. - Bewußtsein: knowing-of; a hybrid or Mischbegriff when made to contain experience. - das Unbewußte (functional / dispositional senses). - Doppelbewußtsein (double consciousness). - Perzeption / Apperzeption. - Bewußtseinserlebnis (consciousness-experience): a non-concept. - Logisierung (logicization of lived experience).

Contribution to the whole. This opens Book III by turning the experience/knowledge distinction of ch. 10–14 against psychology. It sets up the positive theses of ch. 25 (no experience is conscious) and ch. 26 (continuity of experience vs gappy consciousness). The critique of Lipps foreshadows ch. 40 (Palágyi) as the positive counter-model.

Critical note. The diagnosis that "consciousness" equivocates between the experiencing and the knowing-of is acute and anticipates later critiques of the "Cartesian theatre". The argument against the unconscious works against the "same contents, minus awareness" model. It does not touch functional notions of unconscious processing that do not claim the same contents. There is also a tension with the next chapter. This chapter's catalogue of errors condemns imputing "purposefulness or 'finality'" to the ground of life [226], yet ch. 25 credits cell-interplay with a purposiveness "that simply cannot be surpassed" [231]. The two are consistent only if purposiveness is a description of outcomes rather than a mode of causation, and Klages has not yet drawn that distinction.

Chapter 25 — The Unconsciousness of the Life-Processes (Die Bewußtlosigkeit der Lebensvorgänge) [229–238]

Thesis. Lived experience as such takes place before consciousness: "no lived experience is conscious, and no consciousness can experience anything" [229]. Everyday automatisms, skills, recognitions, modern pathic literature and ancient doctrines of possession all testify that sensing, feeling, fantasizing and even purposive movement are not acts of consciousness. Seven guiding theses set out the positive doctrine: consciousness consists of point-like acts of reflection made possible by disturbances in the continuous stream of experience.

Argument. 1. The clock-strike again. Recalling it later shows that hearing occurred, and that it occurred without consciousness. This is first direct evidence for the "this-sidedness" of experience relative to consciousness, which had only been inferred. It is no exception: every so-called conscious appearance can be "merely a lived experience" [229]. 2. Textbook cases. The resolved non-smoker who finds a lit cigar in his mouth; winding the clock while changing clothes; James's house key at a friend's door; Huxley's veteran who drops his dinner on "Attention!"; recognizing a friend without being able to describe his features; sensitivity to disparagement without knowing its signs; not remembering the typeface; sight-reading; the marksman and the fencer (from James, n. 29–30). These are "impressions, inferences, conclusions… without the activity of apprehending, inferring" [229–230]. 3. Inversion of the usual moral. The usual view says habituation spares consciousness. Klages says a consciousness accompanying life-processes is inconceivable, and if one existed no action would ever happen. "It is not consciousness that senses, feels, fantasizes… produces movements" [230–231]. 4. Rebuttal of "the logic of the unconscious". These processes are purposive and error-free. Cell-interplay and wound-healing are equally purposive, and nobody infers cell-thinking. "Either the word 'think' has no meaning whatsoever, or it designates an activity of consciousness." Habit and practice show that reflection can be separated from every "content", so consciousness becomes a spectator whose sleep does not affect the tumult [231–232]. 5. Literary-historical evidence. Modern literature is full of figures driven by inner powers: Hamlet, Tasso, Hoffmann's Medardus and Cardillac, Eichendorff's Taugenichts ("pathics" of black and white magic), Peer Gynt, Dostoevsky. Klages quotes long passages from Crime and Punishment in which Raskolnikov arrives "mechanically" at Razumikhin's and at Svidrigailov's tavern, compared to post-hypnotic suggestion. The nineteenth century's mesmerism and hypnotism belong here too [232–235]. 6. Explanation of the shift. Mankind has not become more pathic. On the contrary, "progress" is the overpowering of the dreaming stance by the willing and waking one. What changed is the conception. Antiquity depicted the same states (Sophocles' Ajax, Euripides' Agave and Phaedra) as possession by daemons, with no "free will", no "unconscious" and no "consciousness" in the modern sense. Plato's Phaedrus on divine mania (prophetic, telestic, poetic) is quoted at length. Even "the proletarian Socrates", creator of the ideal of ever-understanding consciousness, conceded it [235–237]. 7. Thesis. The cultic opposition of "demons and men" made possible the conceptual opposition of phenomenon/noumenon, life/spirit [237]. 8. Seven guiding propositions [237–238]. - I. There are no "contents" or "processes" of consciousness, only temporally unextended acts of spirit, without experience of their own, that give consciousness of the lived. - II. Life is uninterrupted lived experience. Consciousness is the momentary achievement of the point-like act of reflection, and acts relate to one another only through the one stream of experience. - III. In the instant of reflection, what was lived has always already flowed away. - IV. Without disturbances in the stream, spirit and life would stay apart and beings would remain in vegetative unconsciousness. The reach of reflection matches life's susceptibility to disturbance. - V. The ultimate ground of disturbance lies in sensation. Constant wakefulness requires the temporal articulation of the stream, like a wave-motion. - VI. The act of reflection fixes the instant at which one wave ends and the next begins. It "puts division in the place of articulation", and the change of phase becomes consciousness of separate objects. - VII. What remains present of the flowed-away experience points to a difference between the act-bearing boundary-quality (Grenzqualität) and the act-less intervening qualities (Zwischenqualitäten). This is the basis of voluntary recollection and of judgement.

Key terms. - Bewußtlosigkeit (unconsciousness of life-processes), as opposed to the reified "unconscious". - Besinnungsakt (act of reflection). - pathisch / Pathiker. - Mania / possession. - Grenzqualität vs Zwischenqualitäten. - Störung (disturbance). - Gliederung vs Teilung (articulation vs division).

Contribution to the whole. The seven propositions are a programme for the rest of Book III and beyond. IV–V lead into ch. 26–27 (disturbance, gradient). VII's act-bearing boundary-quality returns in Book IV, where the passage through it grounds the derivation of the acts of apprehension and of will (ch. 41, 46). The pathic/possession theme anticipates ch. 26's "pathic soul" and Book V.

Critical note. The examples effectively undermine the identification of mind with consciousness, and the dilemma "either 'think' means a conscious act or nothing" is a clean conceptual point. But the examples support only "these occur without accompanying awareness". The strong thesis, "no lived experience is conscious", is offered not as their conclusion but as the psychological translation of the spirit/life dualism already "proven" [229]. It follows from Klages's point-act concept of consciousness, so the examples can confirm it but cannot test it. The literary evidence shows changing self-interpretation, which Klages admits, and so cannot also serve as evidence about the processes themselves.

Chapter 26 — Living and Mechanical Process (Lebendiger und mechanischer Vorgang) [239–254]

Thesis. Activity always belongs to an I and is inwardly transitive. Process belongs to an It. The mechanical process is an "intransitive It-process" that can neither cause nor be caused, so it does not exist in actuality. The life-process is "transitive": lived experience is always the undergoing of something by someone. The soul is "pathic through and through". Life is uninterrupted lived experience and consciousness is a gappy series of acts. Spirit and life are "two actualities hostile to one another from the ground up", with no third thing that unites them.

Argument. 1. Method. The life-process must be bounded both against the mechanical and against the spiritual act. Every act is I-activity and inwardly transitive (one thinks something, wills something). So processes, mechanical or living, cannot have the I as bearer [239]. 2. Mechanical process. To see a motion as mechanical we must disregard any liveliness in the moving thing. Physicists still speak transitively ("the steam drives the piston"), but they mean a finished state of affairs following another state and nothing self-active. Causes and forces are computational quantities stripped of activity [239–240]. 3. The impersonal pronoun (appeal to language). Es blitzt, es donnert, es regnet point to a bearer that is neither personal nor spirit-like. The It is late in the languages that have it and replaced a vanishing class of subjects: Greek Zeus huei became huei ("it rains"), and Zeus was originally a daemonic, not a personal, being. Grammatical gender is not personification: the river was a masculine living being before it became the seat of a male god. As consciousness separated from experience and life was narrowed to men and animals, the efficacious powers beyond the human lost determinacy, and the neutral It expressed this.
