This is not reification of night but evidence that originally all thing-names were essence-names. Hence hylozoism, and "night breaks in" [382]. 4. Linguistic evidence 2: against "transference" (etymology). Dictionaries say words first had a "physical" meaning later transferred to the soul. Max Müller: poets "draw forth again the latent metaphors of language". Sonnenblick < OHG blic = lightning (miners' Silberblick). The sun can "glance" because the eye could once "flash", so the transference ran backward and forward. The supposed priority of thing-thinking smuggles our matter-of-factness into word-formation: "linguistic atomism" founders as physical atomism does on the living [382–384]. Even real transferences (table-leg, wood anemone) happen through the pull of soul-related images [384]. 5. Against empathy/animism theory (polemic). Finding essence is not "poeticizing-in" or personification. Müller saw that "the roarer" for thunder did not mean a man with arms and legs, but then fell back on "mere simile". The real choice is not figurative versus literal but whether a roaring person is meant by the essence of roaring. The impersonal "it is nighting" (es nachtet) keeps the essence [384–385]. 6. Characterizing symbolic thinking. "Heavy heart", "warm colours", "high tones": for objective thinking these are similes. For symbolic thinking they were consciousness-independent actualities. Both kinds of thinking need things, but with reversed roles [385]. Aristotle on myths as "remnants of vanished wisdom" supports treating them as differences of kind, not developmental stages. Phenomenological parallel: the understanding uses symbols to compare things, while the soul uses things to find characters of appearance. One believes in the causability of noumena (physical "forces"), the other in the transformability of phenomena by living essences. Their series run in logically opposite directions: the lived similarity of spatial height and pitch versus the factual relation of air-vibration and tone [386]. 7. Genesis of the concept by dissection. Overemphasis on making-present threatens to swallow the happening of images into their omnipresent form. "Up" is not intuitively present in pitch, nor warmth in purple, so the pairs fall apart. The spiritual act then correlates tones with tones and colours with colours, which yields general concepts. These timeless universals need local anchoring in things, so overemphasis on embodiment is the complement: no longer for finding bodies, as in animals, but for anchoring universals. Idealism and materialism are opposite directions on the same plane of factual reflection [387]. "Warm redness" is an essence-appearance "struck apart" into factual redness and factual warmth. The isolated property then demands an external cause, which divides into dispositions and conditions. Factual "light" presupposes a symbolic "light", still heard in Lebenslicht [387–389]. Further treatment is deferred to ch. 38. Concepts "poor in meaning" (ch. 11) have lost all ties to intuition. Essence-research is "the irresistible lever that uproots the whole of mechanistic thinking" [389]. 8. Thing-like symbols. Serpent = cycle of life; bird = soul-messenger; meta sudans = phallic Gamos (Pelasgian examples). Klages targets 19th-century ethnologists ("confused representation and thing") and the current psychoanalytic reading ("primitive man as neurotic confusing wishes with facts"), and sneers at a "school" (Jung's, unnamed) whose talk of "primordial images" (after Burckhardt) he calls "venerable tomfoolery". Bachofen held the key (promised for Vol. III) [389–390]. 9. Reductio by examples. Greeks put a roof-tile or an axe on trial. Aztecs buried wooden images of the drowned. Chinese gave paper replicas to the dead. Lutheran and Catholic communion. None of these confuse objects. The key: "two kinds of sameness — the conceptual, or identity of matter, and the symbolic, or identity of essence" [390–391]. 10. Parity argument (tu quoque against science). Physics identifies a cumulus cloud, powder snow and ice as "water", and water with two gases, and organisms with four elements, with no intuitive similarity at all. That needs "a sum of hypotheses". Why should identity grounded on similarity be less legitimate? So symbolic truths are possible and immune to factual objection. Likeness and wine can be appearances of the soul of the original and of blood [391–392]. 11. Higher symbolic chains. The crescent moon is a boat, the boat is womb and grave, so the moon is the gondola of Isis, Persephone's region, Plutarch's "element of souls". Greeks did both image-service (Bilderdienst) and fact-service (Tatsachendienst) without keeping them apart. The lament: "The earth drips with the heart's-blood of shattered… images" [392–393]. Similarity derives from non-intuitive kinship of essence, which is why a tone ladder is possible and why name-identity barred marriage in China and India. "The essential kinship of two twins… need not be any greater than the… kinship of a thing with its name" [393–394]. 12. Symmetry of error. Symbolic error lies next to symbolic truth, as logical error next to logical truth. Image-service becomes ghost-belief when it turns essences into things. Fact-service becomes "soul-denying dullness" when it turns its units of calculation into actualities. The greater horrors are on the side of fact-service [394].

Key terms. symbolisches / begriffliches (or sachliches) Denken. Wesen (essence, a living power that appears). Wesensbedeutung versus Sachbezogenheit. Einerleiheit: Sachidentität versus Wesensidentität (identity of matter versus identity of essence). Zergliederung (dissection) versus synthesis. Erscheinungscharakter (character of appearance). Bilderdienst / Tatsachendienst (image-service / fact-service). Gleichnis (simile).

Contribution to the whole. Develops ch. 35's Promethean stage and ch. 34's kinship/conflict into a theory of symbolic meaning. Prepares ch. 38's genesis of concepts and the wider theory of the "actuality of images" in Book V, especially ch. 72 (Bilderdienst und Wirklichkeitssinn), with Bachofen and the Pelasgian world-picture (chs. 71–75). It cashes out ch. 11 (concepts poor in meaning) and ch. 30 (idealism/materialism).

Critical note. The parity argument is the strongest point. Scientific identity claims (ice = water = H₂O) rest on theoretical identifications with no intuitive similarity, so intuitive similarity cannot be ruled out a priori as a ground of identity. But "identity of essence" is never given criteria that would separate symbolic truth from symbolic error. The only mark offered is formal (image-service becomes "ghost-belief" when it turns essences into things [394]); it says nothing about which essence-identifications are true, so the claimed immunity of symbolic truth to factual objection comes close to unfalsifiability. The etymological evidence is selective: blic shows that "transference" runs both ways, which refutes the priority of the physical sense but does not by itself show that an undivided essence-meaning came first.

Chapter 37 — The Mechanics of Association (Die Vergesellschaftungsmechanik) [395–412]

Thesis. "Association" is a magic word. It names a problem without solving it, and it lumps together processes of fundamentally different kinds. Mere co-occurrence of impressions never produces units of meaning. Those rest on interpenetration (Durchdringung) and on kinship of essence. Even seemingly "chance" connections by contiguity rest on a real kinship: shared participation in one appearance-space, and in the near-character or far-character of what has been lived. The only true exception is the "lending of sense" (Sinnbeleihung) by which the subject's passing feelings colour impressions. That opens a critique of "empathy" and an account of how depth and distance heighten the sense of actuality.

Argument. 1. Palágyi's prior critique (Naturphilosophische Vorlesungen). "Association-scholasticism" (a) labels rather than explains, and (b) invents its "factors" by logically comparing objects. There is always some category-box: Hume's three, James Mill's one [395]. 2. Eighteen cases gathered from current psychologies [396]: "Erlkönig" lines, cloud→storm, flame→warmth, rhymes, opposites, property–thing, thing–name, name–representation, sound–letter, recognition, remembering, recognizing a drawing, distance seen, roughness seen, tones fused into chords, the duration of a tone, feelings compounded into affects, rhythmic movement to song. The word explains "everything", so it might as well explain the moon's orbit [397]. 3. Reductio by distinguishing cases. No sane person takes lightning for a likeness of thunder, though lightning leads one to expect thunder. Warmth is a property of flame, thunder is not a property of lightning. Signs carry meanings inherent in them, whereas lightning is a forewarning, not a sign. Once language exists, everything is apprehended through some sign ("dog", "animal", "something"). The result is five irreducibly different groups: habitual sequence, property-into-thing, recognition in a likeness, understanding a sign, apprehension via a sign [397–398]. Distance-seeing and roughness-seeing are not even two-membered. The supposed components (eye-axes, tactile memories) are inferred, not co-present impressions [398]. 4. The principle. Only habitual sequence looks like the linking of two independent contents. The rest are two sides of one state of affairs that interpenetrate, with thing/property as the model. Mere consorting yields no unit of meaning. The waltz and the girl from the first dancing lesson stay forever separate even though one recalls the other [399]. 5. Diagnosis of the opponent. English "naturalism" confuses consciousness with lived experience. It produces quantifying atomism, astronomer-like noting of "contents", laws of factual simultaneity and similarity, and a commercial vocabulary ("associate"); it even made "the child into the cause of the sexual drive" (a jab at utilitarian-naturalist explanation, not at Freud). Real wholes come first and parts are found by dissection: a garment belongs to a delightful figure as in "warm red", and so do a painting, a melody, a net. The net opens symbolic series ("network", retina, snares). What needs explaining is not connection but the falling-apart of the inwardly united and the merely co-present [400–401]. 6. Even contiguity has a vital ground. Simultaneity goes back to the lived now, that is, sense-space (ch. 31). So every mood is underlaid by an image of surrounding space. One cannot recall a joy without a locality, and cannot date a thought without associating it with a place. Simultaneous processes are therefore "clamped together by a trait of similarity" [402], a kinship that factual thinking discards as accidental but that mattered to primeval appearance-oriented thought. 7. Near- and far-character. Each impression is rooted in the whole prehistory of the life-bearer (traces that alter and blur). The pastness of a recollection is measured by the fullness of what lies between: a rich week seems like months. Nearness and distance are essences in polar opposition, so images group by near- or far-character. Horizon and starry sky favour phantasms of distant pasts, while small-town bustle favours recent ones [403–404]. 8. Sudden ideas (Einfälle). Consciousness lingers only a moment and moves by leaps, so phantasms arrive suddenly. When the intermediate links stay unconscious the result looks like "inspiration": the copper fruit-bowl that brings up "Syndetikon" through an unnoticed fish ornament and the gloss "fish-glue". Little-steered thought follows the soul's pulsing and so yields discoveries [404–405]. 9. Chance and fate. "Accidents" exist only relative to conceived laws, that is, in the world of facts, not in the actuality of images. For symbolic thinking everything is necessary. Examples: Pythagorean rules ("Do not pluck apart the garland", "Do not turn back from the journey"), right/left on a level with east/west (richtig versus linkisch), place-accents (Spencer: the same sign on the pole of a house versus a temple), the different Madonnas of Maria Zell, Maria Schutz and Maria Wörth. Applied to accidents, necessity becomes Moira, Ananke, Heimarmene. Omens and astrology may be errors, but they express belief in the necessity of every world-image. Against the Christian "astra inclinant, neque tamen necessitant": "Astra necessitant", though not causally [405–407]. Hume confused causality with frequency of succession, and the concept of cause is deferred [407–408]. 10. Characterological use of chance connections. The bang heard as danger shows fearfulness, admiration for the charging bull an artistic sense, the match-fear a trauma. Stimulus-word tests for "complexes" (Jung's method, unnamed) are dismissed as a "parlor game". Feelings produce nothing. They are indications of disturbances in the balance of drives (the morbid fear of water) [408–409]. 11. The exception: lending of sense. Feelings about the impression's relation to one's drives and interests do colour its character, as the waltz has an excitation-value of its own. On the assumption of a necessary connection among all appearances, a room where a murder really took place would truly be uncanny (perhaps the murder was drawn there by the room), even for a receptive observer who knew nothing of it. A room merely believed to be the scene is a case of lending of sense that "would speak only of the lender". Barracks during the World War are an example. Only here is "empathy" (Einfühlung) a legitimate term [409–410]. 12. Separability and depth. Colours of occasion (Gelegenheitsfarben) fluctuate more than the impression's proper colours (Eigenfarben) and can be separated from them, as illumination is separable from body-colour. People differ in their inclination to empathize: at one pole the great "antique" artist (Eichendorff quoted), at the other the psychically disturbed. "Every 'subjectivism'… is on the way to psychic disturbance" [410–411]. Feelings can be ranked by how far they open the soul to images: love and veneration versus anxiety and hatred (deferred). Deeper moods give deeper glimpses, and depth and distance imply each other. Two consequences: distance in time (the return to a childhood landscape, ch. 28) and first moments are richer in distance. That is why professionals' exact groping endangers discovery, and why laymen often make first findings [411–412].

Key terms. Vergesellschaftung / Assoziation (association). Durchdringungsverhältnis (relation of interpenetration). Zufallsverknüpfung (chance-linkage). Nahcharakter / Ferncharakter (near- / far-character). Einfall (sudden idea). Sinnbeleihung (lending of sense). Eigenfarbe versus Gelegenheitsfarbe (proper colour versus colour of occasion). Einfühlung (empathy), restricted. Wirklichkeitssinn (feeling for actuality).

Contribution to the whole. Applies chs. 31–36 polemically. It rests on the sense-space = now identity (ch. 31), elementary similarity and kinship (ch. 34), and symbolic necessity (ch. 36). It foreshadows the doctrine of feelings as indicators (Book IV), the theory of nearness and distance (Book V, chs. 62–65), and fate and omens in the Pelasgian world-picture (chs. 71–75). The concept of cause is deferred (treated in ch. 51, "On Cause and Force").

Critical note. The typology of the eighteen cases is an effective conceptual analysis. It shows that "association" covers sign-relations, part–whole relations and mere expectation, and this is Klages at his most convincing. But the move from "contiguous impressions share one sense-space" to "they are kin in essence" uses "similarity" so broadly that anything co-present becomes kin. The thesis that nothing is accidental in the actuality of images then becomes unfalsifiable, and Klages himself grants that on the ground of symbolic thinking there is "no criterion at all" for telling subjective compulsions from objective necessities [406]. The uncanny-room example shows the cost: the one test offered (the uncanniness would reach an uninformed observer) is hedged by "sufficient receptivity", so no failed case could count against it.

Chapter 38 — The In-Forming of the Act of Apprehension (On the Problem of the Origin of Concepts) (Die Ein-Bildung des Auffassungsaktes. Zum Problem der Begriffsentstehung) [413–444]

Thesis. Spirit's act is not a spectator's. It leaves a precipitate in life: it "in-forms" (ein-bilden) itself into lived experience as a limit or check on the flow (Ablaufsschranke). What spirit contributes to conceiving is only the drawing of boundaries, that is, differentiation. Generality does not come from spirit. It comes from lived characters or essences that appear across many images, and abstraction is more an obstacle to general meaning-units than their source. The first and most basic act is the positing of being (Seinssetzung): "there is something". Its ground is the I, or self, the time-constant pivot of life and spirit. The I is the connected system of these boundary-checks: "The in-forming of spirit coincides… with a rule-governed narrowing of life" [435].

Argument. 1. Reciprocity (conceptual argument). If A is connected with B, effects run both ways. So in the personal I nothing happens on spirit's side without a consequence in life. Consciousness is not a mere spectator (correcting the emphasis of ch. 25). Aristotle: the nous poietikos needs a nous pathetikos [413]. 2. The impression is changed by conceiving (experimental and observational evidence). Wundt's grey discs: five grades are re-identified, up to nine only once named. Hearing a known versus an unknown language. Chinese script read or unread. Illegible letters readable only in context [414]. The change lies not in "sensation" but in the experience of meaning. Recognition without self-identification shows that "the achievement of self-identification (Verselbigung) is differentiation and nothing else". The name helps by fixing the line between grey and light grey [415]. This links back to ch. 11's separation of the referring and conceiving functions. 3. Objection from animal discrimination (the sparrow and the scarecrow). Answer: whoever says "grey" means the colour as such, separated from the disc. This requires the object of thought "colour in general", which stands to the thing as property to substratum. The bull attacks red cloth because he does not separate red from its bearer. Animal meanings lie "on the level of essences" and are sorted by kinship and opposition, like hunger and satiety, with no consciousness of boundaries [415–417]. 4. The boundary pursued to its end (dialectical analysis). A boundary contains nothing of what it divides. Detached from life, boundary-consciousness would be consciousness of nothing. Only what is bounded on all sides is conceivable (trot against gallop and walk), but that is cut out of actuality and unrelatable. The Eleatics saw the unchangeability of being. From the inconceivability of change follows that of difference and plurality, hence a rift between the general concept and its cases. Stilpo: "this is not cabbage; for cabbage existed ten thousand years ago". "This", "here", "I" are themselves generalities. Grafted onto Socratic definition, Eleatic negation became skepticism. Paradox: the "unadded content of conceiving is differentiation", yet the model of all inconceivability is difference itself [417–419]. 5. Diagnosis of the logic textbooks. Logicians disagree about categories and concept-genesis because they confuse abstracting with generalizing and locate both in spirit. Sensualists (abstraction: redness, horse) are pressed by idealists (sum, relation, Kant's fir/willow/linden → tree), and idealists (apriority) are pressed by sensualists (application). One is driven to "general representations", the other to world-production by concepts [419–420]. 6. Generality comes from life (linguistic and developmental evidence). "Libyan ox" for elephant, Algonquin "big dog" for horse, "goose feet", "calendar", "steel pen". Wundt's child calls a line of dogs "eijebapp" (railway). Klages's own case: the boy who greets a chubby-cheeked child as "You little, little hamster family!" and was later wounded by the laughter. The shared trait, a roundness inviting a tender touch, spoke to his eros. He confused nothing. His meaning-unit had the generality of "plastic roundness". So meaning-units grounded in intuition "surpass in generality the objects of thought that later congealed out of them". Against Geiger's "confusion precedes distinction", though Geiger's passage (quoted) is partly right [420–423]. Spirit's boundary supplies unity and sameness. Generality accrues "in the measure of the unboundedness" of the co-related, omitted essence. "Bold generalizations" come from the depth of the soul, not the sharpness of spirit [423]. 7. Polemic against "logistic" and phenomenological formalism (Husserl, unnamed: "ideal unity of the species", "intentional objects", "evidence"). The formalist rightly rejects Locke's and Berkeley's general triangle. But splitting ideal from real being makes ideal being unknowable, and formalism is "Platonism… on miserable paper". It cannot explain how the species is "exemplified", because the individual triangle is also an abstractum, cut off from all others (Stilpo again) [424–426]. Mathematical concepts are a bad starting point. They are late, and Pythagorean number was a numerical character, not a quantity [426]. 8. The positive account. Humans always proceed from the logically more general to the more particular. Originally they meant "the tree-like", "the triangle-like", object-characters. The particular arises when the character "this here", grounded in the demonstrative function of perception, is added [426–427]. Evidence from mythology: gods, demons and fairies keep their identity through changes of shape (Athena–Mentor, Zeus–bull, Proteus). That is "no more wonderful than that we find the triangle in the individual triangle". Kinship of character limits the forms: an earth-deity never appears as an eagle [427–428]. Forms versus boundaries: the preconceptual world is boundless. Shadow against brightness is a form, and a boundary is "purely ideal". "Forms transform themselves, boundaries merely shift" [428–429]. 9. The antinomy and its resolution. We need "triangle" to grasp the individual triangle, yet the two objects of thought are absolutely different, as unanswerable as how one atom acts on another. The answer is that there is no participation, only an indispensable relation. Relating needs a ground in life: a pure knife-understanding could not "grasp" anything. "Distinguishing and relating are correlative". Spirit separates, vitality reconnects, and thinking consciousness comes from their counter-working: "the knowledge of what is mutually related yet different" [429–431]. Summary: general meaning-units arise from lived connections, and abstraction then replaces them with concepts and relations [431]. 10. Two kinds of general concept. There are generalities from abstraction, gained by successive splittings and poor in meaning ("greater/smaller", number, elasticity, credit). These are like machines assembled from simplified parts (promised for Vol. II), on the "way upward", and governed by the differentia specifica, which ends in relativism. And there are abstractions from generality (morning/evening, youth/age, love/hate), heavy with meaning, on the "way downward toward the center", and governed by contrary opposition that points to polarities of life [431–433]. 11. Return to ch. 2. The finding of the identical thing is explained. Sameness applies to the actual via time-position, the empirical now and sense-space (chs. 31–32), and re-finding requires elementary similarity (ch. 34). The most general co-relatedness is space-temporality [433]. 12. Signs (Zeichensprache). There is no signless thinking. Thinking is dialogical, Palágyi's "self-intercourse". A name has two "energies": a symbolically indicating one ("kinetic") and a conceptually delimiting one ("potential"). The bare name calls for beholding (Montenegro, "fire!", "ah"), while setting names against one another makes boundaries flash up [433–435]. Each act of spirit sets a new barrier to the feeling of actuality, and their connectedness is the personal I [435]. Hilbert's "In the beginning is the sign" is right for the beginning of thinking [435]. We speak meaningfully of state, government or plant without concepts, carried by "meaning-filled attitudes" (Haltungen) that hover near sense-spatial images. Defining moves away from intuition into an ordering of names [435–437]. Inherited grammatical feelings (case, tense, subject–predicate) are precipitates of spirit's past splittings, and so are attitudes of understanding, will and body: "Take away these attitudes, and man is… blind of spirit" [437–438]. 13. The first act: positing of being (answer to the objection that animals have inhibitions too). Conceptual understanding differs from original reflection only in degree. Even "plain" perception conceives: it flips the mirrored counterpart into "the existing something", and mirroring experience into the meaning-feeling of being-there [439]. Being-positing separates objects and then relinks them through one thing outside the world that represents actuality [440]. No animal experiences the precondition of "there is something", so no animal has "was there / could be here". The deer never asks why no trees stand on the meadow [440–441]. 14. Ground of being-positing: the self (genealogy and linguistic appeal). Not life, and not spirit alone, but "actually being… the time-constant pivot of life and spirit: one calls it the I or self". Only what meets a self as its outside has the de-actualized form of actuality called being [441]. Language: medieval mystics' Ichheit, selbig/derselbe ("the very one"), Selbstbewußtsein, Selbständigkeit as the German for substantia, "self" as "lord, owner". The first boundary-check is the I, at first as consciousness of its mirror-semblance, the actuality congealed into matter. This is the subject–object split, which brings in all further splittings [442]. How spirit first entered life, phylogenetically, in a late stratum of prehistoric man, "remains… an insoluble riddle". Today the disposition is germ-plasmic and is actualized through language [442–443]. 15. Phenomenological confirmations of the "flip". Ambiguous jest-pictures (skull / two children). A child's whimper heard as a cat. Knocking at the door recognized as already the third. A message noticed only on repetition. "Coming to oneself" after vacant staring, a "transforming jolt" [443–444].

Key terms. Ein-Bildung (in-forming, with a play on Einbildung, imagination). Niederschlag (precipitate). Verselbigung (self-identification) = Unterscheidung (differentiation). Grenze (boundary) versus Form. Ablaufsschranke / Grenzhemmung (boundary-check, inhibition of the flow). Abstrahieren versus Verallgemeinern. Charakter (object-character: "the tree-like"). Seinssetzung (positing of being). Ich / Selbst as pivot (Drehpunkt). Haltungen (attitudes). Two "energies" of the name.

Contribution to the whole. This is the culmination of Book III. It answers ch. 2's question about identical re-finding, it completes ch. 11's analysis of the concept, and it uses ch. 36's symbolic/conceptual distinction. It defines the I as a system of inhibitions, which ch. 39 develops as the "fatality of selfhood" and Book IV, the doctrine of the will, builds on. It defers the confusion behind the participation problem to Vol. III, the machine-analogy of concepts to Vol. II, and the intuitive root of the concept of the One to a later chapter.

Critical note. The separation of boundary (from spirit) from generality (from life) is a powerful reframing of the universals problem. It explains both why bare abstraction cannot produce generality and why concepts apply at all, and the hamster-family case is an apt illustration. But the resolution of the participation antinomy, "relation, not participation", grounded in "lived connectedness", relocates the problem into the unanalyzed notion of an "essence" appearing in many images. That is close to the Platonic participation Klages attacks. His dismissal of Husserl attacks a two-world reading that Husserl would reject.

Chapter 39 — On the Fatality of Selfhood (Vom Verhängnis der Selbstheit) [445–457]

Thesis. The I, as a system of spirit-implanted boundary-checks, is a self-multiplying process that consumes life. Every gain in conceiving increases power and possession at the price of an equal loss in the capacity to experience actuality, and so in bliss. The animal is the wrong foil for measuring this. The proper comparison is within ourselves, between mythic/poetic and rational consciousness. The chapter ends by establishing that spirit can be coupled only with individual life, that individuality's mark is unrepeatable uniqueness, and that personality takes its stamp wholly from vitality, while what all persons share is participation in one spirit.

Argument. 1. Formal antitheses [445]. Spirit is outside space and time, while spirit-made-alive is spatial and temporal. Life is boundless, while personal life is finite and historical. Against Heraclitus (B 115, the soul's "self-multiplying logos"): this holds not of the soul but of the "I-shaped soul". Physis is wave and circle, ethos (history) is the tangent that moves away from the origin. The empirical I is "more than merely outwardly comparable to capitalism": it is a self-consumption of life's stuff that would devour the world "golem-like" were it not limited by death. 2. The path of self-becoming [446]. Every "experience" (Erfahrung) adds prohibitions, until the "experienced" person "no longer lives except through experience". The mania for regulation equals the mania to possess and rule (to be shown later). The path runs from moistness to withering, rhythm to rule, polar complementarity to sundering, necessity to law's "ought", whole to part. In world-historical parallel, it runs from the village commons to the state machine. Past thirty barely a remnant of the soul's All survives. Hence the need for ecstasy. 3. Objection: the evident gains of understanding [446–448]. Reading, understood speech, memory available through names, and the Sophoclean "Of things mighty… most mighty is man" (Jordan's translation of Antigone). Compared with the animal (Geiger quoted, n. 71: the animal will not roll a stone onto its enemy, has no foresight, no tools), man's superiority is "incalculable", if power is the measure of worth. 4. Counter-ledger: death [448–449]. Consciousness of being-there equals consciousness of finitude, and foreknowledge is bought with the knowledge of death. Hölderlin: "The child is immortal; for it knows nothing of death!" The dread of ceasing is "the deepest ground of origin of all religions of the spirit", and every creed wishes for "the de-actualization of death, even at the price of a de-actualization of life". Anecdote: Gustaspes and Zarathustra's four wishes (from Kiesewetter). 5. Rejecting both standard frames [449]. Nietzsche's consciousness-as-sickness and a hedonic calculus both still start from a false presupposition. Original man was distinguished not by thought (ch. 35). Humans lived 100,000 years before history, and language was first symbolic. So the transition lies in a transformation of beholding. 6. Methodological turn [449–450]. Compare our own wakefulness of thinking and willing with our own being carried along by the "stream of thoughts". We know animal drive, momentariness and lack of memory only through ourselves (childhood). The detour through the animal projects a foil for a presupposed glory of reason. The question is whether experience widens or narrows in the passage from less to more conceiving apprehension. 7. Phenomenological examples of disenchantment [450–453]. The winter starry sky becomes "poorer, nearer, and flatter" once constellations are learnt. The finding heightens us, but what is found fetters us. "The pleasure of conquest is a pleasure in the I; the bliss of beholding is a bliss in the actual". Every satisfaction of the "will to power" costs capacity for bliss. Hölderlin's Hyperion: "A god is man when he dreams; a beggar when he reflects". Other cases: the labyrinth-park whose paths run radially to one centre; the well plumbed by the child; clouds as demons, then water vapour; Eichendorff's dawn dispelling night-giants. Art studied by connoisseurs: "the connoisseur stands to the man of enthusiasm as the master tailor… to the bridegroom". Goethe; youthful lovers of flowers or stars lost to botany and astronomy; the nimbus evaporating. Note 73 concedes that this verdict targets causal, logocentric ("Heraclean") cognition, not the biocentric or contemplative kind. 8. Mythic consciousness as a stratum [452]. Freedom from "superstition" is the doubtful privilege of the educated. Folk-consciousness ties the threads to prehistory. It is methodologically required to excavate the mythic stratum within rational consciousness. 9. Which life can be coupled with spirit? [453–455]. The problem of sensation was deferred, which left a gap. Not all life-processes can bear acts. Cells live, so they have experiencing inwardness, yet never carry acts. The thinking life-bearer must distinguish its own life from foreign life, with a compulsion "as original" as the axiom of the straight line. So consciousness exists only in dependence on individual living beings. Living beings divide into the unspirited (plants, cells) and the spirited (man). Klages self-quotes on the tree cult [455]. 10. The mark of life: uniqueness [455–456]. Spirit finds no bodily property distinctive of life. Self-movement fails (the unmoved can be seen as alive, and aseity is experienced, not a factual mark). The factual mark is the unrepeatable singularity of the sum of properties: of the mechanical, thousands of copies, of the living, "not a single one". The sole point of comparison among living beings is "their — incomparability". 11. Person versus spirit [456–457]. The person is an individual I, which tempts us to say "I" when we mean "the individual", and so to confuse spirit and life ("spiritual life"). Klages holds that personality owes its stamp exclusively to vitality (plants differ for the same reasons persons do). What distinguishes persons from impersonal bearers of life is participation in one and the same spirit. This leads into ch. 40.

Key terms. Selbstheit (selfhood), Ichhaftigkeit (I-shapedness). Verhängnis (fatality, doom). Erfahrung (experience as intelligent processing, n. 70) versus Erleben. Regelungssucht (mania for regulation). Ekstase. Entwirklichung (de-actualization). physis versus ethos. Individuum (literally "undivided"), Einzigartigkeit (uniqueness). beseelt / begeistet versus unbegeistet (spirited versus unspirited bearers of life). epimetheische Lebensform (the Epimethean life-form, mentioned).

Contribution to the whole. Draws the evaluative and historical consequences of ch. 38's definition of the I. It anticipates Book IV (will as mania to possess and rule, the "man of will"), and Book V's doctrine of ecstasy and the Pelasgian world (chs. 71–75). Supplies the individual/person/spirit distinctions used in ch. 40's critique of Palágyi. Cashes out ch. 35 (original man not marked by thought).

Critical note. The methodological point is strong: compare modes of consciousness within ourselves rather than project onto animals, and ask whether conceiving widens or narrows experience. It turns the debate from capacities to phenomenology. But the examples of disenchantment are chosen to support the thesis, and the claim of strict compensation (every gain in power is "atoned for with an equally weighted" loss) is asserted, not shown. Counter-cases such as wonder deepened by knowledge are not considered, apart from note 73's concession for "biocentric" cognition, which weakens the generality of the verdict.

Chapter 40 — The Researches of Melchior Palágyi (Die Forschungen Melchior Palágyis) [458–476]

Thesis. Palágyi is the one contemporary whose findings run closest to Klages's: four-dimensional "flowing space", the intermittence of spirit's acts against continuous life, the irreducibility of "vital" or "imagined movement", and the "self-estrangement of the life-process". Yet his conscious system is mechanistic and makes life a correlate of consciousness. So, despite his vitalism, he "lacks the concept of original lived experience" [462]. His best discoveries testify against his own premises.

Argument. 1. Portrait [458]. Palágyi tests his principles on special problems in sober school language, makes the front of the sciences "totter", and is ignored by specialists and the lay public alike, who prefer the "ingenious calligraphy" of Bergson or the stylish "sham-metaphysician" Schopenhauer. 2. Space and time (Neue Theorie des Raumes und der Zeit, 1901) [458–460]. He is credited with the four-axis coordinate system, the "showpiece" of relativity. In fact he based it on actuality-time and so completed Heracliteanism. But taking "flowing space" literally abolishes duration, things, motion and all geometric figures. "Conservation" and "flowing" contradict each other. His own illustration (space coloured red turning yellow) presupposes an identical space. Klages's correction: a figure becomes objective only if space is thought as self-identical through time, the "standing space". A literally happening space leaves only the unobjectified spatio-temporal polarity of experience. Quote: "If the point of time becomes another… another world-space" (p. 11). 3. The theory of acts (Naturphilosophische Vorlesungen, 1907) [460–461]. An unequalled analysis of perception founds the opposition between flowing life and intermittent, punctual acts. He proposes counting acts in place of "psychophysical measurement". The interval between two acts is a "spiritual pulse-beat", and psychology becomes "a pulse-theory of consciousness". Klages lists new problems this opens: the pulse's fluctuations, dream versus waking, characterological types, pulse-disturbances. 4. The fundamental divergence: correlation [461–462]. For Palágyi the stream is a stream only for a pulsating consciousness, and consciousness is a pulse only because the stream carries it. Klages: correlation makes life depend on consciousness, contrary to the disturbance-model. It also makes the life of vegetative processes, which Palágyi admits are act-incapable, unintelligible. 5. The decisive text (pp. 11–12) [462–463]. Sensation, feeling and phantasm "have only one immediate witness", and a sensation not yet united with consciousness "does not yet exist for us". Klages's reductio: then unconscious processes are nothing, biology is impossible, and Palágyi coincides with mechanist, spiritualist and Kant ("intuitions are nothing to us… if not taken up into consciousness"). In effect, "there is no actless living-through". 6. Circularity of his definitions [463–464]. Sensation is the process by which we take cognizance of mechanical processes, and feeling the process by which we learn of our own life-process. Both are defined by consequences for knowledge. Since the mechanical world is known only through sensation, defining sensation by the mechanical world is circular (the X/A/Y argument). The motive is belief in the actuality of things, which strikes out the actuality of life. 7. Feeling collapses into sensation [464–465]. If the world of things is posited as known, the body becomes a thing and feeling a "visceral" sensation. His analysis of self-touch is examined: double sensations versus double feelings, feeling as the "organic union" of a sensation-pair a+b. Despite his disclaimer, this reduces feeling to fused sensations. 8. The "single witness" criterion [465–468]. Vital processes have one witness, mechanical ones many. Parody: meteors defined as seen only by Nansen. This criterion really separates individual from extra-individual, and it presupposes a prior concept of experience. Palágyi refutes himself: one's own movement is recognized as one's own "through feelings" (p. 69, p. 78), so the character of the attested process, not the singular witness, grounds ownness. And one sleeps one's own sleep and dies one's own death without "being present" [467–468]. His concept of feeling is disguised sensation, and that is a reflex of the mechanical: "of lived experience he has only the word and not the thing" [468]. 9. Klages's counter-definition [468–469]. "To live through is to suffer, to apprehend is to do", and the mechanical is neither. Lived experience is bipolar and transitive. Awareness of life comes before awareness of one's own life. Individuation arises because consciousness is a being-present that localizes and binds life: "There is no own-I without an alien-I, but… also no own-life without alien life". Individuality is life's sphere of play, not life itself. The ecstatic's "I was beside myself" (it, life, lived it), "all-feelings" and "directly lived alien feelings" (Hebbel's lines) show that the boundary of ownness fluctuates [469–470]. 10. Heraclitus against Palágyi [470]. B 89: "For the waking there is one common world, but in sleep each turns to his own". Klages adds that the dreamer's world is non-objective, yet "it, and it alone, is the actual world, provided… he dreams true". Counterweight: organic life is individual, its boundaries looser in the plant, the original man and the Promethean man, and rigid in the "Heraclean" man of will (Vol. II). Social illustrations: the watered and unwatered plant, the cat and the bird, the hypochondriac and the singing boy, the feasting exploiter and the starving worker, stock jobbers profiting from a poison-gas colonial war. "Death… presses its seal upon the solitude of all" [470–471]. 11. Living versus dead is not fixed [471–472]. We reach the mechanical by abstraction from the living, not the vital by restriction from the mechanical. With logocentric development, the domain of things grows and that of the living shrinks: the sorcerer-priest versus the mechanist. "Here it is not truth standing against error", but more or less life-fettered dispositions of mind. No one can be logically compelled to give up the lived meaning of a world-picture. 12. Appreciation [473–474]. Palágyi is a Columbus who sought India and found America. His achievements: sleep as an experience; the proof that movement cannot be grasped by sensations alone; "vital" or "imagined movement" as a third life-process beside sensation and feeling; perception as the cooperation of sensing, feeling and fantasy; a theory of phantasms called "epoch-making"; and above all the analysis of touch as "self-estrangement of the life-process" (p. 118). Klages's correction is that the dependence is one-sided: sensation is what it is whether or not an act joins it. The life-process is "sovereign", not the counter-pole of spirit. So Palágyi's "self-estrangement" (biological) and "flowing space" (cosmic) both testify to the independence of experience and happening [474]. 13. Afterword [475–476]. Written in 1917, partly published 1919. A 1924 visit brought rapprochement and joint plans. Palágyi died 13 July 1924. The posthumous Zur Weltmechanik and Wahrnehmungslehre (with Klages's introduction) reveal his hidden guiding idea, "the polar character of actuality". Appraisal is deferred to the chapter "Galilei und Palágyi" in Vol. II (ch. 52).

Key terms. fließender Raum (flowing space) versus stehender Raum (standing space). Erhaltungssatz des Raumes (conservation of space). Intermittenz der Akte (intermittence of acts). geistiger Pulsschlag, Pulstheorie (spiritual pulse-beat, pulse-theory). Korrelation (correlation, the rejected model) versus Störung (disturbance). einziger Zeuge (the single-witness criterion). Erleben = Erleiden (to live through is to suffer). Spielraum (sphere of play) of individuality. vitale / eingebildete Bewegung (vital or imagined movement). Selbstentfremdung des Lebensvorganges (self-estrangement of the life-process). Allgefühle (all-feelings).

Contribution to the whole. Closes Book III and Vol. I by setting the doctrine of consciousness against its nearest rival, which sharpens its central theses: consciousness as disturbance (ch. 28 onward), life's sovereignty, experience as suffering, and individuation as bound life. It collects the Palágyi debts acknowledged in chs. 29, 31, 32, 34 and 37. It points forward to Vol. II (the man of will, mechanics, "Galilei und Palágyi", ch. 52), and reads Palágyi's "self-estrangement" as confirming Klages's own theory of estrangement (chs. 12, 33). The claim that the dreamer's world is "the actual world" anticipates Book V's actuality of images.

Critical note. The circularity charge against defining sensation by the mechanical world it reveals is a sharp and fair point, and so is the observation that "own" sleep and death break the single-witness criterion. But Klages's alternative, that experience is "suffering" independent of any act, is itself known only through recollection, as he concedes: "testimonies of recollection" [470]. That leaves it open to the same charge that unconscious life is posited rather than found. His reading of "flowing space" as literal may also do Palágyi less than justice, since Palágyi stresses the "symbolic" character of the fourth axis (ch. 31).

Arc of Ch. 29–40

Chapters 29–40 make up the Second and Third Parts of Book III. Their task is to show where in life the timeless act of spirit can attach, and what it does there. Ch. 28 established that consciousness springs from points where life is disturbed. Ch. 29 turns that into a structural requirement: experience must be rhythmically articulated, and at each turning-point beholding passes into a "mirroring" of what has just been beheld. Chs. 30–32 spell out what this mirroring is. Visible shape is placeless and given in an instant, so it can stand for the timeless object of thought (ch. 30). Sense-space is never past and never changing, and it is identical with the "actual now" that Book I (chs. 2–3) left unexplained. From it objective time is derived as a mere aspect of space (ch. 31). Sense-space itself is the present "cross-section" of an unintuitable happening, produced by a vital mirroring (ch. 32). Ch. 33 then separates the two sides of sensibility: making-present (the soul's side, space, actuality) and embodiment (the body's side, resistance, existence). Ch. 34 fills the one gap left: the images of a wave are united by an elementary similarity that is lived, not judged.

The Third Part ("The Psychic Foundations of Consciousness", chs. 34–40) builds upward from this vital base. Elementary similarity and kinship/conflict of essence explain phantasm, recognition, familiarity and instinct. Recollection proper and identification need an act of self-reflection (ch. 34). Ch. 35 places the human/animal difference in a "change of poles" that freed beholding from sensation, with drawing as its unmistakable sign. That freedom made man vulnerable to spirit, and it yields the Promethean (symbolic) and Heraclean (conceptual) strata of thought. Ch. 36 sets out symbolic thinking as identification by identity of essence and shows that factual concepts arise by dissection of symbolic units. Ch. 37 uses the apparatus against associationism and shows that even "chance" contiguities rest on the shared sense-space and on near- and far-character. Ch. 38 is the culmination. Spirit contributes only the boundary. Generality comes from lived essences. The first act is the positing of being, whose ground is the self. And the I is the connected system of spirit's boundary-checks, "a rule-governed narrowing of life". Ch. 39 draws the evaluative consequences (selfhood as a fate that consumes life, every conquest paid for in bliss) and ties consciousness to individual life. Ch. 40 sharpens all of this against Palágyi, the nearest ally, by rejecting his correlation model in favour of life's sovereignty.

The block presupposes Book I's results: the timelessness of the object, the thing-point, duration, the critique of Eleatism (chs. 1–5). It also presupposes Book II's metaphysics: spirit as an extra-spatiotemporal power that splits the polarity of soul and body. And it relies on the earlier chapters of Book III that separated consciousness from experience (chs. 24–28), as well as ch. 11 (the concept's referring versus conceiving function, meaning-poor concepts) and ch. 23 (beholding versus sensation, colours). It hands forward several debts. These include the theory of sensation and the will (Vol. II, Book IV), the concept of cause (deferred in chs. 36–37; ch. 51), the "One" and the machine-analogy of concepts (deferred in ch. 38), the decisive ground of spirit's equating act and the clarification of the participation problem (Vol. III), the widening of sense-space and the doctrine of distance (Book V, chs. 62–65), symbol theory, Bachofen and the Pelasgian world-picture (chs. 71–75), and "Galilei und Palágyi" (ch. 52). The phylogenetic how of spirit's entry into life is declared "an insoluble riddle" [443].

Several tensions should be noted. (1) Ch. 33 frankly grants an objection: the theory identifies thing-space, mirror-space and dream-space through common presentness. Yet it must also explain why dream-space is judged dependent, and it can do so only by reference to the thinking I. Ch. 33's split between actuality (the present image) and bodiliness (resistance) keeps this consistent with ch. 40's claim that the dreamer's world is "the actual world". But ch. 40 adds "provided… he dreams true", which presupposes a criterion of true dreaming that the theory nowhere supplies. (2) Animals are credited with mirroring, sense-space, dreams and phantasms (chs. 32–34), yet denied the "independence" of the intuitive image (ch. 35). The line between animal and original man then depends on a degree-concept, "independence", whose criterion is the drawing fact alone. (3) Klages repeatedly charges opponents (trace-theorists, associationists, Palágyi) with reifying relations or confusing levels. Yet his own "elementary similarity" and "kinship of essence" act as uniting powers (chs. 34, 36–37), and the "identity of essence" of symbolic thought is not given criteria that would separate symbolic truth from error. (4) The racial and ethnographic typologies (chs. 33–34) are offered as "proof" or "verification", but they are read through the very ratio of making-present to embodiment they are meant to confirm. (5) The physics polemic (ch. 31's reductio of relational motion, ch. 40's reading of "flowing space") rests on treating physical and mathematical claims as metaphysical ones, while Klages himself warns against exactly that confusion of thing-space with actuality-space.

Book IV — The Doctrine of the Will (Die Lehre vom Willen)

Book IV opens at [513]. Its first three parts are: First Part, Law and Arbitrary Will (Gesetz und Willkür, ch. 41–43, [515–565]); Second Part, Drive and Will (Trieb und Wille, ch. 44–47, [566–626]); Third Part, Deed and Actuality (Tat und Wirklichkeit, from ch. 48, [627–]). This block runs from the start of the book to ch. 49 [640–678].

Chapter 41 — The State of the Case and a Preliminary Derivation of Willing (Tatbestand und vorläufige Ableitung des Wollens) [515–532]

Thesis. The will shows the I, and so the working of spirit in man, more directly than thinking does. Spirit's "act" is one and the same timeless intervention in both perceiving and willing. What separates the act of apprehension (Auffassungsakt) from the act of will (Willensakt) is which side of the bipolar life-process the act splits: the impression or image side gives subject and object; the impulse (Regung) side gives the willing subject and its end (Zweck). The end is therefore generated by willing, where the object is found.

Argument. 1. Placement. The doctrine of the will belongs to the doctrine of the conditions of consciousness. It gets a book of its own because it has to be read off from its effects, because it matters so much for understanding historical man, and because the ground is unmapped. Klages takes up again the concrete or personal I from ch. 9, 38 and 39. The I is neither spirit nor life. It is the "extensionless point of relation" of their interaction, as the earth's axis is to the globe and not a rod pushed through it [516]. So the spirit–life union must begin in time and end in time. 2. Appeal to language. We say Willkür (arbitrary will, choice), which ties willing to the pleasure of the one who wills, but there is no word for a choice-dependence of thinking. We speak of "objective thinking", but hardly of "objective arbitrary will" [517]. This makes willing look further from spirit's norms than thinking is, and so closer to life. That appearance is the root of "individualism" or "personalism": the Sophists, the Renaissance, Sturm und Drang, and in part Nietzsche all took the side of "nature", "heart" and "passion" against the "head", but in practice championed the free arbitrary will of the stronger person. 3. Two errors of personalism. (a) It runs together lawfulness (Gesetzlichkeit) and necessity (Notwendigkeit). Klages's examples are the dog that has learned to avoid punishment and the child who remembers a prohibition. Hunger is felt as necessity. A sense of "ought" is felt as demand. A demand is the same kind of thing whether I issue it or receive it, so "law and arbitrary will are woven from the very same stuff" [520], as in Paul's "where no law is, there is no transgression". (b) As a result, personalism values the absolute command above the compelled act. It aims at enslaving life, not freeing it, and ends in sterile scepticism [518–519]. Klages's conclusion is that spirit's power is best read in the willing I, not the thinking I. 4. Objection: perception is undergone, not done. Klages's example is a thunderclap that interrupts a train of thought. The reply comes later. First comes the plain phenomenology of voluntary direction. I must notice the blade of grass as grass, but I need not notice it at all. I can choose to attend to its colour or its form. Sudden ideas force themselves on me, but I decide what to do with them. Human types bear this out: there are "pathics" who give themselves over to their phantasms, and there are "makers" and "doers" [520]. Klages asserts, without proof, that the very concept of an act comes only from our capacity for acts of will. 5. Reductio against the "conflict of motives" model. If the steps of weighing are not willed, their result cannot be a willed decision. If they are willed, the model already presupposes the will it claims to explain. "Either one finds the volition in every deliberate act of reflecting, or one finds it never and nowhere" [521]. 6. Historical sketch. Antiquity had no psychology of the will. Will was a natural or divine force, and its achievement was modelled on perception. Christian philosophy "conquered" the will (Augustine, Duns Scotus, Ockham) but mistook the fervour of willing for the ground of consciousness, and so produced the false dispute over the "primacy" of will or intellect. Actus purus is an apt formula for spirit. Willing, however, needs a subject that persists in time, and at the moment it is carried out the act of willing already lies in the past. Divine omnipotence is coherent only because a timeless act coincides with its own success. A God who willed would be temporal and so no longer spirit [522–523]. 7. Elimination of alternatives. A timeless act has no intrinsic features. The difference between apprehension and will could therefore lie only (i) in the sequence of acts or (ii) in the life-processes that occasion them. Option (i) fails. Combining acts of reflection, for instance in a "doctrine of categories", only ever yields higher acts of reflection. Nor does any feature of a content show whether thinking it was intended: a geometrical theorem can be thought on purpose or simply occur to one [523–524]. So the primacy question is pointless. Option (ii) must be worked out. Shifting where the act strikes along the wave of the life-process would not help, since apart from the turning-point there is nothing fixable. 8. The bipolar vital circle. From Plato and Aristotle (aisthetikon and kinetikon, the latter traced to orexis) up to modern sensory-motor physiology, impression and impulse have been treated as separate. In fact they are "inseparably interbelonging halves" of a closed vital circle. Every impression is answered by a stirring, and every stirring is directed at an image [525]. Example: the hungry sleeper dreams images of being sated. Preliminary answer: at the act-bearing boundary-point, the act is one of apprehension when the impression (the intuitive image) leads, and one of willing when the impulse leads. Splitting the impression side gives subject and object. Splitting the impulse side gives willing subject and end. Neither faculty is a potentia nobilior. What differs is whether a type of person is mainly drive-governed or mainly impression-receptive [525–526]. 9. Even compelled apprehension is a deed. Picking up ch. 9 (the I as a will-to-be-there), Klages argues that a sense of being compelled would be impossible unless a core capable of deeds reacted. Without the counter-act we would still take fright, but we could never become conscious of the compulsion [526–527]. The voluntarist scholastics were therefore right that the spiritual ground of consciousness is a capacity for timeless acts, and right to sense that the object depends on the end. They were wrong to think willing generates finding. 10. Sharpening the difference. (a) What is apprehended is indifferent to whether it was attended to voluntarily, so cognizing and willing cannot occur in the same timeless instant. Knowing an end is not willing it. (b) The bare perceptual act "there is something" has a counterpart in the bare act of will "I will something". Klages's evidence is obstinacy, the "I will to will" [528]. Hence "it is the volition that generates the end, and not the reverse" [528]. (c) Against the claim that willing presupposes cognition: it does, but a find becomes available as spiritual property only once it is "sealed" by an immediate act of will. This sealing act is the judgement (Urteil), which adds "validity" to the apprehension (here Klages sharpens Palágyi). Typology illustrates it: feminine intelligence is strong in apprehension, masculine intelligence in judgement (stat pro ratione voluntas against ratio eget voluntate) [529]. This explains why animals have memory but do not recollect: only judged apprehensions can be recollected [530]. 11. Direction relative to life. A compelled act of noticing (a blow on the arm) follows life-processes. A willed act (raising the arm) precedes them. This holds without exception and grounds the two basic feelings, being compelled and self-activity [531]. Splitting the impulse side cannot yield a "fact", because all finding starts from the sense-impression. What it yields is the end, which has no share in actuality of its own and so cannot be treated as a thing. That is why treating the end as a thing, as every teleology does, is the least promising of all false reifications: it does not even yield the semblance of a solution [532]. Left open: how an act that depends on the impulse leading can seem to cause life-processes.

Key terms. Willkür (arbitrary will, choice); Akt (timeless deed of spirit common to perceiving and willing); Auffassungsakt / Willensakt; Regung (stirring, impulse) and Eindruck (impression) as the two halves of the vital circle; Zweck (end, purpose), which is generated and not found; Urteil (judgement), the act of will that seals an apprehension and gives it validity; Gesetzlichkeit vs Notwendigkeit; Selbsttätigkeit (self-activity) vs Genötigtsein (being compelled); the "pathic" and the "doer" types.

Contribution to the whole. This chapter sets the frame for Book IV. The will is placed in the I as the pivot of spirit and life (ch. 9, 26, 38–39). The ch. 31 thesis that the act responds to a life-convulsion is applied. The end is defined as the product of splitting the impulse, which ch. 45–46 will analyse. The lawfulness/necessity distinction is introduced for ch. 42. The judgement is defined as an act of will, which ch. 47 extends into the "will to truth". The problem of how an act can seem to cause bodily motion is handed on to ch. 48–49.

Critical note. The strongest step is the dilemma against the "conflict of motives" model (step 5), which shows that deliberative accounts quietly presuppose volition. The weakest point is the claim that the end is "generated" while the object is "found". It rests on asserting that the act has no features of its own, and on the obstinacy example, which could equally be described as an impulse with a vague object rather than a will with no object at all. The gendered typology of apprehension and judgement is asserted, not argued.

Chapter 42 — Lawfulness and Necessity (Gesetzlichkeit und Notwendigkeit) [533–550]

Thesis. Necessity (the actual course of life-happening) and lawfulness (spirit's rule-form) differ "as life and spirit" do [533]. Determinism and indeterminism are sham opposites. Both place in "law" the commanding will of spirit that they claim to set against freedom. A law is only a concession that the will makes to the resistance of living actuality. Real necessity, as the archaic belief in fate shows, is not a law, and a person can experience it as a blessed "must".

Argument. 1. Diagnosis. Every account of freedom has failed by swapping necessity for lawfulness, and arbitrary will for lawlessness. A process can be lawless and still necessary [533]. 2. The Stoa as test case (historical-textual). Klages quotes Zeno, Chrysippus and Poseidonios: the world is ruled by a world-reason that is a "law of nature". The Stoics still placed the arche of willing in the person, issued precepts of duty in a manner Klages likens to Yahweh as penal judge, and defined freedom as willing obedience to the law: "everything belongs to the wise; for the law gives them perfect freedom" [535]. Klages leaves aside their theodicy contradiction (how error is possible in a world of reason). His point is that the omnipotence of law and the omnipotence of arbitrary will "flow from the very same source" [535]. 3. Three later samples. Abelard: conscience, the natural law and God's will are one. Spinoza: natural law and moral law are identical, and freedom comes from the activity of clear thinking, so the determining power is modelled on human self-activity. Kant: strict determinism of appearances combined with freedom as the capacity to obey the moral law unconditionally ("You can, for you ought"). This is the primacy of practical reason, which Klages translates back into Augustine's omnes nihil aliud quam voluntates sunt and Duns Scotus's arbitrary God [536]. Note 1 [781] argues that Kant's ethics adds nothing new to the Stoa. 4. Genealogical thesis (asserted, polemical). The categorical imperative and natural-scientific "law" have one "Judaic" source: an omnipotent will that commands ("Let there be light"). "Law" is the word that both Scripture and modern science rank highest [537]. "Determinism … means: determination of the cognizing spirit for the purpose of the complete absolution of the willing spirit" [537]. The writing here is openly antisemitic and polemical, and nothing in it argues the point. 5. Why no open "monism of will"? The despot example: an order to execute prisoners can be carried out only through bodies (gesture, servants, weapons). The decision is not its own manifestation, and execution meets the "resistance of actuality" [538]. That resistance comes from the world's life, which cannot in principle be conceived. It does not come from its thinghood, which is conceivable and so open to the will. Things regulated by natural law are therefore already a compromise between actuality and spirit. "Lawfulness" is the share of the willing spirit in the object [539]. 6. Verdict. "Law" splits into two: the moral law, which "merely demands", and the natural law, which "merely enforces the demand read into it", and with forces not its own [539–540]. Kant never showed how a causality "according to concepts" fits with mechanism. Klages: "there are no natural laws, and the moral law is rebaptized arbitrary will" [539–540]. Treating the universe as a state run by laws is a way of breaking up life so that it can be conquered. 7. Indeterminism. Epicurus preferred myths of the gods to the "slavery" of natural necessity and admitted necessity, design and chance. That still mixes up legislating reason with the creative powers of life. A "law of nature" is just the rule "B whenever A, unhindered" [541]. Determinism and indeterminism turn into each other, so the question cannot even be posed on a world of things governed by sufficient reason. 8. Nietzsche's lawless power-monads (reductio). Nietzsche suggests (in Beyond Good and Evil §22) that nature is necessary and calculable because laws are absent and every power "draws its consequence". Klages argues that purely unconditioned power-monads would produce "nothing at all". An observer, and indeed any act, needs something that undergoes the action, which limits the power. And a power that acts must face a not-yet, so it would "suffer the urge" of the world's condition. A pure power-monad either becomes an all-power, God, beyond all actuality, or it is limited [542–543]. Psychological conclusion: there is no experience of power without the experience of powerlessness, and no willing without the "resistance of necessity". Examples are the not-being-able that willing builds on: being in two places, reversing time, hastening the future [543]. The "Almighty" is "all-powerless" as a willer. Klages then asks whether felt self-authority is an idle epiphenomenon or a power that really contends with necessity, and answers the second [543]. Ch. 43 has to make good on that answer. 9. Necessity without law (anthropology of fate). Before mechanical causality came "personal" or magical effecting (Zeus's frown, Leto turning peasants into frogs). Before that came impersonal daemonic powers of fate: the Moirai, Keres, Erinyes, Norns, fairies. They are plural, mostly female, and tied to weaving earth-mothers and priestesses [545–546]. Klages argues against students of religion that general concepts came out of these powers, not the other way round. Iliad 22: Zeus does not decide Hector's death but weighs the lots, and Hector's fated day "sank heavy toward Hades" [546–547]. Divination works only on accidental happenings, so it is incompatible with belief in regularity. This is aimed at the Stoa and at Schopenhauer (note 2). 10. The fate-attitude stated as principles. Everything that happens to or through a person, including resolves, is a "befalling". Every event hangs together with every other, like a spider's web. Person and fate are two sides of one essence [547]. Enthusiasm, Plato's "divine madness" and Nietzsche's "my love kindles itself only at necessity" show a blessed must. Moralists wrongly treated necessity as a prisoner's chain [548]. Obedience to law, too, is affirmed only through sharing in the power-will of the commanding spirit. Nietzsche: "where life grows rigid, the law piles itself up". 11. Typology. Six basic feelings of life and kinds of ethos: egoist ("I will"), altruist ("I ought"), man of feeling ("you will"), ascetic ("he wills, I must"), animal man ("it drives, I must"), elemental man ("it happens, I must") [549]. The middle two are intermediates. The first two are one kind, since "I ought" is only "I will" generalised into law. The last two share the "must" of essence. So the six come down to two attitudes. I-originatedness (Ichursprünglichkeit) splits willing from event, and willing from lived experience. Life-originatedness (Lebensursprünglichkeit) knows a single kind of must. The question of freedom now becomes how I-originatedness finds room within necessity, not how it fits with law [550].

Key terms. Gesetzlichkeit (lawfulness, spirit's share in the object) vs Notwendigkeit (necessity, the actual course of happening, which coincides with being actual); "law" as concession or compromise; Machtmonaden (Nietzsche's power-monads); personal and magical effecting; powers of fate (Schicksalsmächte); Ichursprünglichkeit / Lebensursprünglichkeit; the blessed "must".

Contribution to the whole. This chapter applies the earlier claim that laws come from demands of spirit (Book I's critique of the thing and of causality) to ethics. It sets up ch. 43's account of what in the will is necessity and what is spirit, and promises ch. 50–51's critique of mechanical cause and the conservation of energy. The fate material looks ahead to the Pelasgian world-picture (Book V, Part 2) and back to ch. 37's treatment of the "conditionedness of the accidental" (chance, omens, fate).

Critical note. The reductio of the power-monads is strong. It shows that "power" is a relational concept that needs something resisting it, and that grounds the claim "no willing without necessity" without question-begging. The weakest step is the move from "laws are spirit's form" to "there are no natural laws". It ignores that laws might describe necessity correctly, though partially. The racial-theological genealogy of "law" is pure assertion and polemic, and it adds nothing to the conceptual argument.

Chapter 43 — Conditionedness and Unconditionedness of Willing (Bedingtheit und Unbedingtheit des Wollens) [551–565]

Thesis. Willing is fully conditioned by the personality, but the personality joins two different kinds of condition. One is the vital constitution, which supplies impulses as necessity. The other is the spiritual constitution, which supplies commands that cannot be derived from necessity. A volition arises when a command of spirit fastens a suffered impulse to a thought. "Motives" are not causes. The motive is the purpose seen from its "why" side, and its force comes from an impulse. Commands of spirit go out only on the occasion of impulses referable to fact.

Argument. 1. Against "freedom of action" as the naive meaning. Aristotle, Hobbes, Locke, Leibniz, Hume, Schopenhauer and others say ordinary self-reflection means only "I can do what I will". Klages argues it means that the I produces its willing. Grammar shows this: "I will it but cannot do it" and "I did it but did not will it" are coherent, but "it was not I who willed my willing" contradicts itself [551]. 2. The command structure. Willing is a command from the centre of the personality to itself. Wavering, for instance between two paths at the start of a walk, makes this visible, and what holds there holds for all willing, which is therefore always choice [552]. The imperative confirms it linguistically. "Come here!" cannot be separated from its speaker, while a report of Charlemagne's death can [552]. 3. Against mechanical subsumption. The falling leaf does not waver or resolve, and the storm does not command. To derive the command from a more distant cause would cancel the very cause one had named [553]. The determinist scheme (a thing-like personality plus motives gives the volition, as wind gives the falling leaf) reifies the motive. If the purpose is generated by the I, so is every motive, and determinism counts effects of the I among its causes [553]. Anyone can list another person's motives as an exercise in probability without being moved by them, so thinking a purpose is not the force of the purpose [554]. 4. Objection: knowledge of one's ability is another given. Klages's gradation of wishes: the logically impossible (undoing the past), the humanly impossible (a journey to the moon), the personally impossible (lifting three hundredweight, buying a castle), the fulfillable. Awareness of incapacity motivates not willing. Within the fulfillable range, why does one "motive" win? Not by logical force, or decisions could be predicted. We can predict that an insulted man will feel indignation, but not what he will do [555]. 5. Nietzsche's critique of motives (cited and endorsed). Twilight of the Idols, Beyond Good and Evil §32 and Zarathustra: the motive is a "surface phenomenon" that conceals the true antecedents of a deed. Klages: motives in the received sense "do not exist" [555]. Yet a decision of will exists only with reference to a consciousness of intention. "Purpose" names the goal ahead of one, and "motive" names why that goal was chosen [556]. 6. Deliberation is inner action. Schopenhauer's picture, in which motives "try their force against one another" on a will that is not yet awake, turns the will into a prize fought over by others. Explaining volition from motives is like explaining burning by fire [557]. Three features of the motive: (i) it is always a thought (running from an explosion is either a reflex impulse or presupposes the thought of danger); (ii) to its bearer it looks like a ground; (iii) its moving force must come from elsewhere, from a vital impulse that a command of spirit has "fettered" to a thought [557]. 7. Separation of necessity and spirit within volition. Impulses are given, suffered and necessary. The command is the I's own and falls under no concept of necessity. It turns the driven I into a willing I [558]. The personal I is an "essence riven within itself" whose having-to side and active side keep interfering with each other [558]. The joining of vital and spiritual constitution can no longer be understood from necessity. 8. Outer and inner necessity. The limits of my ability are outer necessity, and impulses that become motives are inner necessity. Outer necessity counts for us only insofar as it shows itself through inner necessity (an impulse suffered regardless of my pleasure). Belief that a resolution can be carried out is always present. Hence: "commands of spirit go forth exclusively on occasion of impulses referable to fact" (sachbeziehbare Antriebe) [559]. Only inner processes with a process-limit, already split, are capable of will. 9. On primacy, settled. Stressing purpose and means leads to building will out of thoughts. Stressing the "reactivity" of apprehension leads to building thought out of deeds. Both are wrong, but the second is "a little closer to the truth", because in an inspirited being the will-ready stance is more original [560]. 10. Character dispositions of will (some new to research): tensibility of will ("energy"), ease of will (will-talent), and resoluteness (how easily a feeling-impulse passes into a will-impulse) [560–561]. Within these limits, does choice remain free? Yes, it is the personal I that decides. But the I is the "geometrical locus" of spirit and life, so it decides according to its constitution. The alternative needs an I behind the I, ad infinitum [561]. 11. Drives vs motive-springs. Drives (Triebe) are the substrate of impulses not referable to fact ("elementary" and "animalic" ones). A purely animal being forms no purposes. Motive-springs (Triebfedern = "interests") are the substrate of impulses referable to fact. "The problem of will coincides with the problem of the genesis of motive-springs out of mere drives" [562]. Conversions, the "new life" made by one resolution, are themselves choices made because of a suffered impulse, with a motive-spring beneath it. No intention exists without a "because". The true cause of willing is a life-process to which a groundable thought of the goal has attached itself in statu nascendi through the split [563]. 12. Character is variable. Acquired motive-springs are a "mean proportional" of innate ones and contingent circumstances. Twins raised alike still diverge. Conversion succeeds or fails according to the character at the time [563–564]. 13. Difference from determinism, and close of Part One. The command of spirit really contends with necessity and brings about changes in appearance. Inwardly it must modify existing impulses by splitting them. Outwardly, mechanical causality cannot by definition include a purely spiritual act. Klages calls that the standpoint of a consistent determinism and leaves it untouched. He announces instead that spirit's "immeasurable effects" happen "this side of" all causal connection. Mechanical law is "an extremely artificial net with which one imagined one could scoop out the ocean" [565].

Key terms. Befehl (command of spirit); Willensantrieb (impulse of will); Antrieb (impulse, suffered); Beweggrund / Motiv (the purpose seen as its "why"); Zweck / Absicht (purpose, intention); sachbeziehbare Antriebe (impulses referable to fact); Triebfedern (motive-springs, "interests") vs Triebe (drives); outer and inner necessity; the will dispositions (Spannkraft/energy, ease of will, Entschlußfähigkeit/resoluteness).

Contribution to the whole. This chapter finishes Part One with a compatibilist-sounding position. The I decides, and decides according to its two-fold constitution. It defines what Part Two must explain: the genesis of motive-springs from drives (ch. 44–47), and what the spiritual act does to an impulse. It also promises the "outer side" (ch. 48–51): spirit's effects on appearance outside mechanical causality. The treatment of motives is used again in ch. 49 (true vs supposed purpose).

Critical note. The point that deliberation is itself willed, and that motive-thoughts lack force of their own, is sharp and anticipates later criticisms of belief-desire models. The vulnerable step is the claim that the command is "the I's own" yet "according to the measure" of a constitution. That is either determinism by another name or a regress Klages blocks only by stipulating that the I is a mere "geometrical locus". The asymmetry he wants (command not under necessity, impulse under it) is asserted, not demonstrated.

Chapter 44 — Critique of Previous Conceptions of Drive (Kritik bisheriger Triebauffassungen) [566–575]

Thesis. Almost everything written about drives is a "heap of nonsense" [566]. It interprets drive by analogy with will. It reads purposes into impulses, studies the consequences of drives instead of the drives themselves, and projects the I's interest in self-preservation onto all life as a "self-preservation drive". This utilitarian teleology, shared by idealism, materialism, vitalism and Darwinism, turns nature into a business firm and blocks any real psychology of drives.

Argument. 1. The task. Part Two must show what the spiritual act separates off in the impulse. That needs a concept of drive, but the existing literature is worse than ignorance. Partial exceptions: Romantic philosophy, and Nietzsche, whose concept of drive was bent by the "will to power" [566]. 2. Three mechanisms of distortion. Whether one reads drive as will (Lipps) or will as drive (Schopenhauer), spirit gets carried into the sub-spiritual. (a) Will pursues purposes, so the life-impulse is said to purpose and nature becomes a system of purposes. (b) The purposes of will are read off from what they achieve, so the consequences of drives are studied in place of the drives. (c) Only an I wills and asserts itself, so the I's self-preservation interest becomes a universal self-preservation drive [567]. 3. Nutrition. Animals eat without knowing about calories. A newly hatched chick pecks grain, birds migrate. Positing a "nutritive drive" grounded in self-preservation yields "unpurposed purposes, unthought thoughts, unconscious consciousness" [567]. A financially trained "nature" is planning on the animal's behalf. 4. Reproduction (reductio).
