Distance-action is "a question not of physics but of logic". Any real unit marks off a sphere of effect, and isolated units can neither act nor be acted on. Contact either leaves something between them or fuses them. "Isolated units purchase their existence at the price of the incapacity to exert or receive effects" [720]. Swapping mass-points for force-centres or energy changes nothing. Note 28 adds that "acting at a distance" reifies a verb (only a shining thing exists, not "shining"), and that the hereness of the far becomes intelligible only if one starts from the polarity of nearness and distance as a primal phenomenon rather than from the body's Here: the seed of ch. 56. 10. The genealogy [721–730]. The Galilean "powerless force" starts the line Hume → Kant → relativism (relativism being "the most nearly right"). Klages rejects Kant's causal constitution of the mind: myth and saints' legends routinely break causal rules, and prehistoric symbolic thought had no rules of succession yet a robust sense of actuality. The compulsion to think causally comes from viewing actuality as existence of things, and the thing-point is the I-point thrown outward (ch. 39). Because of the will's priority over cognition, "causality [is] a volition mirrored back" [722]. Will alone accomplishes nothing (it only splits). So the force distilled from it borrows its working from another force, and so on endlessly: this is why the causal chain never closes [723]. Lawfulness still concedes something to actuality: spirit must accept conditions it did not choose, and it aims at a single invariant (monism). It is a "heap of splintered mirrors". A nine-point parallel shows will-power and physical force are "the same" (both meet actuality as resistance, are uniform, goal-directed, imageless, graded, past-less, directed to an imaginary future), except that force lacks the vital vehicle [726]. The mechanist who wants a first cause must posit a Logos-deed or creative command. Descartes's continuous creation (Meditation III) and Stirner's self-creating I (causa sui) show that existence is the I's self-assertion renewed moment by moment. Mechanism and teleology are mirror images, and both fail on happening, which has no leaps. Western metaphysics revolves around the uncaused cause (proton kinoun akineton, natura naturans), and with it "intelligible character" and "transcendental freedom". As selfhood grows, spirit "builds at the prison of life". The wishing I invents posthumous immortality and pre-existent self-determination [730].

Key terms. Entkräftete Kraft (de-forced force: mechanical force emptied of activity); Drang (urge, "tension"); Beharrungsgesetz/Trägheit (persistence, inertia); Zwangsläufigkeit (compulsoriness of process, versus lived Notwendigkeit); Wirkungsbereich/Wirkungseinheit (sphere or unit of effect); Noogenese (genesis of concepts from spirit, as opposed to psychogenesis); ego militans / ego triumphans.

Contribution to the whole. The central chapter of Book IV's attack on mechanism. It cashes out ch. 21 (properties and forces), ch. 36 (dispositions and conditions), ch. 39 (thing-point = I-point) and ch. 42 (lawfulness versus necessity). It grounds ch. 53's "heraclean" stage and the technology diagnosis of ch. 53–54. The ban on action-at-a-distance between things is expressly reread in ch. 56 [830] so that "working of distance" (Ferne) can be granted to the living.

Critical note. The dissection of the circularity of force and mass, and of the "transmission" problem for isolated units, is strong. It belongs to a real line of criticism (Mach, Hertz, the Leibnizian critique of impact), and Klages uses it well. The genealogical step from "force formally resembles will" (the nine-point table) to "force is a mirrored volition" is a genetic inference from analogy. It does nothing to touch the empirical adequacy he concedes, and the dismissal of success as mere power-fitness is asserted, not argued.

Chapter 52 — Galilei and Palágyi (Galilei und Palágyi) [731–742]

Thesis. Melchior Palágyi's "world-mechanics" is the only serious attempt to rebuild the basic concepts of physics for a polar world-picture. It recasts inertia as the impulselessness of matter, posits an impulse-bearing ether as matter's counter-pole, and turns causality into the interaction of ether and matter. It succeeds only by covertly bringing life back into its concepts, so it destroys itself as physics. What it actually offers is a myth and "the first steps toward a biology of the cosmos".

Argument. 1. Framing [731–732]. Ch. 51 read Galileo as the inventor of de-forced force. Palágyi reads him the opposite way. Klages fulfils the promise of Vol. I (ch. 40) to finish sketching Palágyi's worldview and uses him to support his own view of physics. He relies on Weltmechanik ("Galilei und das Übertragungsprinzip", "Kopernikus und die Relativitätstheorie") and Die Relativitätstheorie in der modernen Physik (1914). 2. The Copernican principle [732–733]. Palágyi's "principle of insufficient perception of motion" was wrongly made objective, so that the relativity of perception became a relativity of motion itself (Newton's relativity already, Einstein in full). In the ship dialogue the relativist confuses perception with motion, and the non-relativist fails to distinguish motion from transmission of motion. 3. Galileo as discoverer of transmission [733–734]. Every moving body is a "vehicle" carrying its parts as passengers, so there is no motion without transmission. Matter has no motive impulse of its own and only receives impulses. Uniform happening (straight line) corresponds to inertia, non-uniform (curve) to receptivity for impulse. Galileo's single word "motion" for both bequeathed the conflict between mechanics and the physics of the ether. 4. Ether and matter as poles [734–737]. If matter is pure impulse-receptivity, impulse needs another bearer: the ether. The ether is the immobile "static substrate" and bearer of tensions (potential energy); matter is the ever-moving "dynamic substrate" (kinetic energy). Conservation becomes their exchange. Causality is to be the doctrine of ether–matter interaction (p. 124). Propagation in an immobile ether is a relay of alternating positive and negative impulses, giving a "static velocity" that does not add to dynamic velocities. So the source's motion does not add to the speed of light, and the supposed Fizeau/Michelson conflict disappears. The view is close to Lorentz but reached from mechanics. 5. Against uniform rectilinear motion [737–738]. Palágyi argues that such motion is impossible in three-dimensional space: bodies moving uniformly relative to each other "would stand in absolutely no relation" and could not share a world. Mechanical space is inhomogeneous, a field of tensions. The positive counterpart is a uniform motion of all world-matter along the imaginary fourth (time) axis of "flowing space", experienced as the stream of time. So the ether bears time and matter bears space. 6. Klages's verdict [738–740]. Palágyi's real motive is to make the transeunt character of actual happening physically intelligible, which "can never be understood physically". Physics always asks "how can actuality be thought as a machine?" What Galileo found was not the impulselessness of matter as such but of the individual body, which needs no second substrate. Bringing an impulse-giving power into physics plants "an explosive" in it. Questions without answer: how a motionless ether of pure states communicates motion to extended matter (contact is metaphysically illusory, and a body cannot be "touched" by a state), how it permeates matter, and how it "fills" the four-dimensional manifold. The system stayed a torso for structural reasons. 7. Reinterpretation [740–742]. The key is "never moved, ever excited". Anthill example: when poked, we do not see faster motions but agitation (Erregung). "States" are inner states or nothing. Physics speaks of states only figuratively, through measurable consequences (heat and temperature); genuine states can only be estimated, like a man's anger compared with his measurable movements. Palágyi's ether against matter is active against suffering, male against female, soul against body (note 30: mechanism as the "castration" of the female pole; relativism removes the ether too). He solves transmission and cause only by vitalising his concepts. The conservation law takes a new sense, since the ether's initial impulse is spontaneous and the shapes in which impulses return are unforeseeable.

Key terms. Übertragung (transmission); Vehikel; statische vs. dynamische Geschwindigkeit; Äther (static substrate, bearer of tension and time) versus Materie (dynamic substrate, bearer of motion and space); fließender Raum / Längsraum (flowing or longitudinal space, fourth axis); Erregung (agitation as genuine inner state).

Contribution to the whole. Closes the Palágyi thread begun in ch. 40 (Vol. I) and confirms ch. 51: transeunt working belongs only to living, experiencing poles. It anticipates Book V's cosmic biology (elemental life-forms, ch. 56 [830]; Pelasgian world-picture, ch. 71–75) and the thesis that time is the soul of space (ch. 56).

Critical note. The strongest step is the correction of Palágyi's Galileo: inertia concerns the impulselessness of the individual body, disturbed by a second body, so physics never needed an impulse-bearing second substrate, and adding one breaks it. The anthill argument is weaker than it looks. That we see agitation in ants shows that the everyday word "excited" carries inner-state meaning. It does not show that Palágyi's technical "never moved, ever excited" ether must mean an inner state, so the conclusion that his concepts are "vitalized" rests partly on reading his vocabulary in its ordinary sense. Klages's own unanswered questions (how a state "touches" extended matter) are, by his admission, unanswerable only from mechanism's standpoint; he can treat the same efficacy as a primal polarity (cf. note 28 to ch. 51), which he accepts but does not argue for.

Chapter 53 — Will and States of Consciousness (Wille und Bewußtseinszustände) [743–768]

Thesis. This summing-up chapter states what the doctrine of will implies for consciousness and for history. Freedom is the felt opposition of resolve to drive within a fully determined character. Consciousness arises when an extra-spatiotemporal power splits life-processes. Thinking is inward action from stance to stance. The history of Western spirit runs from a "Promethean" stage (will dependent on life, knowledge bound to images) to a "Heraclean" stage (life dependent on will, cognition serving power). The second was prepared by Christian asceticism, released outward in the Renaissance, and ends in a post-historical, unconscious humanity steered by spirit as a parasite, with the earth destroyed.

Argument. 1. Freedom [743–745]. Only the personal I wills, and only historical man has one. What and how one wills is fixed by momentary character, which comes from the original character and its history. Yet in the person a power has "clamped itself together with organic life" and turns life into a tool. The feeling of freedom is wrong that we could have willed otherwise (remorse is classed among "deluded states") and right that every decision testifies to "extra-vital deed-force" entering necessity. Raspberry-bush example: genus and conditions are predictable, vigour is not ("self-sovereignty of life"). In man the incalculability of spirit is added to that of life, so a people's future is less predictable than a species'. Formula: "Whatever we will … we must will it". Freedom is resolve against drive-impulse. 2. Consciousness [745–746]. Without being seers ourselves, no anatomy of eye and retina would reveal seeing. "Experience" (Erleben) is a "demonstrative concept". Consciousness requires an extra-spatiotemporal power that splits off groups of life-processes by unextended acts. This is the "displacement of the mid-point of life" (ref. Grundlagen der Charakterkunde ch. 9). The organic reorientation is inherited, while spirit's activity starts anew in each infant some months after birth, together with language. Child and adolescent development and crises are thrusts of spirit's power, and evaded "confrontations" ground mental illness. 3. Thinking as inward action [746–748]. Concluding a Vol. I line: thinking is "a leaping transition from stance to stance", like striding, not a motion. What steers it is not phantasms in consciousness (which hinder it) but a will-stance adjusted to the goal, visible in the mimicry of the eye. Signs are "sighting-instruments". Direction is purposive relatedness modelled on space, and the phantasm of sense-space must be continually abstracted from, or reification follows (the modern picture of consciousness as a container). 4. Spirit's point of attack [748]. Spirit attaches to disturbances in the movement of life, on the side of impulse, not impression. Its awakening is a resistance voiced by the "No". The I is the resulting particularity of stance, and will is its determining capacity. It narrows the soul's room "as if one were to force the flood of a sea into a system of canals". Western spirit has been identical with will from the start; Asian spirituality has succumbed. 5. Two phases [748–753]. (a) Life-dependence of will: apprehension led by experience, knowledge for its own sake, will to the work, depth of beholding, seership (Sehertum). (b) Will-dependence of life: apprehension led by thought-purposes, research led by will to power, will to the deed, vehemence of impulse, sobriety (Nüchternheit). Greece ran from seership through logocentric to formalist thought in three centuries, and Aristotle "ended and perfected" it. The symbols, given "in a more dogmatic form" [750]: Prometheus (with Cassandra) is "powerless knowledge", foreknowledge without power to avert doom. He brings fire and consciousness, and consciousness equals foreknowledge of death (ch. 39); out of pity he implants hope. Chained to the Scythian rock, he remains earthbound, son of Themis. The Promethean stage has image-bound thinking, unintentional knowing and Heimarmene, and remains dominant in China and Asia. Herakles (quoted from Klages's own unpublished 1903 text, note 34) strangles the serpent, chooses toil, is armed by motherless Athena, and kills in sequence the offspring of Gaia: the Nemean lion, the Hydra, the hind of Artemis, Antaeus, the Amazons, Troy as stronghold of matriarchy, Cerberus. He is "the fully prefigured mold of the Christian world-idea", Apollo slaying Python. Herakles freeing Prometheus means spirit freed from life's bondage becomes destructive deed. The Stoics took Herakles as their hero. 6. A spectrum of states of consciousness [753–757]. Consciousness lies between the warm darkness of lived experience (deep sleep, dream, absorption) and the cold darkness of the "deed in itself", pure willing. Aristotle's akineton is reread as a limit no longer capable of consciousness. Klages rejects the brightness metaphor and asks whether the irreversible drift toward spirit has causes on spirit's side too. On life's side: heightened impulse-vehemence = heightened reactivity, which presupposes a new disturbability, a locus minoris resistentiae (with a mythical symbol promised for later). The Promethean stance is a "scarring-over" by knowledge; the Heraclean armours the bearer of spirit against experience. On spirit's side, acts vary (i) in energy, the depth and completeness of the split ("a wedge driven into the life-cell", aiming to de-soul the body and disembody the soul), and (ii) in frequency, matching the range from deep sleep to utmost wakefulness. Men of action have quicker acts (reading while listening). Both lead to an upper limit where consciousness dies, either by complete cleavage or by "infinite" quickness that collapses acts into one point. Even finite frequency is capped, because an act yields consciousness only at the turning-point of a life-wave and is bound to its pulse [757]. A possible third variable, the act's point of impact, is deferred to Vol. III. 7. Christian mysticism as Heraclean [757–763]. Saints' "ecstasies" come from extreme overstraining of the will, not from fullness of life. Medieval monastic "regimentation of the blood" prepared the modern will to the deed; the Middle Ages ended with printing and firearms. Evidence comes largely from Görres's Christliche Mystik: Abbot Daniel (the body withers, the soul flourishes; Klages substitutes "spirit"), Bernard of Clairvaux's deadened senses, deliberate disgust in diet, the fight against sleep (two to three hours), Ruysbroeck, Elpidius, Macarius, Andreas of Salos's vision, stigmata, and John of God's superhuman labours and rescue from fire. Mission, the Reconquista, Crusades and heresy-hunts show a superpersonal will to power. Eckhart's "detachment", the "imageless" and the "pure nothing" are the will turned inward producing emptiness. This is the inward counterpart of the Eleatics. 8. Renaissance and after [763–766]. The "Protestant" personality is a brief interlude. The Renaissance fulfils Christianity rather than opposing it (long self-quotation from 1907, note 36: Medusa-glance, Botticelli, Leonardo's smile). Kant's autonomy is followed within 54 years by Stirner's, and the owner of the will becomes possessed by the will ("racing-machine … idol of the greatest number"). Mechanism flows into formalism, and calculation ends in sign-manipulation without thought (the calculating machine). The "fully consummated formalist would be an unconscious apparatus of precision" (from Grundlagen). 9. Eschatology [766–768]. History, about 7,000 years, is a brief interruption of the cosmic course. Promethean phase until late antiquity, Heraclean since Christianity. Post-historical humanity, "close before the door", will be unconscious again, steered by "the parasite named spirit" in place of the soul, and will soon die out because parasitic processes run out of material. Signs: "Americanism", press-made opinion, marionettes blinded by narrow-mindedness and wire-pullers blinded by evil will. Note 38 glosses narrow-mindedness (Borniertheit) as the inherited, organic effect of the will and so worse than conscious evil will. Technology has wiped out tribes and species within four generations. "The hour of counteraction was missed". The closing wish is that this humanity perish quickly so the forests can return.

Key terms. Persönliches Ich; Charakter (original and momentary); Bewußtsein = Besinnungsvermögen (capacity for reflection); Stellung/Richtungsbestimmtheit (stance, directional determinacy); prometheische / herakleische Stufe; Sehertum / Nüchternheit; Wille zum Werk / Wille zur Tat; Spaltungsenergie and Aktfrequenz (energy and frequency of acts); obere Grenze (upper limit of consciousness); posthistorische Menschheit.

Contribution to the whole. It sums up Books II–IV (spirit as splitting act; consciousness as reflection, ch. 24–39; will, ch. 41–52) and turns them into a philosophy of history. The Promethean/Heraclean scheme and the theme of seership return in the Pelasgian part (ch. 71–75). The deferred questions (point of impact of the act, esoteric states) are explicitly handed to Vol. III. The mythical symbol promised for the "vulnerable spot" is evidently the subject of ch. 70 ("The Achilles' Heel"). The waking/sleeping axis set up here is taken up at once in ch. 55.

Critical note. The two-variable model of spiritual acts (depth and frequency of splitting), with consciousness lost at both limits, is the chapter's most original and potentially testable idea, and it explains why both mystical emptiness and frantic busyness count as "spirit". The historical part rests on mythological interpretation that Klages admits he presents "in a more dogmatic form" [750] and on evidence picked from Görres. Its claim that Christian ecstasy cannot be an excess of life does cite markers (preceding mortification, the anaesthesia of the ecstatics in note 35, Eckhart's own "imageless" and "pure nothing"), but none of these excludes a vital rapture with ascetic preconditions, so the classification still does most of the work. The fatalism ("whatever we will … we must will it"; remorse as delusion) sits poorly with the close, which does not summon ("the hour of counteraction was missed") but condemns as "infamy" deeds whose agents, on this account, could not have willed otherwise.

Chapter 54 — Ancient and Modern Type of Researcher (Antiker und neuzeitlicher Forschertyp) [769–780]

Thesis. The predictive and technical success of physics does not prove that its objects are actual. Countability, uniform rectilinear motion and duration all contradict the actuality of images and of actuality-time. Machines can destroy life but never make it. The modern researcher is not a weaker mind than the ancient one but an unknowing servant of a will hostile to life: an examining magistrate where the ancient was a reader of oracles.

Argument. 1. The case for physics [769–770]. Eclipse prediction, Joule heating, precipitation, predicted planets and elements, solar chemistry, Römer and Bradley on the speed of light, Mayer's mechanical equivalent of heat, Pascal's paradox, interference. The world of machines seems an experimentum crucis. 2. Countability fails [770–771]. Counting actual units would need strictly individuated, congruent portions of appearance. But appearing already means connection with a beholder and with the whole. Each appearance is "the momentarily illumined place of the appearing All", and a moved jug is no longer "the same". The countable thing is a bearer postulated by spirit. 3. Uniform rectilinear motion fails [771–772]. Every motion changes the moving thing's relation to the world-whole, and so its conditions. Every place is unique. Klages admits these are still analogies, because in pure appearance there are no places at all. 4. Duration fails [772–774]. Lived experience and happening share one time. The soul's gaze falls toward the past and sees "unstoppable fleeing", an "eternity-presentness", so each moment is the offspring of all earlier ones. Ascribing duration (sameness over a span) destroys what makes each moment other. "Actuality-time cannot be quantified" [773]. Applying the transience of living beings to everything: planets, rocks, metals and the chemical properties themselves are born and die (Heraclitean and Indian kalpas, Nietzsche's Nachlass: "a longer-lasting whim?"). The apparent constancy of laws reflects how short our observation is, like the apparent fixity of the fixed stars. 5. The fact of life [774–775]. Mechanics meets living things within its own world of things and has only concepts that understand machines. "In a world of things and forces there can be no living beings". The reverse direction works: mechanics can be read as separated aspects of a cosmic living whole. 6. The success argument reversed [775–776]. The machine is "nature outwitted and forced to enslave itself". Mechanical mastery would prove truth only in a world that was itself a machine; for a living world, proof would be making life. Eagle and gun, forest and newsprint, rivers measured in horsepower. Energies are conserved because lifeless and reversible, while life is no quantum and what the will kills does not return. We subdue nature "only in the measure of the extermination of life within it" [776]. 7. Two types of researcher [776–779]. The hostility works unconsciously: modern scientists are no less pure or penetrating than the ancients. The ancient met nature in fear or reverence, as a higher power whose hints are read (oracle-interpreter). The modern meets it as a conqueror (examining magistrate) and the experiment is "diplomatic scouting-services for a will to power". Comparisons: depth against breadth, clairvoyance against acuteness, mythic system-palaces against an "armory" of facts, tectonic mastery against analytic mastery. The admiration is real; the warnings concern lifelessness, the "feeling-ascetic matter-of-factness", and curiosity that "believes torture to be justified". When the mechanistic apparatus was applied to the world of essences it proved "a grammar of knowledge of the dead". 8. Antiquity's advantage and the three fabrications [779–780]. It was not lack of technology but the agreement of ancient thinking with the thinking of language that stopped Greek mechanism short of calculating physics. Its concepts were "poorer in object-content, more pliant". Still, the Greeks already took all three wrong turns: toward the thing (realism, materialism, atomism, sensualism), toward spirit (idealism, solipsism), and toward root concepts (criticism, panlogism, "so-called phenomenology"). The danger rises in that order, since things can be perceived, the I can at least be felt, and the pure concept can only be thought. The atomist can recover; the panlogist or phenomenologist is "draumstoli", denied dreams by the gods. Hence the insights into life among the atomists, Aristotle calling the hylozoists dreamers, and Nietzsche praising Epicurus beside Heraclitus.

Key terms. Tatsachenglaube (belief in facts); Wirklichkeitszeit (actuality-time) versus Dauer (duration); Ewigkeitsgegenwärtigkeit ("eternity-presentness"); Vergänglichkeit (transience); Forschertyp; the three Erdichtungen (fabrications) of thing, spirit and root concept; draumstoli.

Contribution to the whole. Book IV's epilogue against physics. It applies Book I, Part 2 (ch. 4–6: the incapacity of motion for being, the unactuality of the time-object, appearance against thought-thing) and ch. 26 (living against mechanical process). It answers the obvious objection left by ch. 50–52. The ranking of the three fabrications fits Klages's long polemic against Husserlian phenomenology and logicism, and the verdict on atomists prepares the Greek survey in ch. 57.

Critical note. The point that conserved energy and reversible processes are exactly what is lifeless, and so cannot stand as a model of life, is well aimed. The argument against countability is weaker. From "appearing is being-in-connection with a beholder and the whole" it infers that nothing appearing can be individuated. But relational embedding does not by itself rule out countable individuals, and Klages needs exactly such individuation to say "the same jug" has changed. He does offer an explanation of predictive success: physics takes a standpoint so distant that real transformation drops below perceptibility (as with the fixed stars), and it calculates only the time-constant, lifeless aspect of things. But this concedes that the aspect is really there to be calculated. So the charge of "fabrication" comes down to a charge of one-sidedness, and the claim that mastery would prove truth "only in a world that was itself a machine" is asserted, not argued.

Book V, Part One — The Actuality of Images: The Doctrine (Die Wirklichkeit der Bilder: Die Lehre)

Chapter 55 — Waking and Sleeping (Wachen und Schlafen) [803–823]

Opens Vol. III, Book V "The Actuality of Images", Part 1 "Doctrine", Section 1 "Spirit and Soul".

Thesis. Sleep is not an absence of lived experience but a state of beholding (Schauen) without sensing (Empfinden). The proof is that dreams contain every sensory quality but never real bodily pain. So the one world of everyday speech has two sources: beholding an actuality of images and sensing the bodily palpability of those images. Waking is a repeated being-awakened by the sensing body. Since reflection happens only when awake, spirit gets its grip on life through the body (sensing), not through the soul (beholding).

Argument. 1. Method [803–804]. From now on conceptual language serves mainly to point at the inconceivable. Beholding lies "this side" of both perception and intuition, reachable only by the pointing word. The bridge is the difference between waking and sleeping. 2. Against school psychology [804–805]. It is really a theory of knowledge, ignoring that the activity of the senses and consciousness stop and start periodically. Its "stream of consciousness" is really a stream of life. Saying the sleeper has consciousness is the mirror-image of the Eleatic error: the Eleatics denied the continuity of happening, these thinkers assert a continuity of consciousness, and both treat only consciousness as actual. Reductio: a conscious sleeper could at any time reflect on his own sleeping and look back on the day, but he cannot and does not know he dreams. The "I am dreaming" dream is a transitional state and gives no retrospective relation. 3. Dreaming refutes sensualism [805–806]. If one only grants that dreaming is possible, the sleeping state is a kind of beholding without sensing. This exposes an ambiguity in nihil est in intellectu quod non prius fuerit in sensibus (sensibility ≠ capacity for sensation), as it already did in esse est percipi. Objection from the congenitally blind: how dream relates to sense-intuition is a separate question from whether its awareness of images is sensing. 4. Structure [806–808]. "We are unceasingly sleeping … beings, with intervals of act-capable sensing". Waking is always fully interrupted by sleep, never sleep by waking; beneath waking, beholding flows on. Polarity of inner (central) and outer (peripheral) pole, never symmetrical. Waking is "an ever and again renewed being-awakened". Without awakening stimuli we slide into sleep or waking dream. How easily, and how deeply, one sinks into the sleep-state are differences of character. Dream-waking predominated in prehistoric symbolic thought, reflective waking in "civilized" conceptuality. 5. The criterion: painlessness [808–811]. Dream and waking match in felt actuality, emotion, meaning and qualities (colours, sounds, even tactile qualities). The difference: Liezi's dream-people walk through fire unharmed, and more generally bodily pain is undreamable. Dreamers who report pain confuse the phasm of something painful (or real anguish of soul) with bodily pain. Evidence: the critical majority of reports, dreams of the sick, ecstasy, and above all the universal habit of testing waking by pain (Abu Hassan in "The Sham Caliph", Arabian Nights: "bite my finger"). Premise: every sensation can rise to pain. So nothing is sensed in dream, and dream actuality is bodiless. 6. Conclusion [811–812]. Dual source: beholding (images) and sensing (bodily palpability). The sleeper visits cities while his body lies in a room. He can never dream real toothache or real orgasm (which wakes him). "Qualities are elements of images, and images are beheld … but … never sensed" [812]. We posit things because we sense bodies. Sensing is the periphery of the life-sphere whose centre is beholding (the Romantic "primal sense"; see ch. 23). 7. Genetic order and the ideology of the triad [812–816]. The sensing pole needs the beholding pole, not the reverse. With its soul-side the living being touches the pre-polar cosmos, "Night … mother of Day" (Görres). So the ranking body–soul–spirit is wrong twice: spirit is not coordinate with life, and soul precedes body as sleep precedes waking. Plant = sleeping soul-actuality, animal = waking bodily actuality (Schubert, Hufeland, tree cults). Taking daylight for spirit's light is a "siren-voice". Heraclitus: "Death is what we see while waking". Plato, Plotinus and Novalis, though deep, wavered toward a "higher" spirit. The only genuine trinity is soul and body polarised in the life-cell. The Dionysian, not the Apollonian, is the world's image-streaming eye (against early Nietzsche); Socrates is the model of spiritual man. Hegel was right that world-history is spirit's self-development, but in destructive opposition to life. 8. Spirit allies with the body [816]. This restates, "more briefly", a result of the Book III section on mirroring beholding (ch. 29–33). Reflection happens only when awake, so its immediate enabling ground is sensing. "Spiritualization" works through a one-sided alliance with the body, which explains why the disciples of the Logos split into materialists and idealists. 9. Dreamless sleep is still experienced [816–823]. We remember having slept deeply, and recollection concerns what happened. We wake in a changed mood (forebodings, resolutions). Plato's Apology on dreamless sleep. We wake knowing time has passed (the "head-clock"). We can wake at a set hour. Against Locke (Adam and Eve's lost 24 hours) and the "noetician" who sees unconscious spirit in timed waking: the resolve only retunes vitality's metre; vitality, not thought, does the timing. This is supported by how absorbing talk or boredom distorts the timing. The source of time-experience is the rhythm of organic (and cosmic) happening, above all breath and pulse, most even in sleep. Birds turning up at feeding time and bees returning at the same hour show unconscious time-estimation without counting. Sleep-physiology changes with sleep-duration, so each wave of sleep carries "temporal signs". Timing is more exact asleep, because the gusty sensory-motor outer rhythm, broken up by spiritual acts, is quiet. What is experienced in dreamless sleep is again images, but of the vegetative cell-life, which consciousness cannot attest. The body is released "in the fluids of the All". Temple-sleep, somnambulism and Carus's "life-innering" point to a "night-pole of the soul".

Key terms. Schauen/Schauung (beholding: imageful lived experience prior to sensation and intuition); Empfinden/Empfindung (sensing: bodily, pain-capable contact with corporeality); Anschauung (intuition, beholding plus sensing); Phantasma/Phasma (dream-image); Erwecktwerden (being-awakened); Leibseele; Kopfuhr (head-clock); Lebensverinnerung (Carus, "life-innering"); Nachtpol der Seele.

Contribution to the whole. Opens the positive doctrine of the actuality of images. It cashes out Vol. I's beholding/sensing distinction (ch. 17, 22, 26–28) and supplies the soul/body polarity used throughout Book V (ch. 58–61), the plant/animal stage-theory (ch. 56, 64), and the thesis that spirit attacks through the body (ch. 56 [841]). It also answers ch. 53's question about the "warm darkness".

Critical note. Using pain as the decisive criterion is an elegant phenomenological argument. It explains the folk practice of pinching oneself and gives a non-question-begging line between imaged and sensed corporeality. But it rests on two contestable premises: that pain is never dreamed (Klages himself notes about a third of those asked disagree, and explains them away), and that every sensation can rise to pain. Even granting both, the inference is short. Absence of pain shows only that nothing in the dream is sensed intensely, since faint sensations need not approach pain. And the claim that qualities are "never sensed", even when awake [812], does not follow: the dream shows that qualities can appear without sensing, not that waking qualities are not sensed.

Chapter 56 — Beholding and Conceiving Wakefulness (Schauende und begreifende Wachheit) [824–849]

Thesis. Beholding as such is receptivity for distance (Ferneempfänglichkeit): the presence of the There, and of the past, in the Here of the living being. It is shown in plants by sleeping cosmic rhythms. Animals add bodily wakefulness (sensation of nearness). Man alone reaches the appearance of distance in a beholding wakefulness. Conceiving wakefulness (begreifende Wachheit) turns qualitative distance into measurable remoteness (das Entfernte) to be overcome and owned. It is will in disguise and the deadly enemy of beholding, while beholding at its height is a fusion with fleeting actuality: "the soul is the transience of the body", and "time is the soul of space".

Argument. 1. Two sleeps, two wakings [824]. Plants' sleeping beholding is not our sleep. In sleep only the sensory-motor outer layer rests, and the vegetative layer goes deeper. New question: how waking beholding differs from sleeping beholding. 2. Beholding and working [824–825]. The counterpart of beholding is a "creative" (not mechanical) working (Wirken), the ancient symbol of weaving. The two belong polarly together. Since an onlooker reads the soul only from what is "wrought", Klages begins with observable happening. 3. Cosmic rhythms [825–827]. Day and night, seasons (sleep depth; annual curves of conceptions, sex crimes, suicides), moulting, migration, hibernation, rut. Lunar rhythms: menstruation and gestation, felling timber by the moon, sea anemones keeping tidal rhythm in still water (and opening to artificial waves only at tide-time), and the palolo worm swarming exactly by moon-phase. Method: proceed from the widest whole to its members. Telluric rhythms (snowmelt, groundwater, magnetism, aurora) and forms (dunes, barchans, cloud-trains, ranges), with river networks, trees and dendrites alike. So organic and cosmic processes are polar forms of one rhythmic whole, alive "even if supra-organically so". 4. Against "sensation" and "mechanism" explanations [827–829]. To call these responses sense-processes or mechanisms is "a frivolous game … with words". Senses serve approach and flight, not moulting. Graeser on bird migration: larks arrive in apparent winter, and autumn departure happens when food is most plentiful. The marmot's lined burrow and the hamster's stores. "Instinct" only renames the problem. 5. Definition [829–830]. Instinctive happening presupposes "a distance-connection, or a receptivity for distance". That is beholding as such, and the soul insofar as it beholds. Reconciling this with ch. 51's ban on action at a distance: that concerned body acting on body without a medium; here it is "the working of distance itself", the presence of distance in the bearer of soul. Mechanically impossible, and for that reason called the gift of beholding. 6. Microcosm [830–831]. Remove God from the Renaissance microcosm idea and keep the truth that every self-being is the cosmos again. Distance appears in organic processes as against processes of nearness (if nearness predominates the life-horizon narrows; if distance predominates the tension slackens). The cosmos contains organisms, which means "elemental" cosmic life-forms (not the Romantic organism-cosmos), with reciprocity. The Hippocratic sympnoia mia is deferred to ch. 57. The plant, fixed in place, survives by fine receptivity to light, darkness and gravity, and in part is day, night and season. Leaf-fall is "harmonized" from inside, not caused by cold. Vegetation shapes climate as climate shapes vegetation, so deforestation alters climates and "climatic essences". 7. Animal ladder [832–834]. The animal adds bipolar bodily wakefulness: sensation (receptor side) and locomotion from drive-impulses (effector side), suited to the near. From protozoa to mammals the soul grows more awake, and distance enters as appearance (homing in ants, bees, birds, predators). Sleeping beholding has distance only as working; waking-capable beholding has the appearance of distance. In original man (ch. 35) the poles reverse: the soul's distance-sight leads the body's sensation of nearness. Temporal side: presence of distance is presence of the past. Reproduction, morphogenesis, and the hand, eye and semicircular canals are "temporal distance congealed into life-forms". Klages ranks the time-depths: formation (millions of years) > instinct (generations) > memory (hours to one lifetime). This explains (and limits) the "memory of matter" theories. 8. Why physics cannot reach this [835]. Physical effects (weight, illumination, induction) happen "on the spot" and carry no pointer to their source. Life, and above all beholding, is "the Here-being of the There as There", with the interval beheld along with it. 9. Man's distance-space [835–838]. The meadow, tree and drops before me become the tiny cut-out of an intuited All joined to sky, clouds, poplar avenue, church tower and névés. An animal's world falls short of ours as ours would fall short of a four-dimensional being's. Only now is the near-image an "image in the narrowest sense". Likewise the individual life appears against the ancestors: man remembers, the animal has memory, and earliest humanity is bound to its prehistory by cult. Key equation: what the waking beholding soul sees in the distances of space "are — distances of time". Historical illustration (after Wölfflin): Baroque and later painting deepens space (Bouts, Massys, Patinir, Dürer, Titian against the Brueghels, Rembrandt, Ruisdael, Claude, Corot, Turner, Friedrich). A self-quotation on the sea-horizon and the steamer's smoke equates felt spatial depth with absorption in an irretrievable youth. 10. The turn to conceiving wakefulness [838–841]. Rubens is "full of space" but without the magic of distance: massive nearness. In the original image distance is a quality, polar to nearness and unreachable (chase the sunset at the earth's speed and the red stays far; go faster and it fades into day). When judgement takes hold, quality falls away and only the remote is left, reachable in principle and different from the near only in amount. Space and time of actuality become metric space and time, and the two-poled whole becomes the directed stretch between things. "Conceiving" (begreifen) means taking possession (compare antasten, laying hands on; touching cult images as sacrilege). So "conceiving wakefulness is unconsciously willing wakefulness". Technology (rail, car, plane, telephone, radio, star-rocket) is distance-hatred: measure distances in order to erase them. 11. Why spirit could enter [841–843]. Sleeping beholding could not produce consciousness (distance stays enclosed in the Here), and neither could animal wakefulness (no image of distance). Only a being with "beholding-at-a-distance" brings a dangerous tension that loosens the body–soul bond and opens a way in for spirit. Spirit's threefold aim is to separate nearness from distance, sensation from beholding, and body from soul, in each case to kill the second. A second danger is the "disparateness" of the senses. The sleeping all-sense had to split into individual senses for a world-wide appearance of distance, risking the soul's failing "clamping power", and these senses become spirit's dissecting tool. Waking consciousness is potentially counting consciousness, and number is a symbol of changelessness. Will deals with the individual case and empties the image of its boundlessness (back-references to pp. 646, 664, 676). 12. Nimbus and the actuality of the past [843–849]. The felt size of space varies with psychic bearing: the remembered childhood barn, Piranesi's Rome, Calame's Alps. The ancients' "images in the nimbus" (candida et lucida nubes; Achilles' flaming head, Iliad 18.203–227): the nimbus is the "fluidum" between soul and image, through which every archetype (Urbild) reaches to the All. The beholder is "embraced by the past" and does not remember it at a temporal distance. Two pointers: (a) the returning wanderer who sees not things but "monuments of generations-long fates", and "Archetypes are appearing souls of the past" [846]; (b) the shudder of transience, without which no before-and-after (the bird leaving the branch) could be experienced. Time-experience: we are ordinarily conscious only of objective time. Happiness shortens and boredom lengthens time only relative to it. Below that lies "unstoppable fleeing", which is the substrate that lets judgement distinguish time from space. In beholding, the estranging phase gives way to a fusing phase: the "one truly genuine unio mystica", unity with happening at the time-pole and the image's "becoming-two" at the space-pole. The circle closes on the proposition anticipated "at the entrance of this book": "the soul is the transience of the body", for every self-being from the unicellular organism to the galaxies, and "time is the soul of space". The chapter ends on a comma in both the German and the English text [849]; the formula returns in ch. 62 ("Time as the soul of space, every distance a distance-of-the-past").

Key terms. Ferneempfänglichkeit (receptivity for distance = beholding as such); Wirken und Weben (creative working and weaving); Ferne (qualitative distance) versus das Entfernte / Entfernung (measurable remoteness); Ferneerscheinung (appearance of distance); schauende / begreifende Wachheit; Begreifen (as grasping and possession); Mikrokosmos; elementarische Lebensformen; Urbild (archetype, primal image); Nimbus; Allsinn (all-sense); Vergänglichkeit (transience); Wirklichkeitszeit.

Contribution to the whole. This is the heart of Section 1. It defines the soul through beholding of distance and links the spatial and temporal poles. It prepares the "Nearness and Distance" section (ch. 62–65; ch. 64 is cited for plant rhythms, ch. 65 treats the elemental souls), the efficacy of images (ch. 66–70), and the Pelasgian account of ancestor-cult and the past (ch. 71–75). It cashes out ch. 17, 26–28 (Gefälle, transience, the irretrievable) and ch. 35 (original man's pole-change), and turns ch. 51's critique of action-at-a-distance into a positive vitalist counterpart.

Critical note. The contrast between Ferne as unreachable quality and Entfernung as quantity to be crossed is a real phenomenological finding (the sunset example is exact), and it ties the critique of technology to the theory of perception without strain. The weak point is the move from rhythmic correlations (palolo, sea anemones, leaf-fall) to a "receptivity for distance" in the soul. Klages concedes that the anemones are coupled to the moon through the tidal wave. He then rules that kinship through a third term is still essential kinship ("daughter and her mother's brother") and that inquiry must go from the whole to its members [826]. On that rule every mediated causal correlation counts as a distance-connection, so the thesis cannot be refuted by finding a mechanism. He admits the reading is gained by viewing the process "as though we ourselves were its bearer" [832].

Arc of Ch. 50–56

The block joins the end of the Doctrine of the Will to the start of the Actuality of Images, and its unity is the reversal at that joint. Chapters 50–52 test the theory of volition from ch. 45–49 (will as a timeless act splitting the drive-impulse into quality and a driving or inhibiting intensity) against physics. Chapter 50 uses it to dissolve the will/conservation antinomy: the will spends no energy and only "evaporates" quality. Chapter 51 turns the same model into a genealogy. Physical force is will minus vital vehicle projected onto thing-points, and cause is generalised force. The endless causal chain, the force–mass circularity and the impossibility of transeunt action between isolated units all follow from that projection. Chapter 52 tests the result on the one rival who tried to make physics polar (Palágyi) and finds that his ether works only because it is secretly alive. The shared lesson: real working (Wirken) belongs only to living, experiencing poles, and physics by its nature gives only intransitive, calculable, life-emptied relations.

Chapters 53–54 sum up the volume. Chapter 53 translates the will doctrine into a theory of consciousness (energy and frequency of splitting acts, upper and lower limits), a theory of thinking (inward action from stance to stance), and a philosophy of history: the Promethean and Heraclean stages, Christian asceticism as the training-ground of will, the Renaissance as its release outward, and a post-historical, unconscious, spirit-steered humanity with a ruined earth. Chapter 54 answers the argument from predictive success. Countability, rectilinearity and duration fail against the actuality of images and of time. Machines kill but do not make life. The modern researcher is an unknowing agent of the will. The block thus presupposes Book I's thing/appearance and being/happening distinctions (ch. 4–6), Book II's properties-and-forces (ch. 21), Book III's thing-point = I-point and fatality of selfhood (ch. 38–39), and Book IV's lawfulness/necessity (ch. 42).

Chapters 55–56 change register openly. Conceptual language is now used to point. The positive counterpart to the dead "thing" is built in two steps. First (ch. 55), beholding is separated from sensing by the painlessness of dreams, which gives the soul/body polarity, the priority of sleep over waking, and the claim that spirit enters through the sensing body, not the beholding soul. This last claim revises the usual idealist picture and matches ch. 53's point that spirit attacks on the side of impulse. Second (ch. 56), beholding as such is defined as receptivity for distance. It rises from sleeping plants through animals to man's waking appearance of distance, while conceiving wakefulness, unconsciously willing, flattens distance into remoteness to be measured and abolished. The ending ("soul is the transience of the body"; "time is the soul of space") gives positive content to what ch. 51 and 54 denied of physics: unquantifiable actuality-time and transeunt working.

Several tensions are worth noting. (1) Ch. 51 declares action at a distance a logical impossibility, and ch. 56 rescues "working of distance" by changing the subject from bodies to souls. Whether this is a principled distinction or a reclassification is the crux of Book V. (2) Ch. 50's solution makes the will a separator that changes direction "toward the purpose", which is hard to reconcile with ch. 50's own rejection of energy-free "steering". (3) Ch. 53's strict determinism ("whatever we will … we must will it"; remorse as delusion) sits awkwardly with the moral force of its close and of ch. 54. (4) Ch. 55 places spirit's foothold in sensation and the body, while ch. 53 speaks of the will "de-souling the body and disembodying the soul". The two are compatible only if "body" means the sensing periphery, not organic life. (5) Ch. 52 praises in Palágyi exactly the unexplained polar efficacy that ch. 51 denies to physics, and asks it to pay the price of being "myth". Klages accepts this, so the doctrine of images claims a different, non-explanatory kind of truth whose standards Book V must still state.

Handed forward: the "point of impact" of spiritual acts (ch. 53, deferred to Vol. III) and the mythic "vulnerable spot" (evidently ch. 70, "The Achilles' Heel"); the Greek prehistory of the doctrine of images (ch. 57, announced at [830–831]); the nearness/distance polarity and the stretching of tension in man (ch. 62–65); elemental life-forms (ch. 65) and the reciprocity of microcosm and cosmos and the efficacy of images (ch. 66–70); and the actuality of the past through ancestor-cult and the nimbus (Pelasgian part, ch. 71–75).

Chapter 57 — From the Prehistory of the Discovery of Images (Aus der Vorgeschichte der Entdeckung der Bilder) [850–923]

Thesis. The doctrine of the reality of images (Wirklichkeit der Bilder) is not Klages's invention. It is the end-point of an intermittent, repeatedly buried line of Western insight. Every earlier bearer of that insight distorted it with "partisan additions" from spirit (Geist). And the insight's coming and going is itself explained by the life–spirit struggle, punctuated by "telluric turns," the last of which (c. 1890) has passed.

Argument. 1. Disclaimer and method [850–853]. This is not a history of ideas. It aims only to show which past insights the doctrine owes something to, at one remove. Interpretive rule: each finding carries additions from its discoverer's will to master life, and its true sense shows less in the formulas than in the "sensuous colouring" that betrays the begetting intuition. Hence the reading against the grain that runs through the chapter: what was beheld (heart) is separated from how it was construed (head). The early Greeks are privileged because they won their concepts directly from language and myth, not from a ready-made technical vocabulary. The meta-scheme of Greek development: symbol → person → judgement/spirit → concept and object, with the will hidden in the middle. 2. Heraclitus [853–859]. The Eleatics discovered being; Heraclitus discovered actuality (panta rhei). Concepts cannot take in the flux, so the world of objects is the deceptive one. Positive additions: rhythm and polarity in the alternation of images; strife = harmony; universal aliveness (panzoism); his "abstracta" as symbolic, not rational, concepts (the "Delphic style"). Defection: the solar logos, the "all-governing" world-spirit, nomos. This hands the Stoics their whip. 3. Protagoras (with a brief nod to Empedocles and Democritus's eidola) [859–861]. Perception is an undergoing (paschein): the aistheton is begotten anew each moment by the meeting of soul and happening. That is a near-discovery of the image. But Protagoras merges image with thing and so slides into scepticism about the validity of judgement. Plato's self-refutation objection is correct against the scepticism, not against the underlying intuition. 4. Plato [861–866]. Plato divides the world into phenomenal and noumenal and the soul into three parts, and he aligns noumena / Eleatic being / spirit's power against phainomena / Heraclitean flux / spirit's powerlessness. His key "falsification": he gives the soul's gift of beholding (anamnesis, Eros, the sudden vision of primal images) to discriminating reason, so as to make noesis attractive. Result, extracted against his will: the body has drives and sensations, the soul beholds, the spirit apprehends. Only the last is active. 5. Aristotle [866–868]. Three findings that break his own system: thought thinking itself (noesis noeseos) as the mark of the I; the split between nous poietikos and nous pathetikos; and spirit entering "from outside" (thyrathen). The last anticipates Klages's thesis that consciousness arises from the clash of two irreducible powers. 6. Plotinus, Plutarch [868–873]. Nature's "contemplation" is like sleep-consciousness; reflection marks a falling-off. The emanation scheme works only by quietly substituting a reality of images (eikon) for the spiritual. Plutarch's essay on the Delphic E (quoted at length) frames Western thought as the undecided contest between Eleaticism and Heracliteanism. 7. Medieval philosophy [873–875]. Greek wisdom passes through the dogmas of a will-religion: all inwardness recast as deed, creation from nothing, history as the register of deeds. Yet it still falls back on visio … sine comprehensione (a seeing without grasping). 8. Renaissance mysticism (Cusanus, Paracelsus, Agrippa, Bruno, van Helmont, Campanella) [875–887]. The Copernican change is read as a change in lived space: the near-Eros of the closed Greek sphere gives way to an Eros of distance. Three gains: form as a limit on the boundless; individual being as the expression of cosmic life (microcosm, physiognomics of the universe); and, weightiest, the first real separation of sensing from soul-beholding (imaginatio, "sidereal body," images as real and acting at a distance, waking in sleep). The verdict: important contributions to the prehistory, "but by no means that discovery itself." The missing feature is the shudder of transience. These thinkers grasped the image of space, not the primal image of time, so the age ended in mathematics and mechanism. 9. Romanticism (Carus, Schubert, Burdach, Hufeland, Passavant, Schindler, Oken, Novalis, Görres, Baader, Kieser, J. J. Wagner) [887–901]. A day-pole (brain, senses, will, judgement) and a night-pole (ganglia, the "all-sense," instinct, clairvoyance). Error: they model beholding on sensation and so invent a second sensory centre. Decisive advance: "for the first time" the experience of time governs the experience of space ("space is a precipitate out of time"); the depth of the past opens; polarity is read as cosmic Eros (sun/earth, male/female). Novalis brushes against the primal polarity of soul and image. Limit: they confuse the older telluric phase with the rule of spirit itself. 10. Arndt and Daumer [901–906]. The first to take an explicit stand against spirit. Arndt: spirit as "separating and de-deifying," it "made the whole of nature into a cadaver." Daumer: spirit in the Christian sense as absolute negation. Arndt's Gestalt, the dark womb of becoming, effaces "the last remnant of the Platonic Idea." Romantic poetry, as distinct from Romantic thought: "the actuality of images is — present." 11. Bachofen, Nietzsche, Schuler [906–911], reprinted from Vom kosmogonischen Eros. They stand to Romanticism "as knowledge stands to presentiment." Bachofen: night-consciousness and the mother (chthonism) came before day-consciousness (uranism); split between heart and head. Nietzsche: the Dionysian, but an unclear "Apollonian" that is really Socratism. Schuler works within symbolic thinking itself. 12. Concluding reflection: a theory of history [911–923]. Idealist and materialist historiography both fail, because consciousness is only ever effect, never cause. The effective powers are life and spirit. Life changes in three ways: the earth ages; a vitality coupled to spirit swings periodically, at shorter and shorter intervals; and there are telluric turns, outbreaks of the earth's life-fire, marked by aura in their bearers (the Romantics have it, the Classicists lack it). Each turn shows first in inwardness and only later in deeds. The last turn peaked c. 1890 (late Nietzsche, Schuler, Klages's own formative experiences of 1889–92), with the 1892 Warundi frenzy given as parallel evidence. It was "the last, because with it the essence left the globe of the earth." Hence "dithyrambists of decline."

Key terms. Entdeckung der Bilder (discovery of images); Vorgeschichte (prehistory, here of a doctrine); Eros der Nähe / Eros der Ferne (Eros of nearness / of distance); Tag-pol / Nachtpol (day-pole / night-pole); Allsinn (all-sense); siderischer Leib (sidereal body); imaginatio; tellurische Wende (telluric turn); Aura; Dithyrambiker des Untergangs (dithyrambists of decline).

Contribution to the whole. It gives the systematic doctrine of Book V a lineage, and it uses the doctrine's own criteria to rank predecessors: images not things (Ch. 20–23), beholding not sensation (Ch. 29, 56), time above space (Ch. 58–62), spirit as adversary (Book II, Ch. 39). It applies the life–spirit duality as a philosophy of history (prepared in Ch. 28 and 53), and it previews the Pelasgian world-picture (Ch. 71–75) and Bachofen (Ch. 74).

Critical note. The reading of each thinker into heart and head is fertile but unfalsifiable: whatever fits is "beheld," whatever does not is spirit's "addition." The theory of telluric turns rests explicitly on an inner certainty that, by Klages's own admission, cannot be checked [923]. That turns the chapter's historical argument into prophecy at its climax.

Book V, Part 1: Book V Section 2 "Soul and Body" (Zweiter Abschnitt — Seele und Leib, ch. 58–61) and the opening chapter of Section 3 "Nearness and Distance" (Dritter Abschnitt — Nähe und Ferne, ch. 62).

Chapter 58 — The Here and Now (Das Hier und Jetzt) [924–955]

Thesis. Every sense-experience has two components that cannot be reduced to each other: sensing (Empfinden) and beholding/intuiting (Schauen/Anschauen). Taken by itself, sensation is the experience of resistance and nothing else (Widerstandserlebnis). It contains only differences of degree, it cannot be separated from self-movement (Eigenbewegung), and in it lies the experience of the "here", which is doubled into an acting here and a here that receives the effect. Corporeality (Körperlichkeit) belongs only to a being that both intuits and senses through its own movement. It is impenetrability, and impenetrability lasts longer than mere appearance.

Argument. 1. Setting the problem [924–926]. Klages lists the questions left open so far: how sameness can apply to an actuality that is always happening, what makes the existential judgement (Existenzialurteil) possible, how movement, corporeality, place and matter are apprehended. He says all of them turn on finding where the "here" comes from, as the essence of the "now" was found earlier. He proceeds by "distinguish and then combine" (Goethe): first he isolates sensation artificially and drives the isolation to its limit, then he puts it back into beholding. The guiding example is walking into an armchair in the dark. That collision gives experience of resistance = hindered self-movement = corporeality of the hindering thing = corporeality of one's own body = a "doubled here". This is offered as the "breaking-point" where spirit's "separating lightning-deed" can strike. Klages admits the procedure is more geometrico: he states the theses first and proves them afterwards. 2. Polarity premise [926–927]. Vital poles are never symmetrical, as natural symmetries are never exactly mirror-equal (self-quotation, n. 28). They can always be expressed as one pole depending on the other. This prepares the claim that one pole rules. 3. Seven theses on sensation, taken from touch [927–931]. (1) Sensation is experience of resistance. (2) It is experience of intensity, since resistances can be compared only by strength. Klages notes that comparison already goes beyond "pure" sensation and that complete isolation is impossible. He then converts the equation (resistance = intensity): every experience of intensity is an experience of resistance, so differences of brightness, loudness and so on are differences of resistance suffered. He says openly that this is "dogmatic" for now and that the grounds come later. (3) Sensation involves pressure and counter-pressure (pushing a wardrobe, stopping a rolling ball). (4) It involves conflict. Passive touch, as when a finger touches the back of my hand, still contains counter-pressure "potentially" as a feeling of tension (Spannungsgefühl). Klages avoids the words activity and passivity "for the time being" because they carry associations of will and spirit. (5) There is no sensing without self-movement, and no self-movement without sensing. Genetically, motility is the ruling pole, so trying to understand animality from its receptivities is "fundamentally mistaken". A recap of the drive-impulse from ch. 46 follows: it is felt as seizing its bearer, it is directed, and it is tied to want. Want becomes an impulse only through resistance of position. Scent, colour and sound are not resistances; they are accompaniments of another origin. Klages takes back the word "stimulus" (Reiz) for this narrow sense alone. Stimuli differ only in strength, and so play no part in what a song or a painting is. (6) Sensation contains the here-experience. (7) Through the conflict within resistance the here doubles into an acting and a receiving here, so sensation contains the condition for separating here from there. 4. Protozoa as confirmation [931–942]. This is the empirical core, drawn mainly from Jennings, Das Verhalten der niedersten Organismen. Amoeba, bacteria and Paramecium/Stentor show: unceasing self-movement ("the animal is an event", Jennings; "never does a stimulus strike the same animal twice", von Uexküll). They react to resistance with avoidance, and react more readily when moving than at rest. Klages takes this as proof that the movements are spontaneous, not reflexes. Reflexes he reserves for multicellular organisms with subordinate apparatus. Front and rear ends differ in irritability, which is a rudimentary localization ("here-in-front" differs from "here-behind"). Reaction is graded by stimulus strength. Klages corrects Jennings, who held that every reaction is ultimately negative: there are genuine seeking movements, such as amoeba pursuing a euglena cyst, or conjugation. These show optima valued for themselves, so resistance alone cannot explain seeking. 5. Why intensity is always resistance [937–940]. A song or a painting is experienced as itself, not as the ear or eye. But a pistol shot by the ear, or a look at the noon sun, splits experience into image and pain in the organ. Every impression can be heightened to pain, and at the limit the image is destroyed ("dazzled"). So a degree-sensitive component of resistance runs through every sense. It is strongest in the near senses (touch, taste), weaker in smell, weakest in hearing and sight. The "skin senses" (pressure, warmth, cold, pain) are grouped as sensible apparatus, spread over the body surface, which report one's own body. The sensory apparatus, confined to small regions, report images of a universe that does not concern the body. Here Klages locates the "vital source of comparison". The concept of degree comes from sensing, and we carry it over onto qualities we only appear to measure. Linguistic support comes in n. 30: sehr < MHG sêre, "painful". 6. Nerveless sensitivity [941–942]. Protozoa respond in graded ways to chemical, thermal, light and acoustic vibrations without sense organs. Stentor turns its sensitive front away from the light "as if it could see". Klages concludes that sensitivity is a property of animal protoplasm, not a "specific sensory energy". In developed organisms it serves as a "platform" for localizing images. 7. Reinserting sensation into the whole [943–947]. Sensation alone could not give an outside or a duality. Beneath it lies the "effecting–beholding" state already described for the plant. Protozoa show (1) receptivity to kind: they rest on fibrous but not glass surfaces, and purple bacteria gather in particular spectral bands. This is the experience of similarity proper to beholding. They show (2) the polarity of voluptuous pleasure and pain (Wollust und Schmerz): pleasure dissolves one's form into its living space, pain binds and isolates it. This polarity is distinct from the psychologist's continuous pleasure–unpleasure scale. They show (3) spatio-temporality: experienced locomotion implies an experienced space and time of movement, so memory of impression exists. Stentor runs through a series of behaviours, each changed by the one before. Front/back comes first, right/left begins in infusorians, above/below appears with Metazoa. In animals the working pole wakes before the beholding pole, and "beholding is led by the hand by sensing" all the way up to man. 8. Place, now-here, and the derivation of corporeality [947–955]. Klages quotes three programmatic passages from Vol. I (pp. 199, 308, 387) to show where the argument stands. Tactile space is built from a succession of here-impressions, as when I note one fingertip and then another. That succession is reversible, which distinguishes it from time and makes it the direction of space. The here-experience is always a now-here (Jetzthier). The now becomes independent by attaching to the place of resistance, and space, set against it, becomes the "always" that leads in the end to Euclidean space. Two kinds of alienation are contrasted. Appearance is far from my body but close to my soul. Alien corporeality is close to my body but far from my soul. Appearances can change as fast as a flash of lightning. Impenetrability has the character of persistence: it does not "vanish", it only moves on, while appearances transform and vanish absolutely. This is the germ of "resistance to non-existence", which reflection later develops into Democritean atomism. Conclusion: corporeality "befalls the gift of intuition of self-movingly-sensing beings". A being that only beheld would live in a "placeless void of placeless transformations". A being that only sensed is inconceivable. Intense sensation can replace the soul–world polarity with the soul–body duality, which is the split the next chapter uses.

Key terms. Empfindung/Empfinden (sensation: experience of resistance and degree only, not of quality). Schauen/Anschauen (beholding/intuiting, the qualitative, image-receiving component). Widerstandserlebnis (experience of resistance). Eigenbewegung (self-movement, translatory locomotion, preferred over Selbstbewegung to avoid any suggestion of the I). Spannungsgefühl (feeling of tension). Hiererlebnis, "doubled here" (an acting here and a receiving here). Jetzthier (now-here). sensibel vs sensorisch (body-reporting vs image-reporting apparatus). Wollust und Schmerz (genuine vital polarity, opposed to the scalar Lust/Unlust). Körperlichkeit (corporeality, defined as impenetrability).

Contribution to the whole. This chapter supplies the bodily counterpart to the genesis of intuition and the now in Book III and ch. 55–57. It gives a bodily location to the thesis, quoted from p. 280 (ch. 29), that reflection springs from points of disturbance. It gives ch. 59 its "doubled here" as the place where spirit strikes, and ch. 60 its formula for matter. Its protozoa material comes back in ch. 62, and its account of degree comes back in ch. 60 (intensity as the "primal predicate"). It also points ahead to ch. 63–65, Nearness and Distance.

Critical note. The strongest step is the phenomenological observation that intensity heightened to pain destroys the image. This is good evidence that degree and quality are separate components. The most vulnerable step is the converse equation, that every intensity is resistance. Klages asserts it "dogmatically" and supports it only by the pain-limit argument. That argument shows that a resistance component is always present, not that brightness or loudness as degree is nothing but that component. The protozoa evidence is also read through his own categories ("experience", "memory of impression"). His disagreement with Jennings shows how much of the reading is interpretation.

Chapter 59 — Body and Thing (Körper und Ding) [956–973]

Thesis. Spirit's original act (Urakt) cannot split a living polarity directly. It can split only a duality that has worked loose within it: the doubled impenetrability in a collision of bodies. By fixing a boundary there, spirit turns the body into a thing (Körperding), the "objectively primordially-identical" (das objektiv Uridentische) or "factual primordial object" (sachlicher Urgegenstand). This finding of the thing is the original existential judgement. All identity, and all decisions about existence, go back to tangible corporeality. The thing's sameness reflects the I's sameness, driven into the content of experience.

Argument. 1. Impenetrability means conflict [956–957]. Set shape aside and corporeality means impenetrability. This is not impenetrability for space, since a carried sphere does not push space along, but impenetrability for another body. Place therefore implies at least two places, first real places (bodies), later thought places, and finally objective and Euclidean space. 2. Where spirit strikes [957]. Polarity rests on the reciprocity of soul and image. Without the participation of intuition, sensation would be a mere duality of impenetrabilities. Spirit "cannot immediately split a polarized whole, because the splitting ray lacks a point of impact". It can split a duality that has gained relative independence. The soul does not yet experience the boundary in a collision, because beholding keeps the connection. But if spirit's "lightning" strikes at that moment, own body and alien body separate as bounded, fixed corporealities. This is asserted metaphysics, not argument, and it presupposes spirit's "ready-to-intervene presence". 3. Genealogy of the over-weighting of sensing [957–958]. Two sides are given. On the side of the soul: special senses develop, sight becomes dominant, and elementary sensibility weakens into "spectatorship". On the side of the body: capacity for pain increases. Klages names flight from pain as the chief motive of "Promethean" ideologisms from Plato to Schiller, and he says the "deeper wisdom of Asia" seconds it. 4. The thing as identical [958–962]. The bounded body has, for any now, a determinable place relative to another thing. Judgement presupposes the sameness of what it judges, and so presupposes thingness. Spirit can later see through thingness as a reinterpretation made to render the body thinkable, but it cannot give the reinterpretation up without giving up conceiving. Shape is not the principle of identity. A boundary has no shape (cf. pp. 506–508). Shape can be carried over entirely into visible appearance (mirror-image, perspective drawing), and it is always only a now-shape. The examples are a melting piece of ice, a snowman from its pedestal to its dirty remnant, and the ash of a burned letter that we still identify as "the letter". Animals can recognize through changes of shape, but only humans identify, and there is no limit to identifying. Thingness is "a mere noumenon — though an unavoidable noumenon". At the top of the ladder of abstraction (atoms, ether, the extensionless point) stands "the thing once again — now, however, stripped of its corporeality". 5. Formula of the primordial object [961–962]. Factual primordial object = a position-determined point of relation for an arbitrarily small, but not vanishingly small, point of impenetrability. Impenetrability is the "primordial property" (Ureigenschaft). All other properties are secondary or tertiary. 6. Against Democritus and Aristotle [962–964]. Democritus counted shape (σχῆμα) among the atom's primary properties. Klages answers that impenetrability has no shape, just as impetus has none ("we do not speak of an impact-shape"). Shape flows in from appearance. It is "the one secondary property without whose co-presence the primordial property would sink down into the abyss of mere possibilities". Aristotle would be right about this if he had recognized that matter is independent rather than mere potentiality for immaterial form. Matter as experienced is intensive and gradable (plasticine, fluids, gases). Etymology: weich (soft) from weichen (to yield), hart (hard) cognate with κρατύς. Atomism is a hopeless attempt to quantize the continuity of intensity. The two camps err in opposite directions [962]: the materiologist thinks something can be found without experienced intuitability, the ideologist thinks shapes can be found without things already found. 7. Thing as self-asserting place; I as source of identity [964–967]. Within the now-here, the here bears the primordial object and the now bears the primordial concept. That is why every cult of the concept is paired with a cult of shape. The thing is "a self-asserting place" (ein sichbehauptender Ort). The I is the extensionless pivot of the personal I and the "ever-repeated deed of self-assertion"; since the primal act bounds body from body, the bearer of spirit must itself be corporeal. All vital working is assimilation of like to like, and the likeness that lets spirit set the boundary is not that of soul and image but the like disturbability of the soul-body and the image's body: the impenetrability of both [966]. Spirit forms the primordial object by shooting "a copy of the I-point" into the corporeality of the appearance. The object's fictive sameness is a "reflex of the real sameness of the subject". Early, anthropomorphic thought therefore made persons of objects. As spirit gains power it bores the bare I-point into actuality, to the point where persons can doubt that persons exist. What spirit gains is possession, on the model of the fish on the hook and the bird in the cage, "and every living thing definitively by killing it". Contents of experience are "nailed" onto the spaceless plane of objects of thought: Stat crux dum volvitur orbis. These are wishes for de-temporalization. 8. Existence [967–968]. Ex-sistere means to step forth. The existent is supposed to be both being and actuality, a fusion of timelessness with real time. Klages resolves this for the thing: thingness is fictive and corporeality is actual. For the personal I, the fusion has "in fact taken place" (deferred to ch. 61). 9. Two sub-theses on the existential judgement [968–973]. (a) No object of thought exists without the prior finding of the thing. (b) Doubts about existence are always settled by appeal to things. One's own body plays a double role: bearer of the I, and a thing among things that "becomes the tormentor of the I" in pain. The I-consciousness "kindles" on the consciousness of its self-moving corporeality (cf. ch. 12, 46). On recognition versus identity (cf. ch. 34): Odysseus' dog recognizes by similarity, while the nurse identifies by the scar. Corporeality is the last link in every chain of identification, even in inference to processes on the sun from a deflected magnetic needle. Examples of existential appeals to touch: Heraclitus' common world of the waking is a world of things, not of appearances. Then come the desert mirage, the stick bent in water, converging street walls, and the Zöllner illusion corrected by a ruler. Polemic against relativism: even a contraction hypothesis presupposes the rigid body, and a thought fixed on visible appearance turns its object into "rules of the game".

Key terms. Urakt (primal act of spirit, which sets the boundary). Grenze vs Gestalt (boundary, which has no shape, vs shape/form). Körperding (body-thing). das objektiv Uridentische and sachlicher Urgegenstand (the thing as the first identity). Ureigenschaft (primordial property, a minimal field of impenetrability). Jetztgestalt (now-shape). Selbstbehauptung (self-assertion, both of the thing as place and of the I as act). Ichpunkt (the extensionless I-point). Wiedererkennen vs Identifizieren (animal recognition vs human identification). Existenzialurteil.

Contribution to the whole. This chapter gives a vital ground for the account of the I as self-assertion (ch. 9) and of the finding of the I by way of the thing (ch. 12) and for the willing I in ch. 46. It turns the "doubled here" of ch. 58 into the point where spirit enters. It prepares ch. 60's polemic about matter and ch. 61's derivation of the One and Being from the thing and the I. It also sets the pattern of every later critique of "ideologism" (cult of shape/Idea) paired with "materiologism" (cult of the atom).

Critical note. The analysis of identity through change of shape (snowman, ash) is acute, and so is the case that boundary is not shape. Both are good reasons against Democritean and Platonic identity-by-form. But the core move is a metaphor. Spirit "shoots a copy of the I-point" into the body. The thing's sameness is a "reflex" of the I's. Klages can say both that the I is found by way of one's own body as a thing (968–969) and that the thing's sameness is a reflex of the I's, because he separates the order of finding from the order of grounding. But the grounding claim rests on the I's "real sameness" as an act identically repeated, and that is asserted here (and only defended in ch. 61), not shown. The anti-relativity paragraph proves less than it seems: that measurement presupposes a rod rigid "for an empirical now" is something relativity itself grants, so it tells against no physical theory, only against a "relativism" that Klages simply equates with fixation on visual appearance.

Chapter 60 — Matter and the World of Appearance (Materie und Erscheinungswelt) [974–1000]

Thesis. Matter cannot be derived from the thing or the point. In the terms of the science of life, matter is "the placed-determinateness of the transformation of appearance" (die Ortsbestimmtheit der Erscheinungswandlung), the "receiving-place of images". It stands to the image (happening) as place stands to space, or as the receptive feminine stands to the begetting masculine. "Pure" appearance, as the dream shows, would be placeless and so would lose identity and existence. The original seat of disturbance (Urstörungsstelle), and so the precondition of apprehension and judgement, is the experience of resistance. Its corporeality yields the grammatical subject and its strength yields the predicate.
