Appearance clings to its bearer like a garment, so the killing can be moved (the Sacaea criminal = king = Marduk). 4. Deification vs. demonisation [1408]. Since sacrificer merges with victim and victim with god, sacrifice is the "deification of the human being through his participation in — the demise of the god." If the emphasis falls on deification, a fork opens. Demonisation (of soul) is life's utmost release. Deification (of the person, by spirit) is "the utmost outlawing of life." Christianity made the latter a principle (God as person), and Christian mysticism perfects it. Pelasgian traces of the confusion: the over-tabooed, isolated priest-king; the Mexican god-king swearing to make the sun shine ("pagan superstition"). 5. Going-under as original sense [1408–10]. If the emphasis falls instead on going-under, the original sense is reached. Everyone must sacrifice, because everyone receives life's goods only by giving back (suum cuique). This is not a trade in goods but an "exchange of fluids or essences." In a wholly demonic humanity it would happen without events. Once spirit has a say it happens in surges: the "painfully joyous festival" of giving up what one loves most, made holy by the "heightening of Eros through the necessity of parting." Fullness is unevenly shared. Its bearers (heroes, poets, kings, priests, vestals) give the greatest sacrifice, and their death "weds whole peoples anew to the 'ever-living fire'." The bright side: Pharaoh's circuit "guaranteeing" the sun, as a link between the people's souls and the sidereal images, not physical causation. The warrior's blood-sacrifice (Schiller: "if you do not stake your life..."): "only in going-under lies fulfillment, only in the dying of the divine images the guarantee of their eternity" [1410]. 6. Pagan eternity vs. Christian immortality [1410–12]. The "carrying-out of Death" by women in Central Europe: one kills Death and brings back Life. This is conceptual analysis. Strict unchangeability is "empty sameness" = absolute being = spirit, whose place in the world is the person. Belief in personal immortality fuses timelessness with endless duration. Athanatos/immortalis means "deathless": not that dying can be avoided, but that death can be destroyed. The Isis initiate "has passed through the gates of death" without loss of existence. The reaped ear of Eleusis shows that everything his own passes, while "the primal image of the ear of grain rises up, shining and deathless." "Christian eternity is timelessness and the delusive notion of the endless continuance of the person; pagan eternity is the eternity of the being-less transformation of that which passes away into that which arises" [1412]. 7. Why sacrifice: death as catastrophe [1412–15]. Passing-and-arising (seasons, moon, tides) only distantly resembles dying-and-being-born for a being that "bears the center of its being-alive within itself." For such a being death is the end of its world, never to be experienced again (spring returns, the dead man does not). A demonic being would be "wholly microcosm": its centre would coincide with the centre of the All, and its birth and death would be like the moon's waning and waxing. So Pelasgianism's deepest striving was to "magically transform back the catastrophes of dying and being born into the cosmic rhythm," and one way was sacrificing the god's bodily images. "In the shudder of going-under... death melted away" and the newly arising demon rose. The tree is the chief mediating being. The book's body ends on Odin's self-sacrifice on the windy tree (Hávamál). In "the language of Christendom" the god sacrificed himself and rose, but not "for mankind": "in loosing himself he found the runes of knowledge . ." [1415]. The text breaks off with an ellipsis, as the last line of the main text.

Key terms. Opfer (sacrifice); Hieromanie; Urbild as victim; Epiphanie vs. Stellvertretung (representation); Dämonisierung vs. Vergottung (demonisation vs. deification); Untergang (going-under); suum cuique; Ewigkeit vs. Unsterblichkeit; athanatos (deathless); Mikrokosmos as the demonic ideal.

Contribution to the whole. It completes ch. 73's anticipation (sacrifice of the demon in plant-mask) and ch. 74's "debt of life" and time-circle. It also states, at the level of cult, the book's metaphysics of personhood: spirit's place is the person (ch. 7), personal immortality is spirit's wish, and life's eternity lies in images (chs. 67–69). Ending on the self-sacrifice of a god who finds runes (signs, knowledge) leaves an ambiguity in view: knowledge born of going-under.

Critical note. The distinction between deathlessness (the destruction of death through participation in the image's renewal) and endless personal duration is the most philosophically developed result of Part 2, and it follows from the earlier analysis of "being" as spirit's empty sameness. The weakest step is presupposition (ii). By making the symbol "without restriction" equal to what it symbolises, Klages makes killing a human being and burning a straw figure "no fundamental difference" [1405]. That is consistent with blaming bloody excess on spirit, since the primal sense never requires a real killing. But it also means the primal sense contains no reason against the real killing. Klages puts the victim's lack of compassion into the primal sense itself, through presupposition (i) (rapture) and ch. 73's remark that sacrifice "no more requires" a real killing than it "knows ... mercy" [1312]. So his principle can tell degenerate from genuine sacrifice only by the sacrificer's state of consciousness, never by what is done to the victim.

Retrospect

Retrospect (Rückblick) [1416–1432]

Thesis. Klages's closing self-interpretation. He does not summarise results: the planned "synoptic table of the main results" is "superfluous," partly because Part 2 already pointed back to the main results and partly because he has promised one elsewhere [1416]. He does three things instead. (1) He clears up recurring misunderstandings of the basic thought. (2) He names the main emotional source of resistance to his writings, the demand for a program, and shows it to be a misunderstanding too. (3) He leaves the reader with a question whose answer would show how much of the work's essential content they have made their own [1416].

Argument.

A. The objections, as Klages states them [1416–17]. The adversaries are "especially those who have precisely never read the 'Adversary'." He numbers them: - (1) He calls the disposition to all disvalues "spirit" and to all values "soul," so the opposition is won by definition. He confuses spirit with degenerate late-19th-century rationalism and intellectualism, so the work is a time-bound reaction. Spirit is primarily a synthetic, not merely analytic, power. - (1a) The follow-on claims: only spirit can repair the harm done by its misuse; Kantian or Catholic positions aiming at "spirit = God"; and he is an incurable Romantic laudator temporis acti. (Klages answers "spirit = God" with objection 1 and the laudator charge with objection 2.) - (2) Is understanding, will and reason to be abolished? That is senseless, since no one can choose it, and if one could it would be barbarism. - (3) Personal self-contradiction: he uses the spirit he rejects. - (3a) Logical self-contradiction: intellect fights intellect with dialectical subtleties.

B. Replies to 3 and 3a [1417–20]. 1. On (3): to possess a property and hold it valuable are different things. People suffer from their own traits, and since Nietzsche we know a thinker's driving forces and the values of his work may agree or conflict. An author's traits never count against his work. They matter only for explaining errors found by examining the work itself (note 123 cites his Nietzsche studies and graphological essays). "Conflicts of properties are one thing entirely; self-contradictions are quite another!" [1418]. 2. On (3a), which would be fatal if valid. Klages sets out the alleged syllogism: refuting is judging, judging needs spirit, denying spirit's value denies the value of judgment, so judgment denies its own value, which is absurd. "No, that is not absurd." Anyone who thinks so shows a hidden, unnecessary "belief in the sanctity of the spirit." His recap of the work: (a) there is a spirit-dependent and a life-dependent thinking, the same faculty of judgment moved by different vital impulses. (b) At the highest level of reflection, the only form of spirit's life-dependence still possible is logic, or objectivity ("two 'views' of one and the same state of affairs"), and truths are found only by it (he cites chs. 11, 14, 47 by title). (c) "Truth is never actuality." We can know and judge that being woven into actuality is "incomparably more perfect" than seeking truth. Finding truths relates to experiencing actuality "as lack relates to fullness," not as life-denial to life-affirmation. "Only truth instead of actuality" is "the least... price" for the stance of objective judgment, which irruption of spirit made possible and which excludes experiencing [1418–19]. Note 124 gives the exact formula: judgment does not deny its capacity to judge but the value of the judging state relative to the judgment-free experiencing state. 3. On "irrationalism" [1419–20]. The label is ambiguous. If rationalism means the cult of understanding or reason, the work rejects it. If irrationalism means disempowering objectivity (logic) in favour of subjectivism or relativism, which is the current sense, the work is rationalist and opposes that irrationalism as "a last and worst trump card of — the spirit!" Spirit does not create logic by itself; it creates it under the compulsion of experiencing actuality. Holding thinking to be a state of lack that arises from a disturbance of life is entirely different from despising truth in thinking. So the believers in spirit's sanctity are the ones who readily "sacrifice the intellect."

C. Reply to objection 1 [1420–21]. 1. In Klages's simile, intellect, as strictly objective judgment, is an "intermediate realm" where spirit asserts itself only "thanks to considerable concessions made to life" (Promethean phase). Further toward spirit lies the will, which uses logic but not "out of logic" or "for the sake of knowing." 2. "Our work finds the key to the essence of the spirit not in the intellect, but in the will." As spirit's self-sovereignty (= "the positive nothing") grows, objectivity gives way and thought serves will (Herakleian phase). The freed will coincides with "absolute arbitrariness" (Willkür). So spirit as such is "deed 'in itself'," and in the human organism it is "the constitutional center of deeds." With that, objection 1 collapses: the work is not a mistaking of an ephemeral cult of understanding for metaphysics. Without repeating the proofs: the deed in itself can only "destroy (= dissolve = analyze)," more precisely "cleave apart," and in judgment only the capacity for distinguishing attests spirit [1420]. This answers the "synthetic potency" claim. 3. The name. Names are not "sound and smoke." After 150 years of usage, swapping "soul" and "spirit" would be like calling the sun moon (he cites R. Hildebrand's Geist article in Grimm's dictionary, and Hegel's Phenomenology of Spirit). Since the dispute is about the matter, he proposes calling the centre of deeds X and the soul Y (the soul/life difference set aside). Then the reader should ask: has the author shown (i) that X and Y both exist; (ii) that they are unmistakably distinct; (iii) that X cannot be reduced to Y, nor Y to X, nor both to some Z; (iv) that X's existence has life-threatening consequences for Y? He has waited "some thirty years" for a disproof, challenging the most significant contemporaries he met. A future thinker might reduce one to another, but any critic of the title thesis must supply one of these proofs; "enthusiastic invocations made 'in the name' of the spirit" achieve nothing [1421]. These four questions are the clearest statement of the logical structure he claims for the book. 4. Spirit = God: he "approve[s] of it in the most decided way," but disputes the role commonly given to the spirit-god toward the universe.

D. The craving for a program [1421–23]. 1. Readers expect every metaphysics to come with a program and turn hostile when its insights imply that programs are futile. "We bring well-founded results of research, nothing more." Rejecting a philosophy because it implies mankind's imminent downfall is like faulting a doctor for predicting the patient will die tonight. His standing reservation of the "miracle" of unforeseeable rebirths weighs more in a system for which fate is "strictly beyond reason," but the present state of "world history" shows only facts that "demand the worst prognosis." 2. A diagnosis of the age: the craving for programs shows an overvaluation of the "thought-thing, the future," flight from the present, joylessness, and an overemphasis on will. The belief in a coming human omnipotence is the wish-image of extreme impotence and "one of the strongest symptoms of the approaching end." The average man is a "monist" who cannot conceive "a metaphysical misfortune" with a "fatal outcome for an entire planet" [1422–23].

E. The "turning-about" (Umkehr), answering 1a and 2 [1423–29]. 1. Questions such as "how to eliminate mind, neutralise it, subordinate it again, return to the past" are not his task. One cannot do any of these, and no return is possible: "nothing which has ever happened is ever repeated" [1423–24]. But someone deeply imbued with knowledge of "mind's struggle against the tragic divinity of life" would live differently, and so would mankind if its leading strata were. No program is needed, any more than one is needed to flee what one abhors or to protect what one loves. Guidelines are worth following only for one to whom they are "wishes of his heart." "Just as there is certainly no return, so there could be — a turning-about" [1424]. 2. Sketch of the turned man: no future-thinking beyond the next day; no work for work's sake, only enough for leisure; one ambition, to do everything "as perfectly as possible." Moments of great experience cannot be willed, but "every perfectly accomplished piece of work bestows a minute of happiness... the only one which man was granted the power to bring about" (house-building, boot-soling, dust-wiping), and in the humblest handiwork "kindred forms from every past bloom and glow" [1424–25]. 3. Wishes can have effects in the direction of fulfilment even when the wish cannot be fulfilled. Example: the wish to abolish death has produced hygiene, antisepsis and longer life spans (whether this is "metaphysically gratifying" is left open). So a turning-about could weaken mind's efficacy, by strengthening life or by withdrawing ground from mind here and there. Mind's absence is not even impossible. Plants and animals, and probably primal man, live without it, so a future mankind "could once again be rid of mind," though never as it was before. But unlike death, no discoveries or measures can be aimed at life's vulnerability to mind (if there were any they would be magic "yet to be invented"). The disposition can only hope that gradual changes of vitality will accompany its "doing and refraining" [1425–27]. 4. Mind-turned disposition = action, deed, conquest. Mind-averted disposition = refraining (Unterlassung) and defence. Many deeds would simply not happen if millions did not want more than their neighbour, a "public name," dependants, a fortune for some collective (up to "mankind," Nobel prizes), or if no people wanted "world domination." His example of defensive willing: in wartime "Neutralia" (Neutralien, his veiled name for a neutral country) centuries-old walnut trees were sold off for rifle butts. A turned mankind would refuse from love of "natural monuments," without any belief in "sacred trees" [1427–28]. 5. He admits he has "left the ground" of his competence. The sketch is no program. It refutes the charge of demanding the abolition of understanding by appeal to intuition: such a canon would weaken the will to deeds and strengthen the will to work. If someone calls it utopian, since such people are perhaps one in millions, Klages agrees: this is "one of the strongest supports of our conviction of the inevitability of the downfall." Everything here concerns voluntary behaviour only. What serves life from life itself, "Eros, ancestor-cult, and festival," lies outside the will's reach and would be harmed by any intervention [1428–29]. He does not believe the book will bring any of this about.

F. What the work is, and what it achieved [1429–32]. 1. Deep and shallow reflection agree that their findings prompt further research and kindle only the person for whom fulfilment means "knowledge independent of person." Metaphysical thinking keeps "truth" for insights into "traits of character of actuality," and consists finally of demonstrations, "makings-present of 'primal phenomena'." So it necessarily takes the form of a world-view doctrine, a "world-picture." Immersion in it can give "a specific happiness," a "sheet-lightning gleam" that lights up meaning even in the "dreariest everyday." That would be the work's strongest and highest effect [1429]. 2. What remained unfound. A great part of the research located the soul's points of "least resistance" to mind. But he has not explained how "the first eruption of mind could have taken place" millennia ago in some human group. This does not sink the system: proving that something exists suffers nothing from its being inexplicable "in principle," let alone for now. It is a "formidable problem" for future metaphysicians (cf. ch. 70) [1429–30]. 3. What he considers achieved: the closing question [1430–32]. Religion scholars, myth researchers and ethnologists now grant the Pelasgian consciousness a "basic legitimacy," even equal value. But none has decided whether magism, the reverse side of symbolism, also has basic legitimacy apart from its degenerations. Subjectivist escapes ("the unconscious," suggestion, epistemological constructions of the outer world) are "barred." The question is: "was the weather... ever actually changed by magic... and can such a thing happen or not?" No one has posed it definitely, because of the prior question of whether that would "overthrow" the laws of physics, which are proved in technology. His answer: physical laws need not hold for every distance or every past. Suppose the same laws held for hundreds of thousands of years and weather-magic is impossible now. Even so, "we believe we have, for the first time, disclosed why... a magical participation in the events of the terrestrial universe might once have been possible — because it was not so much the actuality of the inner as rather actuality itself that was other than it is today" [1431–32]. More generally, he believes he has shown "that and why the most consequential transformations of telluric actuality cannot be discovered by the research methods of physics, and therefore exert no influence either on the shaping of 'physical laws'." Last sentence: "Whoever among our readers is able to substantiate this has appropriated to himself the best and deepest of the content of our work" [1432].

Key terms. Gesamtübersicht der Hauptergebnisse (the abandoned table); spirit as Tat an sich (deed in itself), konstitutionelles Zentrum der Taten, das positive Nichts; Willkür; X/Y test; Objektivität = Logik; Wahrheit vs. Wirklichkeit (truth as "lack" relative to actuality); Irrationalismus (two senses); Programm; Umkehr (turning-about) vs. Rückkehr (return); Tun und Lassen, Unterlassung; Wille zur Tat vs. Wille zum Werk; Wunder; Magismus; tellurische Wirklichkeit.

Contribution to the whole. In Klages's own words, the Retrospect fixes the structure of the book's claim: (i) spirit's essence is will, not intellect (Book IV); (ii) logic is spirit in its last life-dependent form (Book II); (iii) truth is a lack relative to actuality (Books I and III); (iv) the four-part X/Y thesis (existence, distinctness, irreducibility, life-threat) is the title's content; (v) the downfall prognosis is a finding, not a mood; (vi) the doctrine of the actuality of images (Book V) is crowned by the claim that actuality itself changes historically, in ways physics cannot register. That claim is his explanation of how Pelasgian magic could once have worked. The admitted gap (the origin of spirit) is restated from ch. 70.

Critical note. The reply to the self-refutation charge is precise and defensible. Judgment ranks the judging state below the experiencing state without denying its own capacity, so no performative contradiction arises, though the evaluative standpoint from which the ranking is made remains unexamined. The X/Y test usefully shifts the burden of proof, but it hides the fact that (iv) is a historical-evaluative claim, not one of the same logical type as (i)–(iii). The final thesis is the most exposed. A historically variable "actuality itself" whose transformations physics cannot detect is, by construction, beyond any empirical check. It is the strongest statement of the doctrine of images and also the point where it stops being falsifiable.

Arc of Ch. 70–75 and Retrospect

The block moves from the last word of the doctrine to its illustration and then to the author's self-interpretation. Ch. 70 closes Book V's theory by admitting its limit: spirit's first entry cannot be explained. It also gives the historical shape of spirit's work (the Promethean, then the accelerating Herakleian phase) and the anti-reductive argument that consciousness requires spirit. Chs. 71–75 then turn, by Klages's own description, to making the doctrine of the actuality of images "intuitable" through prehistory. The order is close to a phenomenological ascent. Ch. 71 starts from the reader's own experience of being gripped and from poetry. Ch. 72 sets out the forms of image-worship and magic. Ch. 73 uncovers the elemental symbols and their polar logic. Ch. 74 reaches the "middle" of that logic, the Magna Mater, together with her time-form and her natural right. Ch. 75 shows sacrifice as the cultic act that sums up the whole: images offered to themselves in a going-under that turns death back into rhythm.

The block relies throughout on earlier results: elementary similarity (ch. 34), symbolic vs. conceptual thinking (ch. 36), the soul's wedding with the image and the lowering of the I in ecstasy (Book III), the will as spirit's key (Book IV), the word-soul (ch. 66), actuality as images (ch. 67), coincidentia oppositorum (ch. 68), and primal images vs. phantoms (ch. 69). Its main new conceptual gains are three. The idol/symbol pair is treated as body and soul of one phenomenon. The middle that unites the poles is identified with the Mother. And renewal is distinguished from repetition (circular time vs. eternal recurrence). From that last distinction come both the jus naturale (life as fief, talion as healing) and the contrast between pagan deathlessness and Christian personal immortality. The book's quarrel with Nietzsche peaks here, over will to power, the Genealogy's creditor model and eternal recurrence, with Klages claiming Nietzsche's insights against his "belief in the vital significance of the 'will to power'."

The Retrospect reorders all of this. It is not a summary but a defence and a challenge. In Klages's own formulation, the essential claims are that spirit is will (deed-in-itself, which can only split), that logic is its last life-dependent form, that truth is a lack relative to actuality, and that a four-part X/Y thesis is what the title asserts. Its last word does not return to soul and spirit. It returns to the Pelasgian part: the "best and deepest" content is the thesis that actuality itself, not only inner experience, has changed, so that magic may once have worked without contradicting today's physics. This makes Part 2 not decorative but the place where the doctrine of images claims its strongest ontological consequence.

Several tensions remain. (1) Klages warns repeatedly against reading social or physical facts off symbols (against Bachofen's gynaecocracy; "whoever thinks in symbols does not think about symbols"; the analysis "tells us nothing about how a symbol arises"). Yet he draws institutional conclusions from symbols (no incest taboo, no property, boundless Eros) and finally an ontological one (actuality was other). (2) Spirit is a non-spatial nothing that cannot appear, yet its effect is placed ethnically and historically ("Indo-Germans and Semites"; Judaism as particularised Herakleism; "high finance"). These are polemical assertions, borrowed largely from Nietzsche and Bachofen, that the metaphysics does not support and arguably contradicts. (3) In ch. 75 the equivalence of symbol and symbolized makes real and symbolic killing metaphysically indifferent. This lets Klages blame bloody "excess" on spirit-induced fear, but it leaves the primal sense of sacrifice without any ground for sparing the victim. (4) The Retrospect rejects any "program," yet sketches a quietist ethic of refraining and perfect work. It is offered as illustration only, but it stands in some tension with the strict fatalism of the downfall prognosis, which the reserved "miracle" only partly relieves.

Open questions passed forward, as Klages himself frames them: the origin of spirit's first eruption; a "phenomenological science of the body" (left and right, the body's gates, blood-symbolism) that he repeatedly defers; and the empirical-metaphysical question of whether magic ever worked and how a change in "telluric actuality" could escape physics.

Part Two — The Work as a Whole: A Philosophical Analysis

1. What the book claims, in brief

Der Geist als Widersacher der Seele argues for four theses. Klages himself reduces them to a test in the Retrospect [1420–21]: replace "spirit" with X and "soul" with Y, and ask whether he has shown each of the following. 1. Existence. Both X and Y exist. 2. Distinctness. They are unmistakably distinct. 3. Irreducibility. Neither can be derived from the other, nor both from a third, Z. 4. Hostility. The existence of X threatens the life of Y.

The book is his attempt to meet this test. Y is the soul, the pole of a living cell whose other pole is the body. It receives a world that is not made of things but of images (Bilder): appearances that are alive, temporal, and charged with distance and the past. X is spirit (Geist): an atemporal power without location that cuts this flowing actuality into identical, countable points (objects, things, the I). Its deepest nature is not intellect but will. Truth, number, law, cause, the persisting self, the thing, measurable time, history and technology are all products of that cutting. The soul experiences; spirit apprehends and wills. Spirit can live only off life, and it gradually destroys it. The process has a historical shape, from a "Promethean" phase of knowledge still bound to images to a "Heraclean" phase of will set free. Klages's prognosis is downfall. The prehistoric "Pelasgian" world-picture of the last part is meant to show what human consciousness was like when images, not things, counted as actual.

2. The architecture: how the argument is built

Read as a single argument, the five books form a clear sequence, even though Klages's digressions often hide it.

Book I (Being and Actuality) sets up the ontology from the barest starting point: the durationless Now. If the apprehending act is punctual, the faculty behind it and its objects are outside time and space (Ch. 1–3). Then motion, which is the archetype of change, has no being (Zeno is right, Ch. 4). Klages's founding move is to reverse the Eleatic valuation (Ch. 5). What does not fit the concept is not unreal; what is unreal is the concept's object, Being, when taken for actuality. Phenomenon and noumenon, Plato, Kant and mechanism are all diagnosed as expressions of one hostility to the senses (Ch. 6). Then the person is split in two (Ch. 7–9). Spirit is the faculty of points and constitutes the persisting I. Life, or the soul, knows the continuous by experiencing (Erleben). And the person is the site of an irreconcilable rift, because spirit can be actual in a living being only as self-assertion, which is to say as will.

Book II (Spirit and Life) defends this ontology against the theories that would absorb the given into the knowing subject. Against logistic idealism (Ch. 10–14), Klages argues that meaning is not conceiving and that a concept grasps only a direction while pointing to (hinweisen) an occasion it cannot grasp. Subject–object opposition is a vital polarity, not an achievement of spirit, and truth relates to actuality as a musical score relates to music. Against Kant (Ch. 15–17), he argues that a priori space is really the geometer's space-object, not lived side-by-sideness, and he introduces the central positive term Schauen (beholding): deedless, temporal receptivity to a stream of images. Against sensualism (Ch. 18–20), he argues that sensations are abstractions, really properties of things smuggled into experience. His "Copernican reversal" holds that what is given is the cross-modal intuitive image (Anschauungsbild), the appearance of a living essence (Wesen). Ch. 21–23 supply the architecture: one soul, one image-space, the senses as "messengers of the soul." Beholding (the soul side, present even in sleep) fuses with sensing (the body side, the ground of waking and of the image's corporeality), and spirit fixes the result into a thing.

Book III (Consciousness and Experience) relocates consciousness. Lived experience as such is unconscious. Consciousness is a gappy series of point-like acts of reflection, triggered by disturbances in the life-stream, which Klages calls its "gradient" (Ch. 24–28). Only a living, polar process is actual; a mechanical process is an intransitive It-process. Historical self-knowledge comes posthumously. The second and third parts of Book III (Ch. 29–40) give the positive mechanism. - Waves and turning-points. Experience runs in waves. At each wave's turning-point, beholding passes into a "vital mirroring" (vitale Spiegelung) of what has just been lived, and this turning-point is where spirit's timeless act takes hold (Ch. 29, 32). - Sense-space. Sense-space is never past and never changing. It turns out to be the "actual now" that Book I could not find, and objective time is merely spatialised time (Ch. 31). - Two processes of sensibility. Sensibility splits into two processes: making-present on the soul's side, which gives space and actuality, and embodiment on the body's side, which gives resistance and existence (Ch. 33). - Elementary similarity. Elementary similarity binds the images of each wave into one present image and grounds recognition, familiarity and instinct. Animals recognise; only a being with self-reflection remembers (Ch. 34). - Change of poles. Man differs from the animal by a "change of poles": beholding is freed from sensation, and the sign is that no animal draws (Ch. 35). - Symbol and concept. Symbolic thinking (identity of essence) underlies conceptual thinking (identity of matter), and concepts arise by dissecting symbolic units (Ch. 36). - Spirit and the I. Spirit adds only boundaries; the first act posits "there is something," and the I is "a rule-governed narrowing of life" (Ch. 38). - Fatality of selfhood. Selfhood is a fate that consumes life, and every gain in grasp is paid for in lost bliss (Ch. 39).

Book III ends by honouring and correcting Palágyi, Klages's most important contemporary ally (Ch. 40).

Book IV (The Doctrine of the Will) is the decisive turn (see §4.2). The single timeless act of spirit is apprehension when it splits the impression side of life, and will when it splits the impulse side (Ch. 41). Necessity, which belongs to living happening, is distinguished from lawfulness, which is spirit's command-form (Ch. 42). Drive is a pull from non-present images joined to bodily lack (Ch. 45). Volition strips the drive-impulse of its quality and keeps its pull, which it then directs (Ch. 46). From this Klages derives volo ergo sum, the judgment as a hidden prohibition, error as interest acting on a weakened sense of actuality, and the self-contradiction of the will to truth (Ch. 46–47). Volition is a counter-urge against the present. It generates linear, measurable time and memory that depends on the future (Ch. 48). The will steers by inhibiting; it does not drive. Finitude, number, the straight line, the thing, law and cause are all traced back to purpose-consciousness (Ch. 49). Physics then appears as projected will. Force is will without its vital carrier, attributed to points that are mere things; cause is generalised force; energy conservation is never violated because the will adds no energy (Ch. 50–52). The book closes with a philosophy of history (Ch. 53) and a typology of the ancient and modern researcher, the reader of oracles against the examining magistrate (Ch. 54).

Book V, Part One (The Actuality of Images: The Doctrine) gives the positive counterpart, and here the book changes register. - Spirit and Soul (Ch. 55–57). Dreams have every sensory quality but no bodily pain, so sleep is beholding without sensing. Spirit therefore enters through the sensing body, not the beholding soul. Beholding is defined as receptivity to distance (Ferne). The prehistory of this doctrine runs from Heraclitus to Schuler. - Soul and Body (Ch. 58–61). This section is a vital genealogy of formal ontology. Sensation gives resistance and a "doubled here." Spirit strikes there and makes the thing, a "self-asserting place" whose sameness copies the sameness of the I. Matter is the "place-determinacy of the transformation of appearance." Unity, the One and Being are traced back to the thing and the I, and Klages ends with "spirit = being = positive nothing." - Nearness and Distance (Ch. 62–65). Movement is at bottom a pathic being-moved. Perception of movement presupposes the felt body and a pulse of moments of making-present ("actuality-time pulses"). The plant is "sleeping beholding." The elemental souls are argued for transcendentally: if intuition is participation, then happening must be alive. - The Efficacy of Images (Ch. 66–70). Connection (Zusammenhang: real, reciprocal, experienced) is set against relation (Beziehung: conceptual, arising only where connection is cut). Names are evocative and there is no thinking without signs. Actuality is a singulare tantum, graded by depth and distance. The coincidentia oppositorum holds across persons, the cosmos and time. Primal images (Urbilder) are set against phantoms as the metaphysical causes of history. Ch. 70, "The Achilles' Heel," concedes that spirit's first entry into life cannot be explained.

Book V, Part Two (The World-Picture of Pelasgianism) (Ch. 71–75) makes the doctrine intuitable through prehistory: poetry and the experience of being gripped, image-worship and magic, the elemental symbols, the Magna Mater and circular time as renewal (against Nietzsche's recurrence as repetition), natural right, and sacrifice. The Retrospect answers critics, refuses to offer a program, and names as the book's deepest result the thesis that actuality itself, not just inner experience, has changed in history.

3. How the parts hang together: the narrative of the whole

Section 2 described the books one at a time. This section shows why they come in this order and how each depends on the others. Read as a whole, Der Geist als Widersacher der Seele tells one continuous story. It starts from the bare form of a thought, works backward through the conditions that make thought possible, and ends in a world before thought took charge.

3.1 One question, pursued from its most certain end

The Introduction sets the programme. It promises distinguishing marks that settle, for any fact of mental life, what belongs to body, what to soul and what to spirit [7]. It also states the thesis in a single image: spirit is a wedge driven into the life-cell. The book then has to make good on both. It must identify spirit precisely enough to tell it apart from everything living, and it must show that spirit works against the life it lives in.

Klages's order of inquiry follows from a methodological choice. He begins where spirit is most certain of itself and where his opponents are strongest, in logic, in the form of the object and in the concept. Only then does he move toward what spirit cannot grasp. He starts by granting rationalism every proof it can produce: the object is timeless, the identity principle holds, a continuum cannot be built from points. What those proofs establish, he argues, is the shape of an act, not the shape of the world. Each later book asks what that act presupposes. The book therefore advances by regress to conditions, one layer per book:

| Stage | What has been established | What it presupposes, and so the next question | Answered in | |---|---|---|---| | Book I | Apprehension is a timeless act that grasps only points: now-points, thing-points, the I-point. Being belongs to the act, actuality to flowing happening. | We undeniably know the continuous. By what? (Ch. 8 names it "experiencing" but cannot yet say what that is.) | Book II | | Book II | Concepts only point toward an experiential occasion they cannot grasp (Ch. 11). The given is not sensation but the image, received by beholding (Ch. 17, 20). Spirit fixes images into things (Ch. 23). | If experience is a stream of images, and spirit fixes points in it, where and when does spirit get hold of the stream? And what is the "consciousness" in which all this supposedly happens? | Book III | | Book III | Experience as such is unconscious. Consciousness is an intermittent disturbance (Ch. 24–28). Spirit takes hold at the turning-points of the waves of life, where beholding passes into mirroring (Ch. 29–32). Concept, recognition and the I are built on that foothold (Ch. 33–39). | So far the "act" has been treated as neutral cutting. What drives the cutting? Why does spirit cut at all, and why against life? | Book IV | | Book IV | The one act is will. It splits the impulse side of life just as apprehension splits the impression side (Ch. 41). Number, line, thing, law and cause derive from purpose (Ch. 49). Physics is projected will (Ch. 50–52), and history is the will's advance (Ch. 53). | So far, what spirit attacks has appeared only as its victim, defined negatively. What is that living actuality in itself, and exactly where does spirit enter it? | Book V, Part One | | Book V, Part One | Spirit enters through the sensing body (Ch. 55). Beholding is receptivity to distance (Ch. 56). The here and the thing are derived from resistance (Ch. 58–61). Images are alive, of distance and of the past, and they act on one another through connection (Ch. 62–69). The point of spirit's entry cannot be explained (Ch. 70). | All of this has been shown from inside a consciousness, ours, in which images are already dimmed. Was there ever a consciousness for which images, not things, were actual? | Book V, Part Two | | Book V, Part Two and Retrospect | The Pelasgian world-picture: image-worship, elemental symbols, the Mother, circular time, sacrifice (Ch. 71–75). In the Retrospect, the deepest result: actuality itself has changed, not only our access to it [1431–32]. | (none) | (none) |

Read in reverse, the same table gives the order of being as Klages sees it. First there are living images and the polar life-cell that receives them. Then spirit breaks into the cell at the sensing body. Spirit is at bottom will, and its acts produce intermittent consciousness. Consciousness fixes images into concepts and things. Finally the system of things is taken for Being itself. The book therefore runs from effect to cause. It starts with the last product, the concept of Being, and digs down to the first condition, a world of images. The Retrospect closes the circle by making that first condition historical: the world of images was not merely misdescribed but has itself grown poorer. This is why the book cannot end on soul and spirit and must end on Pelasgian magic.

3.2 The three volumes as three questions about spirit

The original three-volume division mirrors this regress. - **Volume I (Books I–III): what spirit does.** It cuts time into now-points and the image into things, it turns the polarity of experiencing and experienced into a subject–object opposition, and it interrupts the stream of life as consciousness. This is spirit observed in its products, which is why the volume is an epistemology and a phenomenology. - **Volume II (Book IV): what spirit is.* Spirit is will: a deed "in itself" that can only split and inhibit. The products described in Volume I are traced back to their producer. (The Retrospect confirms that this is the key: "our work finds the key to the essence of the spirit not in the intellect, but in the will" [1422].) - *Volume III (Book V): what spirit attacks, and what was there before it.** This is the actuality of images, first as doctrine (Part One) and then as a lived world-picture (Part Two).

The volumes therefore move from function to essence to victim. The title's three terms map onto them: Geist is defined across Volumes I–II, Seele is given positive content in Volume III, and Widersacher, the relation of hostility, is the thread running through all three.

3.3 Four threads that run through the whole

The connection is not only between books. Four problems are introduced early, left open on purpose, and taken up again at fixed points, so that each book adds one turn to each thread.

(a) Time, from the Now to circular renewal. - Ch. 1–3: the Now is the durationless cut of the act. Real time cannot be apprehended and flows toward us. "Duration of time" is a paralogism. An unexplained "proto-concrete X" grounds the uniqueness of the actual Now (Ch. 2). - Ch. 8: that X is named. It is life, experiencing. - Ch. 31: sense-space, which is never past and never changing, turns out to be the "actual now" that Book I could not find. Objective time is spatialised time. - Ch. 48: linear, measurable time, the detached Now, and memory that serves the future are generated by the will's turn against the present. - Ch. 56: "time is the soul of space"; beholding is receptivity to distance, and distance includes the past. - Ch. 63: "actuality-time pulses." - Ch. 65, 67: the elemental souls are souls of the past, and actuality is graded by depth. - Ch. 74: circular time as renewal, against Nietzsche's recurrence as repetition.

The thread runs from time as something only negatively accessible (Book I) to time as the substance of the soul and of the world (Book V). Book IV is the hinge, because it explains why we ordinarily experience time as a line: the will made it one.

(b) The I, from impossible existence to positive nothing. - Ch. 7: the persisting I is "in truth an impossible state of affairs" [63], yet it is the one concept that includes its own existence. - Ch. 13: the actuality of the I is "the central problem of metaphysics" [120]. - Ch. 38–39: the I is "a rule-governed narrowing of life," and selfhood is a fate. - Ch. 46: volo ergo sum. The I first becomes aware of itself in willing. - Ch. 59: the sameness of things is a projected copy of the sameness of the I. - Ch. 61: spirit = being = "positive nothing." - Ch. 70: how spirit, and with it the I, first entered life cannot be explained.

This thread carries the book's central difficulty. Each station explains more of what the I does, and less of how it can be at all. It is the most honest thread in the book, because Klages ends it with an admission (Ch. 70; Retrospect [1430]).

(c) The image, from pole of actuality to cosmic power. - Ch. 8: life and image are the two poles of actuality, at first only a name. - Ch. 17, 20–23: beholding, the intuitive image, and essence (Wesen) with its primal image (Urbild). - Ch. 29–34: mirroring and elementary similarity, the mechanism of image-reception. - Ch. 55–56: beholding without sensing in dreams, and the image as an appearance of distance. - Ch. 64–65: the plant as sleeping beholding, and the elemental souls as images not yet released into appearance. - Ch. 66–69: connection and efficacy; primal images and phantoms as the causes of history. - Ch. 71–75: the image as the object of worship, magic and sacrifice.

The image grows from an epistemic category (what is given) to an ontological one (what is real) and then to a historical one (what moves history). §4.5 below describes this shift.

(d) Spirit's point of attack, from "somewhere in experience" to the sensing body. - Introduction: a wedge between body and soul. - Ch. 9: spirit becomes actual as self-assertion against sensuous Eros. - Ch. 25–27: disturbance and the "gradient" of the life-stream trigger reflection. - Ch. 29, 32: the foothold is the turning-point of each wave of life. - Ch. 41, 46: on the impulse side, the foothold is the drive's pull. - Ch. 53: a "vulnerable spot" is announced and deferred to Volume III (most likely Ch. 70, "The Achilles' Heel"). - Ch. 55: spirit enters through the sensing body, not the beholding soul. - Ch. 58–60: the exact site is the "doubled here" of resistance, the Urstörungsstelle (site of primal disturbance).

The thread narrows step by step until it can be located in the body. That is why the book's derivation of the thing (Ch. 58–59) can count as the completion of Book I's critique of the thing.

3.4 The book's mirror structure

The book has a second kind of unity besides the forward regress. Books I–IV are the negative half, an account of spirit and its products. Book V, Part One is the positive half. Several topics of the negative half are taken up again in the positive half, now treated from the side of life. The pairings are this guide's reading, but each rests on explicit cross-references in the text.

| Negative half: spirit's version | Positive half: life's version | |---|---| | Book I: the Now derived from the timeless act; the thing as an abstract point (Ch. 1–4) | Ch. 58–61: the Here derived from resistance; the thing as a self-asserting place; Being as "positive nothing." Ch. 58 states that it does for the here what Book I did for the now. | | Book II: the given is the image, against Kant and the sensualists (Ch. 15–23) | Ch. 62–69: the image is alive and efficacious, with elemental souls, connection rather than relation, and primal images | | Book III: consciousness as a disturbance; mirroring at the turning-point (Ch. 24–34) | Ch. 55–56: sleep and waking; beholding against conceiving wakefulness; spirit's entry through the body | | Book IV: the will's history, from the Promethean to the Heraclean phase (Ch. 53) | Ch. 57, 69–75: the history of images, from the prehistory of the discovery of images and the theory of telluric turns to the Pelasgian world before the will |

Two consequences follow. First, the two halves together supply both coordinates of the object-world. The thing is a now-point (Book I) fixed to a self-asserting here (Ch. 58–59), and both are traced to spirit's intervention: on the beholding side, in time; on the sensing side, in space. Second, the critique in the negative half is always pending until the positive half pays it off. Many chapters in Books I–IV explicitly defer their positive ground. §3.6 below lists the most important deferrals and where they are settled.

3.5 Why the method changes, and why that is part of the design

The move from argument to pointing (§4.6 below) can look like a loss of rigour. In fact it follows from the structure. Book II, Ch. 11 already distinguishes conceiving thought, which grasps directions, from indicating thought (hinweisendes Denken), which points to what cannot be conceived. Ch. 14 assigns the first to "shallow reflection" (science) and the second to "deep reflection" (metaphysics, working through the content of names). So the book announces at the outset that its method must change as its subject changes. As long as the topic is spirit and its products (Books I–IV), conceptual analysis is adequate to it, because like is known by like. Once the topic is life itself (Book V), concepts can only point. Klages admits in Ch. 61 that the soul–body chapters still use "shallow reflection." From Ch. 62 onward he turns to series of examples, language, poetry and myth, and in Part Two to the symbols of a pre-conceptual consciousness. The book thus enacts its thesis. The further it gets from spirit, the less spirit's method can do.

The cost of this design is set out in §4.6 and §5. The point here is only that the change is deliberate, not a collapse.

3.6 Deferred debts: where the book pays them, and where it does not

The book's unity also shows in its bookkeeping. Here are the major promises and where they are settled, according to the guide's chapter entries: - Ch. 8: the content of "experiencing" → Ch. 17 (beholding), Ch. 20 (the image), Ch. 29–34 (mirroring, similarity) - Ch. 9: the will as self-assertion → Book IV, Ch. 41–49 - Ch. 13: error as a "dislocation of lived experience" → Ch. 46–47 (error as interest working on a weakened sense of actuality) - Ch. 13: the actuality of the I → Ch. 38–39, 46, 59, 61 - Ch. 13: critique of formalism and logistic → Ch. 38 - Ch. 16: the relation of dream to perception → Ch. 33, 55 - Ch. 18, 22: the life-function of sensing → Ch. 55, 58 - Ch. 23: kinship of qualities across the senses → Ch. 34 (elementary similarity) - Ch. 23: the place as the vital occasion of thinghood → Ch. 58–59 - Ch. 25: the boundary-quality of experience → Ch. 41, 46 - Ch. 28: access to the past other than by research → Ch. 29, 67, 71–75 - Ch. 36–37: the concept of cause → Ch. 51 - Ch. 38: the One → Ch. 61 - Ch. 40, on Palágyi → Ch. 52 ("Galilei and Palágyi"), Ch. 62 (virtual movement) - Ch. 53: the "vulnerable spot" → most likely Ch. 70 ("The Achilles' Heel"), inferred from the table of contents - Ch. 62: how movement is perceived → Ch. 63

Some debts the book explicitly does not pay: - how spirit first entered life ("an insoluble riddle," Ch. 39 [443]; Ch. 70; Retrospect [1430]); - a criterion for "true dreaming" (Ch. 40); - a phenomenological science of the body (left and right, the body's gates, blood-symbolism), repeatedly deferred beyond the book; - the research programmes of Ch. 66–69 (homologous series of polarities, a characterology of the elemental souls, a historiography based on images).

The unpaid debts are not scattered. They all sit at one point, the junction of spirit and life. Every debt about what life is gets paid. Every debt about how spirit comes to act on life stays open. That is the narrative counterpart of the logical point made in §4.9: the book can describe both poles, but not the dualism between them.

3.7 The narrative in one paragraph

The book begins by showing that the objects of thought are timeless points and that the actual is a flowing happening that thought cannot hold (Book I). It then shows that what we receive is not sensations or things but images, which concepts can only point to (Book II). It shows that experiencing those images is unconscious, and that consciousness arises when spirit breaks into the rhythm of experience at its turning-points (Book III). It shows that what breaks in is at bottom will, whose purposes produce number, line, thing, law, cause, measurable time, physics and history (Book IV). Then the book turns around and describes the life spirit breaks into. Spirit enters by the sensing body. The soul is receptivity to distance and the past. The "here," the thing and Being are deposits of spirit's grip on resistance. Images are living, efficacious souls, joined by connection and not by relation (Book V, Part One). Finally the book shows a human world in which those images were still actual (Part Two), and claims that their weakening is a change in actuality itself, not just in us (Retrospect). The one thing it cannot narrate is the event on which the whole story turns: how spirit first entered life.

4. The major twists and turns

4.1 The founding inversion: Being is not actual

The whole book rests on one decision in Ch. 5. Klages accepts every Eleatic argument and reverses its conclusion. Zeno showed that motion cannot be thought as being. The West concluded that motion is unreal. Klages concludes instead that Being is not actual: what the concept grasps is an abstraction cut out of a happening that alone is real. This is a genuine philosophical move, not a slogan. It lets Klages grant rationalism its logic, since the identity principle, the timelessness of the object and the impossibility of composing a continuum from points all stand, while denying it its ontology. Most of Books I–II follow from this one reversal: the thing as a relational point (Ch. 2), duration as a construct (Ch. 3), properties as acts of drawing limits (Ch. 4), and truth as a "score" (Ch. 13).

What it costs: the reversal needs the premise that the apprehending act is literally durationless (Ch. 1). That premise runs together the mathematical boundary between past and future with the experienced present. It also needs a strict dichotomy, "no mediating concept" (Ch. 8): every power is either point-positing spirit or continuum-receiving life. Every later dualism inherits this exclusion.

4.2 Spirit's essence moves from intellect to will

The deepest structural twist is that the book discovers what spirit is in stages. In Book I spirit is introduced as the faculty of apprehension, a neutral, atemporal "finding" of points. By the end of Ch. 9 it already appears as self-assertion. In Book IV the one act of spirit is shown to be apprehension on the impression side and will on the impulse side. Ch. 49 then derives the categories of the thing-world (number, line, thing, law, cause) from purpose-consciousness, so that what Book I attributed to the intellect is finally traced to the will. The Retrospect makes this explicit and treats it as the key to the title: "our work finds the key to the essence of the spirit not in the intellect, but in the will"; spirit is "deed 'in itself'," which "can only destroy … can only cleave apart" [1422–24].

This is why the book is not just an anti-intellectualism. Klages explicitly sides with rationalism (objectivity, logic) against fashionable "irrationalism," which he calls "a last and worst trump card of — the spirit" [1419]. Logic is spirit's last life-dependent form, the "Promethean" middle realm where spirit still concedes something to life. The enemy is the will set free (the Heraclean phase), and that will uses logic without serving it.

The unresolved difficulty: if spirit is at bottom deed and destruction, Book I's description of the act as receptive finding has to be reinterpreted after the fact as a disguised cutting. The book never fully shows that the timeless act, as such, is resistive rather than simply other (the assessment of Ch. 9 makes this point). The opening ontology describes a spectator; the conclusion requires an aggressor.

4.3 The trichotomy re-ranked, and where spirit gets in

The Introduction revives the Greek triad of body, soul and spirit, but re-ranks it. Body and soul are not the lower storeys under spirit's gable. They are the two poles of one life-cell, and spirit drives into it "like a wedge" [7]. Two later specifications carry this out. - Soul is the transience of the body (Ch. 9, repeated in Ch. 56). The soul is not a substance but the happening of the body's own transformation, and correspondingly "time is the soul of space." - Spirit enters through the sensing body, not the beholding soul (Ch. 55, prepared by Ch. 22 and 25, and located concretely in Ch. 58–60 as the "doubled here" of resistance, the "site of primal disturbance"). This reverses the idealist assumption that spirit is the soul's highest part. Spirit attaches to the most bodily, contact-bound, resistance-feeling side of life. Beholding, the most "spiritual-seeming" capacity (vision, dream, distance), is the one least open to spirit.

The result is a consistent map. The body senses (nearness, resistance, here). The soul beholds (distance, image, the past). Spirit strikes the point where sensing makes a "here" and fixes it into a thing, an I, a now. Ch. 57 says Klages finds this triad, against Plato's intention, in Plato himself: "the body has drives and sensations, the soul beholds, the spirit apprehends."

4.4 Consciousness demoted

Book III makes a claim that is radical for 1929. Lived experience is not conscious, and consciousness is not the medium of mental life but an intermittent disturbance of it, a series of acts that mark boundaries between waves of experience. "Consciousness" and its modern twin, the "unconscious," are both rejected as confusions of experience with knowledge of experience (Ch. 24–25). From this follow the theory of attention through affect (Ch. 27), the posthumous character of historical knowledge (Ch. 28), and, in Book IV, the depth and frequency of spirit's acts as the measure of degrees of consciousness (Ch. 53). In the Retrospect this becomes the core of the reply to the charge of self-contradiction: thinking consciousness is "a state of lack that could only gain a foothold through a disturbance of life" [1419].

4.5 From the critique of the thing to the actuality of images

The negative half of the book (Books I–IV) shows what the thing is: an abstract point set by spirit, then a self-asserting place, then a copy of the I. The positive half (Book V) shows what the image is: an appearance of distance, temporal and alive, that comes to presence in a polar meeting (gamos) between a receiving soul and the image-souls of happening. The decisive bridges are: - Elementary similarity (Ch. 34), the pre-conceptual ground of recognition and symbol, which earlier chapters had to presuppose; - Being-moved (Ch. 62), the pathic, change-of-place-free core of movement, which joins the near-world of sensation to the far-world of beholding; - Connection vs relation (Ch. 66), which turns the ontology of images into a theory of efficacy (Wirkung), so that only living poles truly act on one another, while physics deals in relations emptied of life (cf. Ch. 51–52); - the elemental souls (Ch. 65), where panpsychism is reached by a transcendental argument: if intuition is participation, what is participated in must live.

The shift here is from epistemology to cosmology. A theory of perception, the claim that the given is images, has become a claim about what the world is made of: souls of distance and of the past, renewed in beholding.

4.6 From argument to pointing: the change of method

Klages says from the beginning that experience cannot be communicated, only pointed to (Introduction [8]; Ch. 11). But the book's actual method changes as it goes, and this is the most important feature for evaluating it. - Books I–IV argue in a recognisably philosophical way, with reductios, analyses of concepts, transcendental arguments, phenomenological descriptions and close argument with contemporary science. - In Ch. 61 Klages admits that the method of the soul–body chapters is the very "shallow reflection" he has criticised (flache Besinnung, Ch. 14), and that it cannot achieve the reconnection of sensing with beholding. - From Ch. 62 on he increasingly argues from scales of examples (cradle, carousel, dance), from language and etymology, from poetry and myth. He announces "thinking in images" [1097], ranks esoteric inquiry by "probability" [1194], and offers truths that "one cannot prove … but neither refute" [1213]. - Ch. 57 offers a hermeneutic that splits each thinker into heart (what was beheld) and head (what spirit added). Applied to the whole history of thought, it can absorb any counter-evidence.

The book thus earns authority in its argued half and spends it in its symbolic half. Klages has a principled defence: if actuality cannot be conceived, a metaphysics of actuality must work by pointing, through "demonstrations, 'proofs,' indeed makings-present of 'primal phenomena'" [1429]. But this leaves the central normative distinction of Book V, primal image against phantom, without a shared public test (B7 Arc). In Ch. 69 the criterion shifts between the intuitable, the felt, and "the constitution of the blood."

4.7 History as the war of spirit on life

The philosophy of history is announced in Ch. 8's table of oppositions ("progress" is spirit's victorious struggle against life). It is developed in Ch. 53 (Promethean → Heraclean; Christian asceticism as the will's training-ground; a post-historical humanity, unconscious again), applied in Ch. 57 (telluric turns; the last wave around 1890; "dithyrambists of decline"), and generalised in Ch. 69 (images, not agents, as the causes of history). Two features stand out. First, it is strictly non-programmatic. The Retrospect refuses any remedy, because a remedy would be one more act of will. It allows only an ethic of "turning-about" (Umkehr): refraining rather than deed, the will to perfect work rather than the will to deed, and the protection of what one loves [1423–28]. Second, it is fatalist with a reservation. The prognosis is the "self-attrition of mankind," qualified only by a "miracle" beyond calculation [1422].

4.8 The final twist: actuality itself has changed

The Retrospect ends not on soul and spirit but on Pelasgian magic. It asks whether weather was ever really changed by magic. Klages's answer is that it may once have been possible "because it was not so much the actuality of the inner as rather actuality itself that was other than it is today," and that such "transformations of telluric actuality cannot be discovered by the research methods of physics." He calls this "the best and deepest of the content of our work" [1431–32]. This is where the doctrine of images claims its strongest ontological consequence. If images are actuality, and images are souls that can wane, then the world itself, not only our access to it, has a history of growing poorer. Physics cannot register this, because physics only ever measures the residue spirit has already turned into things. The claim makes the downfall thesis cosmological rather than cultural. It also takes the book furthest from any possible verification.

4.9 The logical form underneath: polarity against dualism

The twists above share one logical structure, and seeing it makes the book's strategy clear. Klages works with two different kinds of opposition. - Polarity holds within life: body and soul, sensing and beholding, nearness and distance, day-pole and night-pole, male and female, sun and earth, receiving soul and acting image. The poles of a polarity are interdependent and pass into each other. Neither exists without the other, their tension is what is actual (Ch. 8, 26, 65–68), and their unity is the coincidentia oppositorum (Ch. 68) or "the Mother" (Ch. 73–74). - Dualism holds between life and spirit. The two terms are irreducible, independent of each other (or, in spirit's case, parasitic), and not mediated by any third term (Ch. 8: "no mediating concept").

Klages's central thesis can be restated in these terms: spirit is the power that turns polarities into dualisms. It cuts the continuous pole-relation into separate relata (Ch. 66: relation arises only where connection is cut). It makes the life-cell's internal tension into an opposition of subject and object (Ch. 12), and the drive's pull into a choice between ends (Ch. 46). So the opposition between spirit and life is the only opposition in the book that is not polar.

This explains two of the book's most characteristic features. First, it explains why Klages refuses every reconciliation. A mediation between spirit and life would make them poles again, and polarity is precisely what spirit has destroyed. Second, it explains the book's deepest exposure to criticism. The dualism has to be asserted from within a philosophy that teaches that everything actual is polar. Scheler, Ortega and Iberico (Part Three) can all be read as insisting that spirit and life are, after all, one more polarity.

5. Structural problems the book does not solve

1. The origin and interaction problem. Spirit is non-spatial, timeless and "positive nothing" (Ch. 61), yet it acts: it splits, inhibits and produces "immeasurable effects" (Ch. 43). Klages calls the coupling of spirit and life "probably unresolvable" [648] and concedes in Ch. 70 and the Retrospect that he cannot say how spirit first entered life. His reply, that a proven fact needs no explanation of its origin [1430], is fair as far as it goes. But the proof of the hostility thesis itself relies on spirit's having causal effects in life, and that is exactly what the interaction problem calls into question.

2. The status of spirit. Book I keeps "actuality" for spatio-temporal happening and puts spirit outside it. Ch. 26 then speaks of "two actualities." Ch. 69 has the purely destructive act produce phantoms with real inducing power. Spirit is said to be unable to build, yet it builds phantoms. These are not slips. They show that a principle defined purely negatively has to be given positive powers to do the work the book needs.

3. Self-reference. The Retrospect's reply to "intellect fights intellect" is the book's best argument. The same faculty of judgment can be moved by life-dependent or spirit-dependent impulses. Logic and objectivity are the life-dependent form, and to rank the judging state as a lack relative to experiencing is not to deny that judging works ("truth is never actuality") [1418–19]. That is coherent. But Ch. 46–47 also make every judgment a hidden negation of actuality and every truth a "tendentious one-sidedness." Klages's own theses then need the "indicating" exemption he grants only to the deep will to truth, and he applies it to his own claims only in principle.

4. Determinism and indictment. Ch. 53 and Ch. 69 are strictly determinist: we must will what we will, and remorse is a delusion. Yet the book's language of guilt, murder, desecration and "treachery" is moral throughout. Klages treats his own partisanship as itself an expression of images. That keeps him consistent, but it puts his evaluations beyond argument by construction.

5. An unfalsifiable hermeneutic and unweighed evidence. The heart/head reading (Ch. 57, 74) cannot be refuted. Clairvoyance, dowsing and psychometry (Ch. 23) and poetic animism (Ch. 20–21, 71) are used as evidence without any discussion of their reliability.

6. The political assertions. In several places (Ch. 42, 44, 47, 48, 70 and elsewhere) Klages gives an openly antisemitic genealogy of law and of the Heraclean phase ("Jahwism," Judaism, Paul, "high finance"), along with sweeping claims about gender, sexuality and peoples. The racial and ethnographic typologies of Ch. 33–34 are a separate problem: they are offered as "verification," but they are read through the very ratio of making-present to embodiment that they are supposed to confirm, so the argument is circular. These must be named for what they are. They are assertions, not arguments, and the metaphysics does not entail them. It arguably contradicts them: a spirit that is non-spatial and cannot appear cannot coherently be assigned an ethnic home. The reader should keep in mind that these passages are part of the text, and judge the book's arguments without them.

6. Overall upshots

What the work achieves, philosophically. - A sustained critique of reification. The analysis of the thing as a relational point (Ch. 2), of properties as acts of drawing limits (Ch. 4), of "duration of time" as a paralogism that breeds a regress (Ch. 3), and of force and cause as projected will (Ch. 50–51) is a serious and detailed account of how the categories of the object-world arise. Its conclusions overlap, from a very different starting point, with the later phenomenological critique of "presence-at-hand" and of calculative thinking, and with the critique of instrumental reason associated with the Frankfurt School. - A phenomenology of the pre-objective. The descriptions of dream as beholding without sensing (Ch. 55), of being-moved and co-movement as the medium of expression (Ch. 62), of the felt body as the indicator of perceived movement (Ch. 63), of distance as a positive quality rather than a measured remoteness (Ch. 56), and of the cross-modal intuitive image (Ch. 20) are the book's most lasting content. They also ground Klages's science of expression and characterology. - A demotion of consciousness and of the will. Book III's account of consciousness as intermittent and triggered by disturbance, and Book IV's model of willing as steering by inhibition, are clear and arguable theses that stand independently of the metaphysics of decline. - A distinctive thesis about time. Real time flows toward us and past a standing point. Measurable time is a product of the will's orientation toward the future. The past is a dimension of depth, and its images can be renewed (Ch. 3, 48, 65, 67, 74). Klages develops this against both Bergson and Nietzsche.

What remains promissory or doubtful. - The founding dichotomy (no mediating concept between point and continuum) is laid down rather than defended, and every later dualism inherits it. - The move from a theory of the given (images, not things) to a cosmology (elemental souls, telluric turns, a change in actuality itself) is carried by transcendental inference, analogy and pointing, and the second half explicitly gives up the demand for proof. - The central metaphysical claim, that spirit is an alien, non-living power that entered life, is restated many times but never grounded, as Klages himself admits in Ch. 70.

The shape of the whole. The book's path is from logic to myth. It starts by granting rationalism everything it can prove (the timeless object, the identity principle, the non-composability of the continuum). It then shows that what rationalism proves is the shape of an act, not the shape of the world. It finds that this act is at bottom will, and it reconstructs the world the act cuts into: a world of images, distance, polarity and renewal, last fully lived in Pelasgian consciousness. Its strongest contribution is the claim that the categories of objectivity have a vital genealogy. Its gravest weakness is that the alternative it offers can be pointed to but, by its own standards, cannot be argued for. Klages's own X/Y test is therefore the fairest way to judge it. On existence and distinctness, the book makes a strong case. On irreducibility, it rests on a dichotomy it assumes. On hostility, it offers a powerful diagnosis and a historical narrative, but it does not offer a proof. Part Three shows that the later reception divided along exactly these lines.

Part Three — Reception, Afterlives, and Kindred Thinkers

This part answers four questions: - Who took up Klages's ideas, and in which direction? - Who argued against him, and on which points? - Which thinkers resemble him without depending on him? - What does the secondary literature now say?

Throughout, three kinds of claim are kept apart: documented reception (a thinker read Klages and responded in writing), influence argued by scholars (a connection proposed in the secondary literature), and affinity (a parallel in thought with no claim that one depends on the other). Where this guide is the only one making a connection, it says so.

1. Contemporary reception (1929–1945)

Walter Benjamin. Benjamin engaged with Klages more seriously than anyone else on the left. In 1930 he urged Scholem to read the newly published Geist als Widersacher, calling it a major philosophical work whatever the political context its author stood in. Scholars have followed several threads from Klages into Benjamin's work: - Aura. Klages's "nimbus" or "aura" of the image, the halo of distance and past that surrounds a true appearance (Ch. 57 [916–918], Ch. 65–67), is widely taken as one source of Benjamin's aura, "the unique appearance of a distance, however near it may be." Benjamin's essay on the work of art then reverses Klages's valuation. The decay of aura, which Klages mourns, Benjamin treats as the precondition of a politics that has left myth behind (Moretti 2013). - Prehistory. Benjamin took up Klages's (and Bachofen's) idea of a prehistoric, "mythic" layer of consciousness that persists in the present. It shapes the Arcades Project's notion of the "dream" of the nineteenth century and its "dialectical images." Lebovic (2013) reconstructs the triangle of Klages, Benjamin and Alfred Baeumler around Bachofen in the years 1914–1926. - Similarity. Benjamin's "Doctrine of the Similar" and "On the Mimetic Faculty" (1933) describe a pre-conceptual faculty for "non-sensuous similarity" that was strong in archaic humanity and has weakened since, surviving above all in language. The parallel with Klages's elementary similarity (Ch. 34), his symbolic thinking (Ch. 36) and his account of the image-content of names (Ch. 66) is close. Whether it is a matter of influence or of a shared source in Romantic language theory is still debated; this guide makes the comparison on its own account.

What Benjamin keeps: the reality of the archaic, the image as the unit of experience, and decay as historical. What he rejects: the valuation (decline as loss only) and the politics.

Adorno and Horkheimer. In 1932, at Horkheimer's request, Adorno agreed to review Geist als Widersacher for the Zeitschrift für Sozialforschung. The review grew into a larger project, and Adorno abandoned it around 1936. Its traces are in Dialectic of Enlightenment (1944/47), whose Odyssey chapter debates Klages (and Rudolf Borchardt) directly (Immanen; see the sources below). The structural parallel is striking. Both books hold that reason, in mastering nature, turns into domination and does violence to the inner nature of the subject itself. Klages's "spirit as will," which can only cut apart (Ch. 46–49), and his reading of science as projected will (Ch. 50–54) foreshadow the critique of instrumental reason. The decisive difference is that Adorno and Horkheimer insist that only reason can criticise reason. Their "dialectic" of enlightenment is meant to escape both Klages's verdict that spirit is the enemy and his turn to myth, which they read as one more form of the domination it denounces. Klages anticipated this objection in the Retrospect [1416–20] (see §4.2 of Part Two).

Max Scheler. In Die Stellung des Menschen im Kosmos (1928), written while the first volume of GWS was in press, Scheler devotes a section to "the doctrine of L. Klages." He groups him with Dacqué, Frobenius, Jung, Prinzhorn and Theodor Lessing (and, in some respects, Spengler) as representatives of a "panromantic way of thinking." Scheler grants that Klages is "idiosyncratic but not without depth," that he rightly saw the creative power of the dark, unconscious drives and the dependence of fate on their symbolic images, and that his observations of late-cultural phenomena are subtle. His objections bear directly on the book's architecture: 1. Klages equates spirit with intelligence, ego and will, and so misses its core, the intuition of essences. 2. Spirit and life are oriented toward each other, not at war. 3. Spirit has no power of its own, so it cannot destroy anything. (This is the "interaction problem" of Part Two, §5.1, put as an objection.) 4. What Klages blames on spirit is really an over-sublimation of brain-bound intelligence. 5. Klages's own romantic flight relies on the willed technique he condemns. 6. He is inconsistent about when spirit "broke in."

Klages's later emphasis on will as the key to spirit (Book IV; Retrospect [1422]) can be read in part as an answer to objection 1.

Georg Lukács. In The Destruction of Reason (1954), Lukács places Klages at the culmination of Lebensphilosophie. Klages, on this account, "actually transformed vitalism into an open combat against reason and culture," and stands, with Baeumler and Rosenberg, in the ideological prehistory of fascism. This reading set the tone of postwar reception and largely explains Klages's eclipse after 1945.

Heidegger. Heidegger read Klages and treated him as a representative of the "philosophy of life," which on his account remains inside the metaphysics it attacks. Klages's soul/spirit opposition is a reversal of the Platonic ranking, not an exit from it. This is close to Part Two's own observation that Klages's ontology grants rationalism everything except its valuation (§4.1). The guide has not verified a specific passage, so this is offered as the standard scholarly characterisation.

The National Socialist context. The relationship is double-edged and should be stated exactly. Klages's antisemitism is explicit in GWS (see the Critical notes to Ch. 42, 44, 47, 48 and 70), and Lukács and Lebovic place his vitalism in the genealogy of Nazi "biopolitics." Yet the regime's official ideologues kept their distance. Alfred Rosenberg declared in 1938 that the movement did not endorse Klages's philosophy, and Baeumler, close to Klages in the 1920s, became a rival. The reasons are internal to the book. Klages's metaphysics condemns will, deed, technology, expansion and the state, and his Retrospect recommends "refraining" and the refusal of every program [1427–28]. That cannot be squared with a mobilising ideology.

Theodor Lessing. Klages's friend in youth and later his opponent, Lessing shared his "biological catastrophism" (Geschichte als Sinngebung des Sinnlosen, 1919; Untergang der Erde am Geist, 1924). This is the thesis that spirit, entering life, sets off a catastrophe. Scheler treats the two together.

Characterology and the psychology of expression. Outside philosophy proper, Klages's widest influence in his own lifetime came through Ausdruckskunde and Charakterkunde (expression and character): graphology, the theory of expressive movement, and character typology. These shaped German "expression psychology" (Philipp Lersch's Gesicht und Seele, 1932, is the best-known example) and, through Hans Prinzhorn, psychiatric thinking about art and expression. GWS supplies their metaphysical ground: expression as the bodily appearance of the soul (Ch. 62, "co-movement"), and the face and gesture as images.

2. Later developments: who carried the ideas further, and how

Neue Phänomenologie (Hermann Schmitz, and after him Gernot Böhme and Tonino Griffero). This is the most substantial philosophical continuation. Schmitz (1928–2021) called Klages his closest kindred spirit. He took up and rigorised, in phenomenological rather than metaphysical terms, several of the book's core claims: - the felt body (Leib, as distinct from the physical body, Körper) as the medium of all perception, cf. Ch. 58–63, where the felt body is the "indicator" of perceived movement; - the pre-objective layer of experience, which Schmitz calls primitive presence, prior to subject and object (cf. Ch. 12, 29–33); - feelings as spatially poured-out atmospheres, not inner states (cf. Klages's essences that appear in the image, Ch. 20–21, and the "mood" of impressions, Ch. 9); - half-things and expressive qualities in the external world (cf. Ch. 21, 65).

What Schmitz drops is the metaphysics of an alien spirit and the historical narrative of decline. He keeps a critique of the "psychologistic-reductionist-introjectionist" objectification of experience, which he traces, much as Klages did, to the Greeks (Democritus, Plato). That is a recognisable descendant of Book I's critique of the thing. Böhme developed the theory of atmospheres into an aesthetics and an ecology of nature.
