Griffero has written explicitly on Klages's primal images and phantoms (Ch. 69). Michael Großheim's Ludwig Klages und die Phänomenologie (1994) is the standard study of this lineage, and the 2024 collective volume Ludwig Klages und die Neue Phänomenologie (eds. Großheim and Smiljanić) documents it in detail. Schmitz's own essay there is titled "Was bleibt vom Philosophen Ludwig Klages?"

Ecology. Klages's address Mensch und Erde (1913), which GWS cites and presupposes, is regularly cited as one of the earliest philosophical ecological manifestos. It denounces the extinction of species, the destruction of landscape and the commodification of nature as expressions of the same will to mastery that GWS analyses. The line runs, mostly without acknowledgement, into German and later Anglo-American "deep" ecological thought. The walnut-tree example in the Retrospect [1427–28] is a small instance of the same thinking.

Biopolitics and the theory of the archaic. Nitzan Lebovic (2013) traces the vocabulary of life and death, ecstasy and the "pathic" from Klages through Benjamin and Baeumler into twentieth-century debates on biopolitics, and on to Agamben. Paul Bishop (2017/18) presents Klages as a "vitalist toolkit," nine portable concepts (image, soul, spirit, expression, rhythm, and so on) usable apart from the metaphysics of decline, and has emphasised the parallels with Jung and Goethe.

The Hispanic world: Mariano Iberico (Peru, 1892–1974). Iberico is the clearest case outside Germany of a philosopher who absorbed Klages and built an independent metaphysics with him. His path ran from Bergson (his 1916 doctoral thesis on Bergson's philosophy drew a personal letter of congratulation from Bergson) to an original "metaphysics of appearance." The Peruvian literature lists Klages's "imaginism" among its decisive sources, together with Plotinus' emanationism, Novalis, Nietzsche, Pascal, Dostoevsky and Unamuno (Pimentel; Sobrevilla). The key works are: - La unidad dividida (1932), on life as division, death and resurrection; - El sentimiento de la vida cósmica (1939), on nature as "a set of powers and images given immediately to man," which is a close echo of GWS's elemental souls and images (Ch. 64–65); - La aparición: ensayos sobre el ser y el aparecer (1950), his main work; - La aparición histórica (1971).

The central concept, the aparición (apparition), is an "image-concept" that dissolves the modern subject–object scheme. The image is a seeing (videncia) without subject or object, an event of encounter co-created in contemplation and memory. Iberico writes in La aparición that there are not two images, one in reality and one in the soul, but that reality is "the unbreakable unity of vision and image," and he uses the figure of the mirror. This is a direct transposition of the polar doctrine of soul and image (Ch. 8, 20) and of beholding as "mirroring" (Ch. 29–32). Iberico differs from Klages in three ways: - He drops the hostility of spirit. His tragic dialectic of division and reunion, drawn from Pascal, Dostoevsky and Unamuno, allows that division can turn into joy and renewal. - He gives the aparición a creationist, event-like character ("creation" as the absolute transition), closer to Bergson's novelty than to Klages's renewal of primal images. - In La aparición histórica he extends the doctrine to history as an apparition "of the second power." It is a constructive counterpart to Klages's view that images are the causes of history (Ch. 69), without Klages's prognosis of decline.

Iberico is also part of a wider Latin American vitalism of the 1920s–40s, in which Bergson, Klages, Spengler, Keyserling and Scheler were read side by side, often through the translations and essays of Ortega y Gasset's Revista de Occidente.

3. Kindred spirits: affinities without dependence

Henri Bergson. Bergson is the nearest major analogue, and Klages argues against him by name (Ch. 3 [27], almost certainly; Ch. 5 [51–52]). The affinities are deep: - Both oppose a spatialising, point-positing intellect to real, flowing time. - Both hold that the intellect is shaped by practical action, Bergson's homo faber corresponding to Klages's spirit as will (Book IV). - Both regard instinct and intuition as more intimate with life than concept. - Strikingly, both begin from images. The first chapter of Matière et mémoire (1896) describes the world as an "aggregate of images," existing midway between the "thing" of the realist and the "representation" of the idealist. That is very close to Klages's thesis that the given is the image, not the thing and not the sensation (Ch. 18–23).

Klages's divergences are explicit: 1. Time and duration. Bergson's durée is still, for Klages, a confusion of time with duration. "Duration" belongs only to things, and the image of cosmic life accumulating "like compound interest" (Ch. 3 [27]) is a progressive picture he rejects. Real time flows toward us and past us; it does not pile up. 2. Space. Bergson located the problem in spatialised time but left space unexamined. Klages insists that the riddle of continuity is spatial too (Ch. 5). 3. Valuation. Bergson's intellect and intuition cooperate, and the élan vital is creative and forward-moving. Klages's spirit is an alien adversary, and his life is rhythmic renewal, not creative evolution. 4. The past. Bergsonian memory preserves the past as a whole in the present. Klages's past is a dimension of distance, whose images can be renewed but not stored (Ch. 65, 67, 74).

Iberico's development, from Bergson toward something Klagesian, shows these two philosophies can be combined.

Nietzsche and Bachofen (sources more than kin). GWS is a sustained argument with Nietzsche, whom Klages saw as the great psychologist misled by the "will to power" (his 1926 book Die psychologischen Errungenschaften Nietzsches is the prelude). Bachofen gives Book V its prehistory (Ch. 74). C. G. Carus and the Romantic natural philosophers (Ch. 57) are acknowledged ancestors.

Miguel de Unamuno and José Ortega y Gasset. Unamuno's Del sentimiento trágico de la vida (1913) shares Klages's thesis that reason is "anti-vital," that "everything vital is anti-rational," while drawing the opposite, existential-Christian conclusion: the agonic struggle for personal immortality, which Klages calls a delusion (Ch. 3, 75). Ortega's razón vital is the corrective that a Klages reader in the Hispanic world was likely to meet. Ortega accepts the primacy of life but refuses the antithesis, holding that reason is a function of life and that the task is to integrate the two. In that sense he answers Klages's irreducibility claim (claim 3 of the X/Y test) in the negative. Neither depends on Klages; both define the space in which he was read in Spanish.

D. H. Lawrence. Lawrence's opposition of "blood-consciousness" to "mental consciousness" (Psychoanalysis and the Unconscious, 1921; Fantasia of the Unconscious, 1922) runs remarkably parallel to Klages's: the solar plexus against the brain, which Ch. 57 also attributes to the Romantics; the indictment of the will; the cosmic polarity of sun and earth, male and female; and a lost archaic consciousness. There is no evidence of influence either way. It is an affinity of the 1910s–20s.

Gaston Bachelard. Bachelard's "material imagination," his reveries of water, fire, air and earth (1938–48), develops the theme that the elements are psychically charged images and not physical substances. That is the theme of Klages's elemental souls (Ch. 65) and elemental symbols (Ch. 73). Bachelard, however, keeps scientific reason and poetic reverie as two separate, legitimate regimes of mind (his "diurnal" and "nocturnal" Bachelard). This is roughly the Promethean compromise that Klages thought could not last.

Michel Henry. Henry's phenomenology of life as pure self-affection, prior to and invisible within the "world" of objects, and his indictment of Galilean science for excluding life (La Barbarie, 1987), restate in a different idiom Klages's claims that life experiences without spirit (Ch. 8) and that physics deals only with relations emptied of life (Ch. 51–54). Henry grounds his position in a Christian metaphysics of the Word, which Klages would have rejected outright. That makes the parallel more telling.

Erwin Straus and Merleau-Ponty. Straus's Vom Sinn der Sinne (1935) distinguishes sensing (Empfinden), a pathic, sympathetic communication with the world, from objectifying perceiving (Wahrnehmen). That is close to Klages's beholding/sensing versus apprehending (Ch. 17, 22, 55). Merleau-Ponty's pre-objective world, his "flesh," and his account of movement and the lived body (cf. Ch. 62–63) belong to the same family. In both cases the parallel is better documented through shared sources (Gestalt psychology, Palágyi, whom Klages discusses in Ch. 40 and 52) than through direct dependence.

Jung, Corbin and Hillman. Scheler already grouped Jung with Klages. The doctrine of the reality and efficacy of primal images (Urbilder, Ch. 69) has an obvious counterpart in Jung's archetypes, and a further one in Henry Corbin's mundus imaginalis and James Hillman's "archetypal psychology," with its "soul-making" and "images as primary." The decisive difference is that Klages's images are cosmic actualities, souls of distance and of the past, not contents of a collective psyche. His Retrospect explicitly bars the "maid-of-all-work 'the unconscious'" as an escape route [1431].

4. A short guide to the secondary literature

- Hans Eggert Schröder, Ludwig Klages: Die Geschichte seines Lebens (2 vols., Bonn: Bouvier, 1966–1992). The standard biography, by the editor of the Sämtliche Werke. It is sympathetic and documentary. - Michael Großheim, Ludwig Klages und die Phänomenologie (Berlin: Akademie, 1994). The best philosophical study of Klages's relation to Husserl, Scheler, Heidegger and Schmitz. - Reinhard Falter, Ludwig Klages: Lebensphilosophie als Zivilisationskritik (Munich, 2003). Reads Klages above all as a critic of civilisation and an ecological thinker. - Nitzan Lebovic, The Philosophy of Life and Death: Ludwig Klages and the Rise of a Nazi Biopolitics (Palgrave Macmillan, 2013). The most synoptic English-language study. It is openly critical, follows the line from Klages into Nazi cultural politics, and also traces his afterlife in Benjamin and Agamben. - Paul Bishop, Ludwig Klages and the Philosophy of Life: A Vitalist Toolkit (Routledge, 2017/18). An introduction organised around nine key concepts, with translated extracts. It is sympathetic and stresses Goethe and Jung. - Michael Großheim and Damir Smiljanić (eds.), Ludwig Klages und die Neue Phänomenologie (Freiburg: Alber, 2024). Includes Schmitz's "Was bleibt vom Philosophen Ludwig Klages?", Smiljanić on Klages as precursor of the Neue Phänomenologie, and Griffero on primal images and phantoms. - On Benjamin and Adorno: Giampiero Moretti, on Klages's "nimbus-aura" and Benjamin (Rivista di estetica 52, 2013); Mikko Immanen, on Adorno's confrontation with Klages and Borchardt in Dialectic of Enlightenment (see the interview and article linked below). - On Iberico: David Sobrevilla, introduction to Ritmos del paisaje (Lima, 2012, a re-edition of Notas sobre el paisaje de la sierra); Sebastián Pimentel, "La Historia en la filosofía de Mariano Iberico"; Francisco Miró Quesada, Despertar y proyecto del filosofar latinoamericano. - Translations: GWS has no complete English translation. The Science of Character (Grundlagen der Charakterkunde) was for decades Klages's only work in English. Selections and the Cosmogonic Eros have appeared in English more recently, partly through small presses of the political right. The reader should know this context but need not let it decide how they judge the texts.

5. What the reception shows about the book

The reception sorts the book's claims into those that travelled and those that did not. - What travelled was the descriptive and critical content: the pre-objective image, the felt body, expression, atmosphere and distance (Schmitz, Böhme, Straus, Iberico); the critique of reification and instrumental reason (Benjamin, Adorno); the elements as images (Bachelard); and the ecological indictment. - What did not travel was the metaphysical dualism of an alien, life-destroying spirit, and its prognosis. Scheler, Adorno, Iberico, Ortega and Schmitz each rejected it, on different grounds: spirit is powerless, or reason must criticise itself, or division can turn to renewal, or reason is a function of life, or the dualism adds nothing to the phenomenology.

In the terms of Klages's own X/Y test, his successors broadly accepted existence and distinctness (life and its images are not reducible to the objects of reason). They contested irreducibility and hostility. That is where Part Two located the book's argumentative weak points.

---
